NOUN · Elementary Mathematics II · MTH 102

MTH 102 Study Guide

Rewritten and checked version of NOUN MTH 102 (Units 1–8), examples verified with SymPy, course mistakes listed per unit.

Exam: about 14 December 2026 · four weeks from 16 November

This page holds the whole course: a four-week plan, the formula sheets together, all eight units with their worked examples and practice problems, every verified mistake in the course text, and where to read more. Work the examples with pen and paper, then do the practice problems with the answers still folded away. The problems marked from outside sources are extra: they are not from the NOUN text and are labelled at every point where they appear.

Two things about the course text

  • Unit 7 is missing from the course body. The contents page lists “Definite integrals (application to areas under curve and volumes of solids)”, but the PDF has no such unit: pages 97–114 cover indefinite integrals only, and definite integrals first appear, unexplained, in the volume unit. The Unit 7 chapter here is therefore written from standard first-year calculus material, not from the course, and says so at its top.
  • PDF pp.50–59 duplicate pp.41–49. They are a second, re-paginated copy of the same Unit 2 text. Study pp.41–49 and skip pp.50–59; Unit 2 cites both places where it matters.

4-week plan

Four weeks, Nov 16 – Dec 13, 2026. Four weeks is enough if the work is regular. Each week uses the same loop: read the Key ideas, work the examples by hand, do the practice problems without looking, check the answers, then re-do the ones you got wrong.

WeekDatesWork
Week 116–22 NovUnits 1–3 — functions, limits, continuity.
Week 223–29 NovUnits 4–5 — differentiation.
Week 330 Nov–6 DecUnits 6–7 — integration.
Week 47–13 DecUnit 8 — volumes, then full revision: every practice problem and the errata.

Read Key ideas → work examples by hand → practice problems without looking → check answers → re-do wrong ones.

Exam format (from the course guide)

  • Assignments: 30% — four TMAs, the best three count.
  • Final exam: 70% — 2 hours, answer 3 questions out of at least 5.

The guide does not list which topics are examined, so the unit notes mark material as “likely”, never as certain.

Master formula sheet

The eight Formula sheets, copied verbatim from the units and set one after another. They also appear again inside their units; this is the single page to revise from.

Unit 1 — Functions and Graphs

Function value and difference quotient

$$f(a)=\text{output at }x=a,$$
$$\frac{f(x+\Delta x)-f(x)}{\Delta x}\ (\Delta x\ne0).$$

For $f(x)=x^2+bx+c$: $\dfrac{f(x+\Delta x)-f(x)}{\Delta x}=2x+\Delta x+b$.

Implied domain rules: $\dfrac1{g(x)}$: $g(x)\ne0$. $\ \sqrt{g(x)}$: $g(x)\ge0$. $\ \sqrt{g(x)}$ has range $\ge0$.

Line tests: vertical $\Rightarrow$ function; horizontal $\Rightarrow$ one-to-one $\Rightarrow$ has an inverse.

Composition: $(f\circ g)(x)=f(g(x))$; $\operatorname{dom}(f\circ g)=\{x\in\operatorname{dom}g:g(x)\in\operatorname{dom}f\}$.

Inverse: $f(f^{-1}(x))=x$ ($x\in\operatorname{dom}f^{-1}$), $f^{-1}(f(x))=x$ ($x\in\operatorname{dom}f$); $(f\circ g)^{-1}=g^{-1}\circ f^{-1}$.

Standard inverses

$f(x)$ $f^{-1}(x)$ Condition
$ax+b$ $\dfrac{x-b}a$ $a\ne0$
$\dfrac1x$ $\dfrac1x$ $x\ne0$
$x^3$ $\sqrt[3]x$ all real $x$
$x^2$ $\sqrt x$ $x\ge0$
$x^2+c$ $\sqrt{x-c}$ $x\ge0$, domain of $f^{-1}$: $x\ge c$
$\dfrac{ax+b}{cx+d}$ $\dfrac{dx-b}{-cx+a}$ $ad-bc\ne0$

Unit 2 — Limits

Laws ($\lim f=L$, $\lim g=k$ finite):

$$\lim(bf)=bL,\qquad \lim(f\pm g)=L\pm k,$$
$$\lim(fg)=Lk,\qquad \lim\frac fg=\frac Lk\ (k\ne0),$$
$$\lim f^n=L^n,\qquad \lim\sqrt[n]{f}=\sqrt[n]{L}.$$

Polynomial: $\lim_{x\to c}p(x)=p(c)$. Existence: $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)=L\iff\lim_{x\to c^-}f(x)=\lim_{x\to c^+}f(x)=L$. Replacement: $f=g$ for $x\ne c\Rightarrow\lim_{x\to c}f=\lim_{x\to c}g$ (if the latter exists).

Factorisations used again and again:

$$a^2-b^2=(a-b)(a+b),$$
$$a^3-b^3=(a-b)(a^2+ab+b^2),$$
$$a^3+b^3=(a+b)(a^2-ab+b^2).$$

Conjugate: $(\sqrt u-\sqrt v)(\sqrt u+\sqrt v)=u-v$, so $\ \sqrt{x+1}-1=\dfrac{x}{\sqrt{x+1}+1}$. Absolute value: $\dfrac{|x-a|}{x-a}=+1$ for $x>a$, $-1$ for $x<a$. Difference quotients (results of Exercises XXVIII–XXX, and the standard ones):

$$\lim_{h\to0}\frac{(x+h)^2-x^2}{h}=2x,$$
$$\lim_{h\to0}\frac{\sqrt{x+h}-\sqrt x}{h}=\frac1{2\sqrt x},$$
$$\lim_{h\to0}\frac{\tfrac1{x+h}-\tfrac1x}{h}=-\frac1{x^2}.$$

Unit 3 — Idea of Continuity

Continuity at $c$: $f(c)$ exists, $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)$ exists, and $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)=f(c)$. Equivalent: $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to c^-}f(x)=\lim_{x\to c^+}f(x)=f(c)$. On $[a,b]$: continuous on $(a,b)$, $\lim_{x\to a^+}f=f(a)$, $\lim_{x\to b^-}f=f(b)$. Polynomial: continuous on $(-\infty,\infty)$. Rational $p/q$: continuous wherever $q\ne0$. Radical: $\sqrt{g(x)}$ has domain $\{g(x)\ge0\}$. Types: removable (limit exists $\ne f(c)$ or $f(c)$ undefined); non-removable: jump (finite, one-sided limits differ) or infinite ($\pm\infty$). Removing a removable discontinuity: set $f(c):=\lim_{x\to c}f(x)$. Factorisations: $x^2-a^2=(x-a)(x+a)$, $x^3-a^3=(x-a)(x^2+ax+a^2)$. Greatest-integer function $\lfloor x\rfloor$ (the course writes $[[x]]$): continuous on every $(n,n+1)$ ($n\in\mathbb Z$); at each integer the left limit is $n-1$, the right limit is $n$.

Unit 4 — The Derivative as Limit of Rate of Change

First principle

$$\frac{dy}{dx}=\lim_{\delta x\to0}\frac{f(x+\delta x)-f(x)}{\delta x},$$
$$m=\frac{\Delta y}{\Delta x}=\frac{y_2-y_1}{x_2-x_1}.$$

Rules

$$\frac d{dx}(c)=0,\qquad \frac d{dx}(ax^n)=a\,n\,x^{n-1},$$
$$\frac d{dx}\big(f\pm g\big)=f'\pm g'.$$

Binomial expansion (positive integer $n$, used on p.71–72)

$$(x+\delta x)^n=x^n+nx^{n-1}\delta x+\frac{n(n-1)}{2!}x^{n-2}(\delta x)^2+\frac{n(n-1)(n-2)}{3!}x^{n-3}(\delta x)^3+\cdots+(\delta x)^n.$$

Trig identities used in the proofs

$$\sin A-\sin B=2\cos\frac{A+B}2\sin\frac{A-B}2,$$
$$\cos A-\cos B=-2\sin\frac{A+B}2\sin\frac{A-B}2,$$
$$\sin^2x+\cos^2x=1.$$
$$\lim_{\theta\to0}\frac{\sin\theta}{\theta}=1.$$

Standard derivatives (course table p.77, with conditions added)

$y$ $\dfrac{dy}{dx}$ Condition
$c$ (constant) $0$
$x^n$ $nx^{n-1}$ any real $n$ (proved in the course only for $n\in\mathbb Z^+$)
$a\,x^n$ $a\,n\,x^{n-1}$ $a$ constant
$\sin x$ $\cos x$ $x$ in radians
$\cos x$ $-\sin x$ $x$ in radians
$\tan x$ $\sec^2x$ radians; $\cos x\ne0$
$\cot x$ $-\operatorname{cosec}^2x$ radians; $\sin x\ne0$
$\sec x$ $\sec x\tan x$ radians; $\cos x\ne0$
$\operatorname{cosec}x$ $-\operatorname{cosec}x\cot x$ radians; $\sin x\ne0$
$e^x$ $e^x$
$e^{kx}$ $k\,e^{kx}$ $k$ constant
$a^x$ $a^x\ln a$ $a>0$ (the course lists this row twice)
$\ln x$ $\dfrac1x$ $x>0$
$\sinh x$ $\cosh x$
$\cosh x$ $\sinh x$

Tangent and normal at $x=a$: tangent $y-f(a)=f'(a)(x-a)$; normal $y-f(a)=-\dfrac{1}{f'(a)}(x-a)$ ($f'(a)\ne0$).

Unit 5 — Differentiation Techniques

$$\frac d{dx}(uv)=uv'+vu',$$
$$\frac d{dx}\frac uv=\frac{vu'-uv'}{v^2}\ (v\ne0),$$
$$\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{dy}{du}\frac{du}{dx}.$$
$$\frac d{dx}(ax+b)^n=na(ax+b)^{n-1},$$
$$\frac d{dx}\sqrt{ax+b}=\frac a{2\sqrt{ax+b}},$$
$$\frac d{dx}\frac1{f(x)}=-\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)^2}.$$
$$(\sin x)'=\cos x,\qquad (\cos x)'=-\sin x,$$
$$(\tan x)'=\sec^2x,\qquad (\cot x)'=-\operatorname{cosec}^2x,$$
$$(e^x)'=e^x,$$
$$(e^{ax+b})'=ae^{ax+b},\qquad (x^n)'=nx^{n-1}.$$
$$\text{Implicit: }\ \frac d{dx}f(y)=f'(y)\frac{dy}{dx},$$
$$\frac d{dx}(xy)=y+x\frac{dy}{dx}.$$

Shortcut (not in the course; fine for checking answers): if the curve is $F(x,y)=0$, then $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=-\dfrac{F_x}{F_y}$ where $F_x,F_y$ are the partial derivatives.

$$\text{Tangent: }y-y_1=m(x-x_1),$$
$$\text{normal slope }=-\frac1m.$$

Unit 6 — Integration: indefinite integrals and techniques

Basic rules

$$\int x^n\,dx=\frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1}+c\ (n\ne-1),$$
$$\int\frac1x\,dx=\ln|x|+c,$$
$$\int k\,f\,dx=k\int f\,dx,$$
$$\int(f\pm g)\,dx=\int f\pm\int g .$$

Course table (p.99), with corrected conditions

$$\int e^x\,dx=e^x,\qquad \int e^{kx}\,dx=\frac{e^{kx}}k\ (k\ne0),$$
$$\int a^x\,dx=\frac{a^x}{\ln a}\ (a>0,\ a\ne1),$$
$$\int\sin x\,dx=-\cos x,\qquad \int\cos x\,dx=\sin x,$$
$$\int\sec^2x\,dx=\tan x,$$
$$\int\sinh x\,dx=\cosh x,\qquad \int\cosh x\,dx=\sinh x,$$
$$\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1-x^2}}=\sin^{-1}x\ (|x|<1),$$
$$\int\frac{-dx}{\sqrt{1-x^2}}=\cos^{-1}x\ (|x|<1),$$
$$\int\frac{dx}{1+x^2}=\tan^{-1}x,$$
$$\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{x^2+1}}=\sinh^{-1}x,$$
$$\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{x^2-1}}=\cosh^{-1}x\ (x>1),$$
$$\int\frac{dx}{1-x^2}=\tanh^{-1}x\ (|x|<1)$$
$$\Big(=\tfrac12\ln\Big|\tfrac{1+x}{1-x}\Big|\ \text{for all }x\ne\pm1\Big).$$

(Add $+c$ to every line.)

Not in the course's table, but used in this chapter

$$\int\tan x\,dx=-\ln|\cos x|,$$
$$\int\cot x\,dx=\ln|\sin x|,$$
$$\int\frac{dx}{x^2+a^2}=\frac1a\tan^{-1}\frac xa,\qquad \int f f'\,dx=\tfrac12f^2,$$
$$\int\ln x\,dx=x\ln x-x .$$

Techniques

$$\int f(ax+b)\,dx=\frac{F(ax+b)}{a},$$
$$\int\frac{f'}{f}\,dx=\ln|f|,$$
$$\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du,$$
$$\int e^{ax}\sin bx\,dx=\frac{e^{ax}(a\sin bx-b\cos bx)}{a^2+b^2},$$
$$\int e^{ax}\cos bx\,dx=\frac{e^{ax}(a\cos bx+b\sin bx)}{a^2+b^2}.$$

Unit 7 — Definite integrals: FTC, evaluation, substitution with limits, area

$$\int_a^bf\,dx=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^nf(x_i^*)\Delta x,$$
$$\Delta x=\frac{b-a}n;\qquad \sum_{i=1}^ni=\frac{n(n+1)}2,$$
$$\sum_{i=1}^ni^2=\frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}6 .$$
$$\textbf{FTC 1: }\ \frac d{dx}\int_a^xf(t)\,dt=f(x);$$
$$\frac d{dx}\int_a^{g(x)}f(t)\,dt=f(g(x))g'(x).$$
$$\textbf{FTC 2: }\ \int_a^bf\,dx=F(b)-F(a).$$
$$\int_a^bf\,dx=-\int_b^af\,dx,$$
$$\int_a^bf\,dx=\int_a^cf\,dx+\int_c^bf\,dx,$$
$$\int_{-a}^af\,dx=\begin{cases}2\int_0^af\,dx&f\text{ even}\\0&f\text{ odd}\end{cases}$$
$$\int_a^bf(g(x))g'(x)\,dx=\int_{g(a)}^{g(b)}f(z)\,dz,$$
$$\int_a^bu\,dv=\Big[uv\Big]_a^b-\int_a^bv\,du .$$
$$\text{Average value}=\frac1{b-a}\int_a^bf\,dx;$$
$$\text{Area between curves}=\int_a^b(f_{\text{top}}-f_{\text{bottom}})\,dx;$$
$$\text{Total area}=\int_a^b|f|\,dx .$$
$$\text{Volume about the }x\text{-axis: }V=\pi\int_a^by^2\,dx .$$

Unit 8 — Volume of Solids of Revolution by Definite Integral

Disc / washer

$$V_x=\pi\int_a^b y^2\,dx,\qquad V_y=\pi\int_c^d x^2\,dy,$$
$$V_x^{\text{washer}}=\pi\int_a^b\big(y_{\text{top}}^2-y_{\text{bottom}}^2\big)dx .$$

Shells (extra): $V_y=2\pi\int_a^b x\,f(x)\,dx$ (region under $y=f(x)$, $x\ge0$). Shifted axis (extra): about the line $y=k$, replace $y$ by the distance to the axis: radius $=|f(x)-k|$; washer outer minus inner radii measured from the line.

Results to know (sympy-verified)

$$V_{\text{cylinder}}=\pi r^2h,\qquad V_{\text{cone}}=\tfrac13\pi r^2h,$$
$$V_{\text{sphere}}=\tfrac43\pi r^3,\qquad V_{\text{hemisphere}}=\tfrac23\pi r^3 .$$
$$V_{\text{frustum}}=\tfrac13\pi h\,(r_1^2+r_1r_2+r_2^2),$$
$$V_{\text{cap of height }h}=\tfrac13\pi h^2(3r-h),$$
$$V_{\text{ellipsoid}}\ (\tfrac{x^2}{A^2}+\tfrac{y^2}{B^2}=1\text{ about }x)=\tfrac43\pi AB^2 .$$
$$V_{\text{segment}}\ (a\le y\le a+h)=\pi h\Big(r^2-a^2-ah-\tfrac{h^2}3\Big)=\tfrac{\pi h}{3}\big(3r^2-3a^2-3ah-h^2\big),$$
$$V_{\text{torus}}\ (\text{circle radius }r,\ \text{centre at distance }b>r\text{ from the axis})=2\pi^2br^2\quad(\text{extra}).$$

Useful integrals: $\int x^n\,dx=\frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1}$; $\int\sin^2x\,dx=\frac x2-\frac{\sin2x}4$ (since $\sin^2x=\frac{1-\cos2x}2$); $(a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2$.

All eight units follow, in course order. Each keeps the course's own section structure; both answer sets stay folded away, and the outside problems sit in a marked box of their own.

Unit 1 of 8

Unit 1 — Functions and Graphs

Page convention. The unit pages carry no printed page numbers. "p.N" always means the PDF page N of MTH102.pdf (the unit is PDF pp.18–40). Figure numbers (1.12, 1.21 …) and example numbers are the course's own; the composite-function example on p.33 has no number, and the section numbers in the text repeat (two sections "1.6", see Errata).

Likely to be examined. The unit objectives (pp.18–19) are only a topic list: definition of a function, function notation, graphs of functions, combinations of functions, inverse function. The unit has no section called "Self-Assessment Exercise"; its self-tests are the Checkpoints (pp.22, 25, 26, 31–32, 34, 35, 36, 38) and the exercise set "1.6 Exercises" with questions 1–19 (pp.38–40). No tutor-marked assignment is labelled in this unit, so treat the exercise set as the assignment-style material. The course guide (pp.11–12) says: four TMAs (best three count) = 30 %, final exam = 70 %, two hours, three questions out of at least five. It does not say which topics are examined.

What you must be able to do

  • State the definition of a function, and decide from an equation or a graph whether $y$ is a function of $x$.
  • Use function notation: evaluate $f(a)$, $f(x+h)$, $f(-x)$ and simplify the difference quotient $\dfrac{f(x+\Delta x)-f(x)}{\Delta x}$.
  • Find the domain and range of a function (formula, piecewise, implied domain).
  • Apply the vertical line test (function?) and the horizontal line test (one-to-one?).
  • Form $f+g$, $f-g$, $fg$, $f/g$ and the composites $f(g(x))$, $g(f(x))$, with their domains.
  • Test whether two functions are inverses of each other ($f(g(x))=x$ and $g(f(x))=x$) and find $f^{-1}$.
  • Explain why a function that is not one-to-one has no inverse, and restrict the domain to get one.

Key ideas

1. Function, domain, range

Intuition. A function is a machine: put in an input $x$, get out exactly one output $y$. Two different inputs may give the same output; one input may never give two outputs.

Definition (p.19). A function from a set $A$ (the domain) to a set $B$ assigns to each $x\in A$ exactly one $y\in B$. The range is the set of outputs that actually occur, $\{f(x):x\in A\}$ (the course's second definition calls the whole target set "the range": use the first one, see Errata).

  • Implied domain (p.27): if only a formula is given, the domain is every real $x$ for which the formula makes sense. Exclude (i) zero denominators, (ii) even roots of negative numbers, and later (iii) logarithms of non-positive numbers.
  • $f(x)$ is "$f$ of $x$", the value of $f$ at $x$. It does not mean $f\times x$ (p.24).
  • To test an equation: solve for $y$. If the result has $\pm$ (two values of $y$ for some $x$) it does not define $y$ as a function of $x$ (pp.20–23).

2. Graphs and the two line tests

The graph of $f$ is the set of points $(x,f(x))$ (p.27). - Vertical line test. Every vertical line meets the graph at most once $\iff$ the graph is the graph of a function of $x$. - One-to-one. $f$ is one-to-one if $f(a)=f(b)\Rightarrow a=b$ (each output comes from only one input). Horizontal line test: every horizontal line meets the graph at most once $\iff$ $f$ is one-to-one. - A piecewise function uses different formulas on different parts of its domain (Example 6b, p.27).

3. Combinations of functions

$(f\pm g)(x)=f(x)\pm g(x)$, $(fg)(x)=f(x)g(x)$, $(f/g)(x)=f(x)/g(x)$ with $g(x)\ne0$. The domain is the intersection of the domains (and $g\ne0$ for the quotient).

Composition (p.33). $(f\circ g)(x)=f(g(x))$: do $g$ first, then $f$. Its domain is $\{x\in\operatorname{dom}g:\ g(x)\in\operatorname{dom}f\}$. In general $f\circ g\ne g\circ f$.

4. Inverse functions

Intuition. The inverse undoes the function: to undo "multiply by 2" divide by 2; to undo "add 8" subtract 8 (p.34). Undo the steps in reverse order.

Definition (p.34). $g=f^{-1}$ if $f(g(x))=x$ for every $x$ in the domain of $g$ and $g(f(x))=x$ for every $x$ in the domain of $f$. Then

$$\operatorname{dom}f=\operatorname{range}f^{-1},\qquad \operatorname{range}f=\operatorname{dom}f^{-1}.$$
  • Existence: $f$ has an inverse function iff $f$ is one-to-one (p.36). If not, restrict the domain to a part where it is one-to-one (e.g. $x^2$ on $x\ge0$).
  • Method (p.35): write $y=f(x)$; swap $x\leftrightarrow y$; solve for $y$; state the domain of $f^{-1}$ (= range of $f$). Check both $f(f^{-1}(x))=x$ and $f^{-1}(f(x))=x$.
  • Graph: the graphs of $f$ and $f^{-1}$ are mirror images in the line $y=x$; the point $(a,b)$ on $f$ corresponds to $(b,a)$ on $f^{-1}$.
  • $f^{-1}(x)$ is not $1/f(x)$.
  • $(f\circ g)^{-1}=g^{-1}\circ f^{-1}$ (undo in reverse order).

Formula sheet

Function value and difference quotient

$$f(a)=\text{output at }x=a,$$
$$\frac{f(x+\Delta x)-f(x)}{\Delta x}\ (\Delta x\ne0).$$

For $f(x)=x^2+bx+c$: $\dfrac{f(x+\Delta x)-f(x)}{\Delta x}=2x+\Delta x+b$.

Implied domain rules: $\dfrac1{g(x)}$: $g(x)\ne0$. $\ \sqrt{g(x)}$: $g(x)\ge0$. $\ \sqrt{g(x)}$ has range $\ge0$.

Line tests: vertical $\Rightarrow$ function; horizontal $\Rightarrow$ one-to-one $\Rightarrow$ has an inverse.

Composition: $(f\circ g)(x)=f(g(x))$; $\operatorname{dom}(f\circ g)=\{x\in\operatorname{dom}g:g(x)\in\operatorname{dom}f\}$.

Inverse: $f(f^{-1}(x))=x$ ($x\in\operatorname{dom}f^{-1}$), $f^{-1}(f(x))=x$ ($x\in\operatorname{dom}f$); $(f\circ g)^{-1}=g^{-1}\circ f^{-1}$.

Standard inverses

$f(x)$ $f^{-1}(x)$ Condition
$ax+b$ $\dfrac{x-b}a$ $a\ne0$
$\dfrac1x$ $\dfrac1x$ $x\ne0$
$x^3$ $\sqrt[3]x$ all real $x$
$x^2$ $\sqrt x$ $x\ge0$
$x^2+c$ $\sqrt{x-c}$ $x\ge0$, domain of $f^{-1}$: $x\ge c$
$\dfrac{ax+b}{cx+d}$ $\dfrac{dx-b}{-cx+a}$ $ad-bc\ne0$

Worked examples

Example 1 — Is $y$ a function of $x$? (Course Examples 1 and 2, pp.20–23; Checkpoint p.22)

Solve each equation for $y$. (a) $x+y=1\Rightarrow y=1-x$: one $y$ for each $x$. Function. (The course prints "$y=v-x$" on p.20; it means $1-x$.) (b) $x^2+y^2=1\Rightarrow y=\pm\sqrt{1-x^2}$. At $x=0$: $y=1$ or $y=-1$. Not a function. (c) $x^2+y=1\Rightarrow y=1-x^2$. Function. (d) $x+y^2=1\Rightarrow y=\pm\sqrt{1-x}$. At $x=0$: $y=\pm1$. Not a function. Course Example 2 (p.22; the printed equation (a) lost its "$=4$"): $x^2+y=4\Rightarrow y=4-x^2$ function; $x^2+y^2=4\Rightarrow y=\pm\sqrt{4-x^2}$, and at $x=1$ $y=\pm\sqrt3$: not a function. Checkpoint p.22: (a) $y=x-1$ yes; (b) $y=\pm\sqrt{4-x^2}$ no; (c) $y=\pm\sqrt{2-x}$ no; (d) $y=x^2$ yes; (e) $y=6-2x$ yes; (f) $y=\pm\sqrt{1-x^2}$ no. (sympy-verified.)

Example 2 — Evaluating functions (Course Examples 3, 4, 7, pp.24–25, 30)

(a) Example 3 (p.24). $f(x)=2x^2-4x+1$: $f(-1)=2+4+1=7$; $f(0)=1$; $f(2)=8-8+1=1$. Since $f(0)=f(2)=1$ with $0\ne2$, $f$ is not one-to-one (the line $y=1$ meets the graph twice). (b) Example 4 (p.25). $f(x)=x^2+5x+5$. $f(x+2)=(x+2)^2+5(x+2)+5=x^2+4x+4+5x+10+5=x^2+9x+19$. $f(-x)=x^2-5x+5$. (c) Example 7 and its Checkpoint (pp.25, 30). $f(x)=x^2-5x+1$: $f(0)=1$, $f(1)=1-5+1=\mathbf{-3}$, $f(4)=16-20+1=-3$. (The course's Example 7 on p.30 prints $f(1)=-5$: wrong; p.25 has the right $-3$.) The question "is $f$ one-to-one?" is left unanswered on p.30: no, because $f(0)=f(5)=1$ (roots of $x^2-5x=0$). The vertex is at $x=\tfrac52$, $f(\tfrac52)=-\tfrac{21}4$. (sympy-verified.)

Example 3 — Difference quotients (Course Example 5, p.26; Exercises 9–11, p.39)

(a) Example 5. $f(x)=x^2-4x+7$. $f(x+\Delta x)=x^2+2x\Delta x+(\Delta x)^2-4x-4\Delta x+7$, so

$$\frac{f(x+\Delta x)-f(x)}{\Delta x}=\frac{2x\Delta x+(\Delta x)^2-4\Delta x}{\Delta x}=2x+\Delta x-4\quad(\Delta x\ne0).$$

(b) Exercise 11. $f(x)=\dfrac1{x-2}$:

$$\frac{\frac1{x+\Delta x-2}-\frac1{x-2}}{\Delta x}=\frac{(x-2)-(x+\Delta x-2)}{\Delta x\,(x+\Delta x-2)(x-2)}=\frac{-1}{(x+\Delta x-2)(x-2)} .$$

(c) Exercise 10. $g(x)=\sqrt{x+3}$: multiply top and bottom by the conjugate:

$$\frac{\sqrt{x+\Delta x+3}-\sqrt{x+3}}{\Delta x}=\frac{(x+\Delta x+3)-(x+3)}{\Delta x(\sqrt{x+\Delta x+3}+\sqrt{x+3})}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{x+\Delta x+3}+\sqrt{x+3}} .$$

(sympy: the difference of the two sides is $0$; as $\Delta x\to0$ it tends to $\frac1{2\sqrt{x+3}}$, which is where this is used in Unit 2.)

Example 4 — Domain and range (Course Example 6, pp.27–28)

(a) $y=\sqrt{x-1}$. Need $x-1\ge0$: domain $[1,\infty)$ (the point $x=1$ is included, $y=0$). A square root is never negative and takes every value $\ge0$: range $[0,\infty)$. (b) $y=\begin{cases}1-x,&x<1\\\sqrt{x-1},&x\ge1\end{cases}$. The two pieces cover $x<1$ and $x\ge1$, so the domain is all real $x$. For $x<1$: $1-x>0$, range $(0,\infty)$. For $x\ge1$: range $[0,\infty)$. Union: range $[0,\infty)$. $y=1$ comes from $x=0$ and $x=2$, so the function is not one-to-one. (c) New. $y=\dfrac1{x^2-4}$: exclude $x=\pm2$ (p.27): domain $(-\infty,-2)\cup(-2,2)\cup(2,\infty)$. Range: for $|x|>2$ the denominator is positive, so $y>0$; for $|x|<2$ it is in $[-4,0)$, so $y\le-\tfrac14$. Range $(-\infty,-\tfrac14]\cup(0,\infty)$. (d) Exercises 5, 6. $f(x)=x^3$: domain $\mathbb R$, range $\mathbb R$. $f(x)=4-x^2$: domain $\mathbb R=(-\infty,\infty)$, range $(-\infty,4]$. (sympy-verified.)

Example 5 — Line tests and one-to-one (Course pp.28–32)

Vertical line test (p.29). (a) ellipse: a vertical line meets it twice, not a function. (b) straight line, function. (c) upward parabola $y=ax^2+\dots$, function. (d) sideways parabola, not. Checkpoint pp.31–32. (i) two horizontal lines: a vertical line meets both, not a function. (ii) a vertical line: the one $x$ has infinitely many $y$, not. (iii) downward parabola: function. (iv) vertical ellipse: not. (v) sideways parabola opening left: not. One-to-one. A function of $x$ passes the vertical test; if it also passes the horizontal test it is one-to-one. Examples: the line (b) is one-to-one; the parabola (c) is not (a horizontal line cuts it twice); $y=\sqrt{x-1}$ is (strictly increasing).

Example 6 — Combinations and composition (Course p.33, redone; Exercises 12–14)

(a) Sum, difference, product, quotient (p.33): $f(x)=2x-3$, $g(x)=x^2+1$: $f+g=x^2+2x-2$; $f-g=-x^2+2x-4$; $fg=2x^3-3x^2+2x-3$; $f/g=\dfrac{2x-3}{x^2+1}$ (defined for all real $x$ because $x^2+1\ne0$). All correct. (b) Unnumbered example, p.33. $f(x)=2x-4$, $g(x)=x^2+3$. $f(g(x))=2(x^2+3)-4=2x^2+2=2(x^2+1)$. (The course writes this under the label $g(f(x))$ and gets $2x^2-2$ because of a slip; the right value is $2x^2+2$.) $g(f(x))=(2x-4)^2+3=4x^2-16x+16+3=\mathbf{4x^2-16x+19}$. (The course adds $+1$ instead of $+3$ and prints $4x^2-16x+17$.) (c) Exercise 14. $f(x)=\sqrt x$, $g(x)=x^2-1$. $f(g(1))=f(0)=0$; $g(f(1))=g(1)=0$; $g(f(0))=g(0)=-1$; $f(g(-4))=f(15)=\sqrt{15}$; $f(g(x))=\sqrt{x^2-1}$, defined only when $x^2-1\ge0$: domain $(-\infty,-1]\cup[1,\infty)$; $g(f(x))=(\sqrt x)^2-1=x-1$, but the domain is $x\ge0$ (the domain of $f$ comes first). (sympy-verified.) (d) Exercises 12, 13. $f=2x-5$, $g=5$: $f+g=2x$; $fg=10x-25$; $f/g=\dfrac{2x-5}5$; $f(g(x))=f(5)=5$; $g(f(x))=5$. $f=x^2+1$, $g=x-1$: $f+g=x^2+x$; $fg=x^3-x^2+x-1$; $f/g=\dfrac{x^2+1}{x-1}$ ($x\ne1$); $f(g(x))=x^2-2x+2$; $g(f(x))=x^2$.

Example 7 — Finding and checking inverses (Course Examples 8 and 9, pp.34–36)

(a) Example 8, redone. (a) $f=2x\Rightarrow f^{-1}=\frac x2$. (b) $f=\frac15x\Rightarrow f^{-1}=5x$. (c) $f=x+8\Rightarrow f^{-1}=x-8$. (d) $f=3x+7$: $y=3x+7$, swap $x=3y+7$, $y=\dfrac{x-7}3$. The course prints $\tfrac13(x+7)$: wrong. Check: $f\!\big(\frac{x-7}3\big)=x-7+7=x$ ✓, whereas $f\!\big(\frac{x+7}3\big)=x+14\ne x$. (e) $x^3\to\sqrt[3]x$. (f) $\frac1x\to\frac1x$ (it is its own inverse). (b) Example 9. $f(x)=\sqrt{2x-3}$. Domain: $2x-3\ge0$, $x\ge\frac32$; range $[0,\infty)$. $y=\sqrt{2x-3}\ \to\ x=\sqrt{2y-3}$, so $x\ge0$ and $x^2=2y-3$, $y=\dfrac{x^2+3}2$. So $f^{-1}(x)=\dfrac{x^2+3}2$ with domain $x\ge0$ (= range of $f$) and range $[\frac32,\infty)$ (= domain of $f$). Checks: $f(f^{-1}(x))=\sqrt{2\cdot\frac{x^2+3}2-3}=\sqrt{x^2}=x$ for $x\ge0$ ✓. $f^{-1}(f(x))=\dfrac{(\sqrt{2x-3})^2+3}2=\dfrac{2x}2=x$ for $x\ge\frac32$ ✓. Points: $(\frac32,0),(2,1)\in f$ and $(0,\frac32),(1,2)\in f^{-1}$ (swapped coordinates) ✓. (The course's p.36 labels the first check "$f^{-1}(f(x))$"; it is $f(f^{-1}(x))$.) (c) Checkpoint p.35. $f=\frac15x$: $f^{-1}=5x$. $f=3x+2$: $f^{-1}(x)=\dfrac{x-2}3$.

Example 8 — No inverse; restricting the domain (Course Example 10, p.37; Checkpoints pp.36, 38; Exercises 16, 17)

(a) Example 10. $f(x)=x^2-1$ on $\mathbb R$: $f(2)=f(-2)=3$, so $f$ is not one-to-one; solving $x=y^2-1$ gives $y=\pm\sqrt{x+1}$, two values, not a function. No inverse. Restrict to $x\ge0$: range of $f$ is $[-1,\infty)$ and $f^{-1}(x)=\sqrt{x+1}$, $x\ge-1$. (b) Checkpoint p.38. $f(x)=x^2+4$: $f(1)=f(-1)=5$, not one-to-one, no inverse on $\mathbb R$. (c) Checkpoint p.36. $f(x)=x^2+2$, $x\ge0$: $x=y^2+2$, $y=\sqrt{x-2}$ (positive root because $y\ge0$). $f^{-1}(x)=\sqrt{x-2}$, $x\ge2$ (the range of $f$ is $[2,\infty)$). (d) Exercise 16. $f(x)=9-x^2$, $x\ge0$ (range $(-\infty,9]$); $g(x)=\sqrt{9-x}$, $x\le9$ (range $[0,\infty)$). $f(g(x))=9-(9-x)=x$ for $x\le9$ ✓; $g(f(x))=\sqrt{9-(9-x^2)}=\sqrt{x^2}=|x|=x$ because $x\ge0$ ✓. The condition $x\ge0$ in $f$ is exactly what makes the second check work. (e) Exercise 17. $f(x)=\sqrt{9-x^2}$, $0\le x\le3$: range $[0,3]$. $x=\sqrt{9-y^2}\Rightarrow y=\sqrt{9-x^2}$ ($y\ge0$): $f^{-1}=f$, domain $[0,3]$ (the quarter circle is symmetric about $y=x$). (sympy-verified.)

Example 9 — Exam-level: rational inverse and composite domain (new)

(a) $h(x)=\dfrac{2x+1}{x-3}$. Domain $x\ne3$. Swap: $x=\dfrac{2y+1}{y-3}\Rightarrow xy-3x=2y+1\Rightarrow y(x-2)=3x+1$, so

$$h^{-1}(x)=\frac{3x+1}{x-2},\quad x\ne2.$$

Range of $h$ is $\mathbb R\setminus\{2\}$ (it never takes the value $2$) = domain of $h^{-1}$ ✓. Check: $h(h^{-1}(x))=x$ and $h^{-1}(h(x))=x$ (sympy simplifies both to $x$). (b) $f(x)=\sqrt{4-x}$, $g(x)=\dfrac1x$. $f(g(x))=\sqrt{4-\frac1x}$ needs $x\ne0$ and $4-\frac1x\ge0$. If $x>0$: $\frac1x\le4$, $x\ge\frac14$. If $x<0$: $\frac1x<0$, always true. Domain $(-\infty,0)\cup[\frac14,\infty)$; range $[0,2)\cup(2,\infty)$ (value $2$ would need $\frac1x=0$). (c) $f(x)=2x+1$, $g(x)=x^3$. $(f\circ g)(x)=2x^3+1$; its inverse is $\sqrt[3]{\dfrac{x-1}2}$, which equals $g^{-1}(f^{-1}(x))=\sqrt[3]{\frac{x-1}2}$ ✓ (reverse order). (sympy-verified.)

Common mistakes

  • Reading $f(x)$ as $f\times x$. It is the value of $f$ at $x$ (p.24).
  • Forgetting the $\pm$. $y^2=d$ gives two values; the equation then does not define $y$ as a function of $x$.
  • Mixing up the two line tests. Vertical test: is it a function? Horizontal test: is it one-to-one (has an inverse)? A parabola $y=x^2$ passes the first and fails the second.
  • Composition in the wrong order. $f(g(x))$ puts $g(x)$ into $f$. With $f=2x-4$, $g=x^2+3$: $f(g(x))=2x^2+2$ but $g(f(x))=4x^2-16x+19$.
  • Ignoring the domain of a composite. $\sqrt{x}$ then $x^2-1$: $g(f(x))=x-1$ looks fine for negative $x$, but $f$ is not defined there.
  • Dropping the "$+3$" when substituting into $g(x)=x^2+3$ (the course's own slip on p.33). Always rewrite $g$ with the new input in brackets: $g(\,2x-4\,)=(2x-4)^2+3$.
  • Inverse of $3x+7$ as $\frac13(x+7)$. Undo in reverse order: first subtract $7$, then divide by $3$: $\frac{x-7}3$.
  • $f^{-1}(x)=1/f(x)$. No: $f^{-1}$ is the inverse function.
  • Checking only one of the two inverse identities. Check both, and state the domain where each holds.
  • Forgetting the domain of the inverse $=$ range of $f$ (e.g. $\sqrt{x-2}$ is only for $x\ge2$).
  • Using the wrong root when restricting. For $f=x^2+2$ on $x\ge0$ take $+\sqrt{x-2}$; on $x\le0$ it would be $-\sqrt{x-2}$.
  • Writing $\infty$ with a square bracket: $[0,\infty)$, never $[0,\infty]$.
  • Cancelling in a difference quotient with $\Delta x=0$. The cancellation $\frac{\Delta x(\dots)}{\Delta x}$ is valid only for $\Delta x\ne0$.

Practice problems

  1. Checkpoint p.22 and Exercises 1–4 (pp.38). Which define $y$ as a function of $x$? (a) $x-y=1$; (b) $x^2+y^2=4$; (c) $y^2+x=2$; (d) $x^2-y=0$; (e) $2x+y=6$; (f) $x^2+y^2=1$; (g) $\tfrac12x-6y=-3$; (h) $x^2+y=4$; (i) $y^2=x^2-1$.
  2. Exercises 5, 6 (p.38). Domain and range, interval notation: (a) $f(x)=x^3$; (b) $f(x)=4-x^2$. Also: (c) $f(x)=\sqrt{4-x^2}$; (d) $f(x)=\dfrac1{x-2}$; (e) $f(x)=x^2+2x$.
  3. Checkpoint p.25, Exercises 7, 8 (p.39). (a) $f(x)=x^2-5x+1$: find $f(0),f(1),f(4)$. (b) $f(x)=2x-3$: $f(0)$, $f(-3)$, $f(x-1)$, $f(1+\Delta x)$. (c) $g(x)=\frac1x$: $g(2)$, $g(\frac14)$, $g(x+\Delta x)$, $g(x+\Delta x)-g(x)$.
  4. Checkpoint p.26, Exercises 9–11 (p.39). (a) $f(x)=x^2-2x+3$: $f(x+\Delta x)$ and $\dfrac{f(x+\Delta x)-f(x)}{\Delta x}$. (b) $f(x)=x^2-2x+7$: $f(-5)$, $f(x+4)$, $f(-x)$. (c) Difference quotient of $f=x^3-x$, $g=\sqrt{x+3}$, $f=\dfrac1{x-2}$.
  5. Checkpoint pp.31–32; Exercises 18–19 (p.40). Vertical line test for the five graphs (i)–(v) (two horizontal lines; a vertical line; a downward parabola; a vertical ellipse; a parabola opening left). Does $x^2+y^2=9$ define $y$ as a function of $x$? Does $x^2=xy-1$?
  6. Exercises 12, 13 (pp.39–40). For $f=2x-5$, $g=5$ and for $f=x^2+1$, $g=x-1$ find $f+g$, $fg$, $f/g$, $f(g(x))$, $g(f(x))$.
  7. Checkpoint p.34 and Exercise 14 (p.40). (a) $f=2x+1$, $g=x^2+2$: find $f(g(x))$, $g(f(x))$. (b) $f=\sqrt x$, $g=x^2-1$: find $f(g(1))$, $g(f(1))$, $g(f(0))$, $f(g(-4))$, $f(g(x))$ with its domain, $g(f(x))$ with its domain.
  8. Exercises 15, 16 (p.40). Show $f,g$ are inverses: (a) $f=5x+1$, $g=\frac{x-1}5$; (b) $f=9-x^2$, $x\ge0$, $g=\sqrt{9-x}$, $x\le9$. State the domain and range of each.
  9. Checkpoint p.35, p.36; Exercise 17 (p.40). Find the inverse (with domain): (a) $f=\frac15x$; (b) $f=3x+2$; (c) $f=x^2+2$, $x\ge0$; (d) $f=\sqrt{9-x^2}$, $0\le x\le3$.
  10. Checkpoint p.38. Show that $f(x)=x^2+4$ has no inverse function. Restrict its domain so that it has one and find $f^{-1}$.
  11. For $f(x)=x^2-4x+7$ with domain $x\ge2$: show it is one-to-one, find its range and $f^{-1}$.
  12. $h(x)=\dfrac{x+2}{x-1}$. Find the domain, range and $h^{-1}$, and verify $h(h^{-1}(x))=x$. Is $h$ its own inverse?
Show answers

Answers

  1. (a) $y=x-1$ yes. (b) $y=\pm\sqrt{4-x^2}$ no. (c) $y=\pm\sqrt{2-x}$ no. (d) $y=x^2$ yes. (e) $y=6-2x$ yes. (f) $y=\pm\sqrt{1-x^2}$ no. (g) $y=\frac{x}{12}+\frac12$ yes. (h) $y=4-x^2$ yes. (i) $y=\pm\sqrt{x^2-1}$ no (two values when $|x|>1$).
  2. (a) domain $\mathbb R$, range $\mathbb R$. (b) domain $\mathbb R$, range $(-\infty,4]$. (c) domain $[-2,2]$, range $[0,2]$. (d) domain $(-\infty,2)\cup(2,\infty)$, range $(-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)$. (e) domain $\mathbb R$, range $[-1,\infty)$ (vertex at $x=-1$).
  3. (a) $1,\ -3,\ -3$. (b) $-3,\ -9,\ 2(x-1)-3=2x-5,\ 2(1+\Delta x)-3=2\Delta x-1$. (c) $\frac12,\ 4,\ \dfrac1{x+\Delta x},\ -\dfrac{\Delta x}{x(x+\Delta x)}$.
  4. (a) $x^2+2x\Delta x+(\Delta x)^2-2x-2\Delta x+3$; quotient $=2x+\Delta x-2$. (b) $f(-5)=25+10+7=42$; $f(x+4)=x^2+6x+15$; $f(-x)=x^2+2x+7$. (c) $x^3-x$: $3x^2+3x\Delta x+(\Delta x)^2-1$ (reading $x+\Delta x$; see Errata); $\sqrt{x+3}$: $\dfrac1{\sqrt{x+\Delta x+3}+\sqrt{x+3}}$; $\dfrac1{x-2}$: $\dfrac{-1}{(x+\Delta x-2)(x-2)}$.
  5. (i) not a function; (ii) not; (iii) function; (iv) not; (v) not. $x^2+y^2=9\Rightarrow y=\pm\sqrt{9-x^2}$: not a function. $x^2=xy-1\Rightarrow y=\dfrac{x^2+1}x=x+\frac1x$ for $x\ne0$ (at $x=0$ there is no solution): is a function, domain $x\ne0$.
  6. $f=2x-5,g=5$: $2x$; $10x-25$; $\frac{2x-5}5$; $f(g(x))=5$; $g(f(x))=5$. $f=x^2+1,g=x-1$: $x^2+x$; $x^3-x^2+x-1$; $\dfrac{x^2+1}{x-1}$ ($x\ne1$); $x^2-2x+2$; $x^2$.
  7. (a) $f(g(x))=2(x^2+2)+1=2x^2+5$; $g(f(x))=(2x+1)^2+2=4x^2+4x+3$. (b) $0,\ 0,\ -1,\ \sqrt{15}$; $f(g(x))=\sqrt{x^2-1}$ on $(-\infty,-1]\cup[1,\infty)$; $g(f(x))=x-1$ on $x\ge0$.
  8. (a) $f(g(x))=5\cdot\frac{x-1}5+1=x$; $g(f(x))=\frac{(5x+1)-1}5=x$; both domain and range $\mathbb R$. (b) $f(g(x))=9-(9-x)=x$ ($x\le9$); $g(f(x))=\sqrt{x^2}=x$ ($x\ge0$). $f$: domain $[0,\infty)$, range $(-\infty,9]$; $g$: domain $(-\infty,9]$, range $[0,\infty)$.
  9. (a) $5x$ (domain $\mathbb R$). (b) $\dfrac{x-2}3$ (domain $\mathbb R$). (c) $\sqrt{x-2}$, domain $x\ge2$. (d) $f^{-1}(x)=\sqrt{9-x^2}$, domain $[0,3]$ (so $f^{-1}=f$).
  10. $f(1)=f(-1)=5$ (also the horizontal line $y=5$ meets the graph twice), so $f$ is not one-to-one, no inverse on $\mathbb R$. Restrict to $x\ge0$: $f^{-1}(x)=\sqrt{x-4}$, domain $x\ge4$. (Or $x\le0$: $-\sqrt{x-4}$.)
  11. $f(x)=(x-2)^2+3$. If $a,b\ge2$ and $f(a)=f(b)$ then $(a-2)^2=(b-2)^2$, and since $a-2,b-2\ge0$, $a=b$: one-to-one. Range $[3,\infty)$. Swap: $x=(y-2)^2+3\Rightarrow y=2+\sqrt{x-3}$ (positive root since $y\ge2$): $f^{-1}(x)=2+\sqrt{x-3}$, domain $x\ge3$.
  12. Domain $x\ne1$. Swap $x=\dfrac{y+2}{y-1}\Rightarrow xy-x=y+2\Rightarrow y=\dfrac{x+2}{x-1}$. So $h^{-1}=h$: yes, $h$ is its own inverse. Range $\mathbb R\setminus\{1\}$ = domain of $h^{-1}$. (Verified: $h(h(x))=x$.)

All numerical and symbolic results in the examples and problems were checked with sympy 1.14.0 (inverses, composites, domains, ranges, difference quotients).

Extra practice (from outside sources)

From outside sources — not in the NOUN course text Copied from OpenStax Calculus Volume 1 (CC BY 4.0); each problem names its section and exercise number.

Not from the NOUN course text. These problems come from the free OpenStax Calculus textbooks (CC BY 4.0). Tap Source ↗ to open the original page. Answers are worked out and checked here.

  1. Find the values for the function, if they exist, then simplify: $f(x)=\dfrac{2}{x}$. (a) $f(0)$ (b) $f(1)$ (c) $f(3)$ (d) $f(-x)$ (e) $f(a)$ (f) $f(a+h)$. Source ↗
  2. Find the domain, range, and all zeros/intercepts, if any, of the function $f(x)=-1+\sqrt{x+2}$. Source ↗
  3. For the pair of functions $f(x)=9-x^{2}$, $g(x)=x^{2}-2x-3$, find (a) $f+g$ (b) $f-g$ (c) $f\cdot g$ (d) $f/g$. Determine the domain of each of these new functions. Source ↗
  4. For the pair of functions $f(x)=\dfrac{3}{2x+1}$, $g(x)=\dfrac{2}{x}$, find (a) $(f\circ g)(x)$ and (b) $(g\circ f)(x)$. Simplify the results. Find the domain of each of the results. Source ↗
  5. (a) Find the inverse function, and (b) find the domain and range of the inverse function, for $f(x)=\sqrt{x-1}$. Source ↗
  6. Use composition to determine whether the pair of functions are inverses: $f(x)=5x-7$, $g(x)=\dfrac{x+5}{7}$. Source ↗
  7. Use composition to determine whether the pair of functions are inverses: $f(x)=\dfrac{1}{x-1},\ x\ne1$ and $g(x)=\dfrac1x+1,\ x\ne0$. Source ↗
  8. Use composition to determine whether the pair of functions are inverses: $f(x)=x^{2}+2x+1,\ x\ge-1$ and $g(x)=-1+\sqrt{x},\ x\ge0$. Source ↗
Show answers (extra practice)

Answers (extra practice)

OpenStax prints answers to the odd-numbered exercises; all eight of these are odd, and each answer below agrees with the book's key.

  1. $f(x)=2/x$. (a) $f(0)=2/0$ is undefined (does not exist). (b) $f(1)=2$. (c) $f(3)=\dfrac23$. (d) $f(-x)=\dfrac{2}{-x}=-\dfrac2x$. (e) $f(a)=\dfrac2a$ (for $a\ne0$). (f) $f(a+h)=\dfrac{2}{a+h}$ (for $a+h\ne0$).
  2. Domain: need $x+2\ge0$, so $x\ge-2$, i.e. $[-2,\infty)$. Range: $\sqrt{x+2}\ge0$, so $f(x)\ge-1$, i.e. $[-1,\infty)$ (the minimum $-1$ is reached at $x=-2$). Zero: $-1+\sqrt{x+2}=0\Rightarrow\sqrt{x+2}=1\Rightarrow x=-1$. $y$-intercept: $f(0)=-1+\sqrt2$. So $x\ge-2$; $y\ge-1$; $x$-intercept $x=-1$; $y$-intercept $y=-1+\sqrt2$.
  3. $g(x)=x^2-2x-3=(x-3)(x+1)$ and $f(x)=9-x^2=(3-x)(3+x)$. (a) $f+g=6-2x$, i.e. $-2x+6$; domain $\mathbb R$. (b) $f-g=(9-x^2)-(x^2-2x-3)=-2x^2+2x+12$; domain $\mathbb R$. (c) $fg=(9-x^2)(x^2-2x-3)=-x^4+2x^3+12x^2-18x-27$; domain $\mathbb R$. (d) $\dfrac fg=\dfrac{9-x^2}{(x-3)(x+1)}=\dfrac{-(x-3)(x+3)}{(x-3)(x+1)}=-\dfrac{x+3}{x+1}$; the domain excludes the zeros of $g$: $x\ne-1,\ 3$ (the value $x=3$ stays excluded even though the factor cancels).
  4. (a) $(f\circ g)(x)=f\!\left(\frac2x\right)=\dfrac{3}{2\cdot\frac2x+1}=\dfrac{3}{\frac4x+1}=\dfrac{3x}{4+x}$. Domain: $x\ne0$ (for $g$) and $g(x)\ne-\frac12$, i.e. $\frac2x\ne-\frac12$, $x\ne-4$. So $x\ne0,-4$. (b) $(g\circ f)(x)=g\!\left(\frac3{2x+1}\right)=\dfrac{2}{\frac3{2x+1}}=\dfrac{2(2x+1)}3=\dfrac{4x+2}3$. Domain: $x\ne-\frac12$ (for $f$); $f(x)$ is never $0$, so nothing more is excluded.
  5. Write $y=\sqrt{x-1}$, swap $x\leftrightarrow y$: $x=\sqrt{y-1}$, so $y-1=x^2$ with $x\ge0$ (a square root is non-negative). (a) $f^{-1}(x)=x^2+1$. (b) Domain of $f^{-1}$ = range of $f$ = $x\ge0$; range of $f^{-1}$ = domain of $f$ = $y\ge1$. (Note that $x^2+1$ on all of $\mathbb R$ would not be one-to-one: the restriction $x\ge0$ is part of the answer.)
  6. $f(g(x))=5\cdot\dfrac{x+5}7-7=\dfrac{5x+25-49}7=\dfrac{5x-24}7\ne x$ (for example $x=0$ gives $-\frac{24}7$). So they are not inverses. (The true inverse of $5x-7$ is $\frac{x+7}5$.)
  7. $f(g(x))=\dfrac1{\left(\frac1x+1\right)-1}=\dfrac1{1/x}=x$ for $x\ne0$. $g(f(x))=\dfrac1{1/(x-1)}+1=(x-1)+1=x$ for $x\ne1$. Both equal $x$ on the right domains: they are inverses (range of $f$ is $y\ne0$, which is exactly the domain of $g$).
  8. $f(g(x))=\left(-1+\sqrt x\right)^2+2\left(-1+\sqrt x\right)+1=\left(\sqrt x\right)^2=x$ for $x\ge0$ (since $f(u)=(u+1)^2$ and $u+1=\sqrt x$). $g(f(x))=-1+\sqrt{(x+1)^2}=-1+|x+1|=-1+(x+1)=x$ because $x\ge-1$ makes $x+1\ge0$. Both give $x$: they are inverses. (Without the restriction $x\ge-1$ the second composite would fail: $g(f(-3))=-1+2=1\ne-3$.)

All results above were checked with sympy (evaluation, composites and their domains, inverses, products and quotients), and each odd-numbered answer was compared with the OpenStax answer key (Chapter 1).

  • OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1 §1.4 exercises 189–194 (find $f^{-1}$ and its domain/range) and 199–206 (test inverses by composition); odd answers in the key — inverses and domain restrictions.

Unit 2 of 8

Unit 2 — Limits

Page convention. The course PDF has no printed page numbers on these pages (only a stray "x" at the foot of a few). So "p.N" here always means the PDF page N (course PDF pp.41–59 are Unit 2). Section numbers are the course's own; note that the contents list on p.41 says 2.3–2.6 but the body headings are numbered 2.1–2.4 (see Errata).

Duplicate material. PDF pp.50–59 are not new material: they are a second, re-paginated copy of pp.41–49 (same text, same examples, same exercises; checked by a text diff and by reading the images). The only differences are page breaks, the heading "MODULE ONE / UNIT 2: LIMITS" on p.50, and worse numbering in the copy (property list numbered v–viii, checkpoints numbered 3–4 with letters a, b, b; see Errata). Study pp.41–49; skip pp.50–59. In this chapter a reference such as "p.42 (copy p.51)" gives both places.

Likely to be examined. The unit has the Objectives (p.41: definition of a limit, properties of limits, the limit of a polynomial, techniques for evaluating limits), the Checkpoints after Examples 3–8 (pp.43, 45, 46, 47, 48) and the 2.7 Exercises I–XXX (pp.48–49). It has no tutor-marked assignment labelled as such. The Course Guide (pp.10–12) says assessment is tutor-marked assignments (the best three of four count 30%) plus a two-hour final exam worth 70% in which you answer three questions out of at least five. Nothing in the PDF says which topics are in the exam, so treat everything below as "likely", not "known".

What you must be able to do

  • State what $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)=L$ means, in words, and say when a limit does not exist (different one-sided limits, or unbounded behaviour).
  • Evaluate limits by direct substitution when the function is a polynomial (or any function that is continuous at $c$) and use the limit laws (sum, product, quotient, power, root).
  • Recognise the $\tfrac00$ form and remove it with the Replacement Theorem: factor and cancel, divide out a common factor, or rationalise a numerator.
  • Compute one-sided limits, including for $|x|$-type and piecewise functions, and decide whether the two-sided limit exists.
  • Recognise unbounded behaviour ($\pm\infty$) and say correctly that the limit does not exist.
  • Evaluate difference-quotient limits such as $\displaystyle\lim_{\Delta x\to0}\frac{f(x+\Delta x)-f(x)}{\Delta x}$ (Exercises XXVIII–XXX).

Key ideas

1. What a limit is (p.41)

Intuition. $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)=L$ means: as $x$ gets close to $c$ from both sides (but $x\ne c$ is allowed to be ignored), the values $f(x)$ get arbitrarily close to one number $L$. The value $f(c)$ itself is irrelevant: it may differ from $L$, or not exist (Example 2c, p.43).

Precise statement (beyond the course text, which only gives the words above). For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is a $\delta>0$ such that $0<|x-c|<\delta\Rightarrow|f(x)-L|<\varepsilon$. You do not need it for the unit's examples; the techniques below are enough.

2. Direct substitution and the limit laws (pp.41–42)

The course (p.41, "2.2 Properties of Limit") says: often $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)=f(c)$ and the limit can be found by direct substitution. That is true exactly when $f$ is continuous at $c$ (Unit 3). It is true for every polynomial (p.43):

$$\lim_{x\to c}p(x)=p(c).$$

Basic limits ($b,c$ real, $n$ a positive integer): $\lim_{x\to c}b=b$, $\lim_{x\to c}x=c$, $\lim_{x\to c}x^n=c^n$, and $\lim_{x\to c}\sqrt[n]{x}=\sqrt[n]{c}$ (for even $n$ you need $c>0$ for a two-sided limit).

Limit laws. If $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)=L$ and $\lim_{x\to c}g(x)=k$ (both finite numbers):

$$\lim[bf]=bL,\quad \lim[f\pm g]=L\pm k,\quad \lim[fg]=Lk,\quad \lim\frac fg=\frac Lk\ \ (k\ne0),\quad \lim[f]^n=L^n,\quad \lim\sqrt[n]{f}=\sqrt[n]{L}.$$

For the root with $n$ even, $L>0$ is the safe condition (the course says "positive"; $L=0$ also works if $f(x)\ge0$ near $c$).

The quotient law needs $k\ne0$. If $k=0$ you may not just write "$\frac L0$": either the numerator also tends to $0$ (form $\tfrac00$, simplify first) or the denominator tends to $0$ alone (unbounded behaviour, §5).

3. The Replacement Theorem (p.43)

If $f(x)=g(x)$ for all $x\ne c$ (near $c$) and $\lim_{x\to c}g(x)$ exists, then $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)$ exists and equals it. In words: two functions that agree except at the single point $c$ have the same limit at $c$. Tool: the factor theorem. For a polynomial $p$, $p(c)=0\iff(x-c)$ is a factor of $p(x)$. So if numerator and denominator vanish at $c$, both have the factor $(x-c)$.

4. Techniques for $\tfrac00$ (pp.44–47)

Situation Technique Course example
polynomial over polynomial, both $0$ at $c$ factor, cancel $(x-c)$, substitute Ex 4 (p.44), Ex 5 (p.45)
square root in numerator (or denominator) multiply top and bottom by the conjugate, then cancel Ex 6 (p.46)
difference quotient $\frac{f(x+\Delta x)-f(x)}{\Delta x}$ expand or rationalise until $\Delta x$ cancels Exercises XXVIII–XXX

5. One-sided limits and unbounded behaviour (pp.47–48)

  • $\lim_{x\to c^-}f(x)$ (from the left, $x<c$) and $\lim_{x\to c^+}f(x)$ (from the right, $x>c$).
  • The two-sided limit exists iff both one-sided limits exist and are equal. If they differ (Example 7: $-2$ and $2$), the limit does not exist.
  • A limit can fail to exist because $f(x)$ grows without bound (p.48). Writing $\lim f=\infty$ describes the failure; it does not say the limit exists. For $\frac{3}{x-2}$ at $2$ the left side tends to $-\infty$ and the right side to $+\infty$, so there is no single value at all.

Formula sheet

Laws ($\lim f=L$, $\lim g=k$ finite):

$$\lim(bf)=bL,\qquad \lim(f\pm g)=L\pm k,$$
$$\lim(fg)=Lk,\qquad \lim\frac fg=\frac Lk\ (k\ne0),$$
$$\lim f^n=L^n,\qquad \lim\sqrt[n]{f}=\sqrt[n]{L}.$$

Polynomial: $\lim_{x\to c}p(x)=p(c)$. Existence: $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)=L\iff\lim_{x\to c^-}f(x)=\lim_{x\to c^+}f(x)=L$. Replacement: $f=g$ for $x\ne c\Rightarrow\lim_{x\to c}f=\lim_{x\to c}g$ (if the latter exists).

Factorisations used again and again:

$$a^2-b^2=(a-b)(a+b),$$
$$a^3-b^3=(a-b)(a^2+ab+b^2),$$
$$a^3+b^3=(a+b)(a^2-ab+b^2).$$

Conjugate: $(\sqrt u-\sqrt v)(\sqrt u+\sqrt v)=u-v$, so $\ \sqrt{x+1}-1=\dfrac{x}{\sqrt{x+1}+1}$. Absolute value: $\dfrac{|x-a|}{x-a}=+1$ for $x>a$, $-1$ for $x<a$. Difference quotients (results of Exercises XXVIII–XXX, and the standard ones):

$$\lim_{h\to0}\frac{(x+h)^2-x^2}{h}=2x,$$
$$\lim_{h\to0}\frac{\sqrt{x+h}-\sqrt x}{h}=\frac1{2\sqrt x},$$
$$\lim_{h\to0}\frac{\tfrac1{x+h}-\tfrac1x}{h}=-\frac1{x^2}.$$

Worked examples

Example 1 — Direct substitution (Course Examples 1 and 3, pp.42–43; Checkpoint 2, p.43)

Solution. A polynomial has $\lim_{x\to c}p(x)=p(c)$, so substitute. - Course Example 1: $\lim_{x\to1}(x^2+1)=1^2+1=\mathbf2$. - Course Example 3: by the sum/difference law, $\lim_{x\to2}(x^2+2x-3)=\lim x^2+\lim2x-\lim3=4+4-3=\mathbf5$. (Directly: $p(2)=4+4-3=5$.) - Checkpoint 2 (p.43): $\lim_{x\to2}(2x^2-x+4)=2\cdot4-2+4=\mathbf{10}$.

Example 2 — Three limits at $x=1$ (Course Example 2, pp.42–43, redone; Checkpoint 1, p.43)

Find $\lim_{x\to1}f(x)$ for (a) $f=\dfrac{x^2-1}{x-1}$, (b) $f=\dfrac{|x-1|}{x-1}$, (c) $f=\begin{cases}x,&x\ne1\\0,&x=1.\end{cases}$

Solution. (a) Substituting gives $\tfrac00$, so factor: for $x\ne1$, $\dfrac{(x-1)(x+1)}{x-1}=x+1$. By the Replacement Theorem the limit is $\lim_{x\to1}(x+1)=\mathbf2$. (b) Do not substitute: $\frac{|1-1|}{1-1}=\frac00$ is undefined, not $0$ (the course writes "$=0$", which is wrong; see Errata). For $x>1$, $|x-1|=x-1$, so $f=1$; for $x<1$, $|x-1|=-(x-1)$, so $f=-1$. Left limit $-1$, right limit $+1$: they differ, so the limit does not exist. (c) For $x\ne1$, $f(x)=x\to1$. The value $f(1)=0$ is ignored: the limit is $\mathbf1$ (and it is not $f(1)$).

Checkpoint 1 (p.43), same method at $x=2$: (a) $\frac{x^2-4}{x-2}=x+2\to\mathbf4$; (b) $\frac{|x-2|}{x-2}$: left $-1$, right $+1$, does not exist; (c) $f(x)=x^2$ near $2$ (the printed "$0,\ x=2$" does not change the limit): $\mathbf4$. (sympy: 4, one-sided $-1$ and $+1$, 4.)

Example 3 — Factor and cancel (Course Example 4, p.44; Checkpoint p.45)

Find $\lim_{x\to1}\dfrac{x^3-1}{x-1}$ and $\lim_{x\to2}\dfrac{x^3-8}{x-2}$.

Solution. Numerator and denominator are both $0$ at $x=1$ ($1-1=0$), so $(x-1)$ is a factor of both. The correct factorisation is $x^3-1=(x-1)(x^2+x+1)$ (the course's line "$\frac{x^3-1}{x-1}=(x^2+x+1)(x-1)$" has dropped the division; see Errata). For $x\ne1$:

$$\frac{x^3-1}{x-1}=\frac{(x-1)(x^2+x+1)}{x-1}=x^2+x+1.$$

The two functions agree for all $x\ne1$, so by the Replacement Theorem $\lim_{x\to1}\dfrac{x^3-1}{x-1}=1+1+1=\mathbf3$. The graph of $f$ is the parabola $x^2+x+1$ with a hole at $(1,3)$. Checkpoint: $x^3-8=(x-2)(x^2+2x+4)$, so $\lim_{x\to2}\dfrac{x^3-8}{x-2}=4+4+4=\mathbf{12}$. (sympy: 3 and 12.)

Example 4 — "Dividing out" (Course Example 5, p.45, corrected; Checkpoint p.46)

Find $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to-3}\frac{x^2+x-6}{x+3}$.

Solution. The course prints the numerator as $x^2+x+\mathbf{6}$ in the question and in the check, but then factors $x^2+x-6$. With $+6$ the numerator at $-3$ is $9-3+6=12\ne0$, there is no cancellation, and the limit does not exist (left $-\infty$, right $+\infty$). The exercise only makes sense with $-6$, as in the course's later lines and graph (Fig 2.2). Check: $(-3)^2+(-3)-6=0$ and $-3+3=0$ (note: write $(-3)^2$; "$-3^2$" means $-9$). So $(x+3)$ divides the numerator: $x^2+x-6=(x-2)(x+3)$. For $x\ne-3$ (the course writes "$x\ne3$": misprint),

$$\frac{(x-2)(x+3)}{x+3}=x-2\ \Longrightarrow\ \lim_{x\to-3}=-3-2=\mathbf{-5}.$$

The graph is the line $y=x-2$ with a hole at $(-3,-5)$. Checkpoint (p.46): $\dfrac{x^2+x-12}{x-3}=\dfrac{(x-3)(x+4)}{x-3}=x+4\to\mathbf7$. (sympy: $-5$ and $7$.)

Example 5 — Rationalising the numerator (Course Example 6, p.46–47; Checkpoint p.47)

Find $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sqrt{x+1}-1}{x}$ and $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sqrt{x+4}-2}{x}$.

Solution. At $x=0$ both give $\frac00$, and factoring does not help. Multiply by the conjugate over itself:

$$\frac{\sqrt{x+1}-1}{x}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{x+1}+1}{\sqrt{x+1}+1}=\frac{(x+1)-1}{x(\sqrt{x+1}+1)}=\frac{x}{x(\sqrt{x+1}+1)}=\frac1{\sqrt{x+1}+1}\quad(x\ne0).$$

The right side is continuous at $0$ with value $\frac1{1+1}$, so the limit is $\mathbf{\tfrac12}$. Checkpoint: $\dfrac{\sqrt{x+4}-2}{x}\cdot\dfrac{\sqrt{x+4}+2}{\sqrt{x+4}+2}=\dfrac{x}{x(\sqrt{x+4}+2)}=\dfrac1{\sqrt{x+4}+2}\to\mathbf{\tfrac14}$. (sympy: $\tfrac12$, $\tfrac14$.)

Example 6 — One-sided limits and unbounded behaviour (Course Examples 7 and 8, pp.47–48; Checkpoint p.48)

(a) $f(x)=\dfrac{|2x|}{x}$ as $x\to0$. For $x>0$, $|2x|=2x$ so $f=2$; for $x<0$, $|2x|=-2x$ so $f=-2$. Hence $\lim_{x\to0^-}f=-2$ and $\lim_{x\to0^+}f=2$. They differ, so $\lim_{x\to0}f$ does not exist (the course states both one-sided values correctly). (b) $\dfrac3{x-2}$ as $x\to2$. Numerator $\to3$, denominator $\to0$: unbounded. Sign: for $x<2$ the denominator is negative, so $\lim_{x\to2^-}\frac3{x-2}=\mathbf{-\infty}$; for $x>2$, $\lim_{x\to2^+}=+\infty$. (The course writes $+\infty$ for both: wrong on the left; see Errata.) Because $f$ is unbounded, the limit does not exist. (c) Checkpoint $\dfrac5{x+2}$ as $x\to-2$. Left: $-\infty$; right: $+\infty$. The limit does not exist. The course's "$\frac5{-2+2}=\frac50=\infty$" is not a valid computation: $\frac50$ is not a number, and the answer is not $+\infty$ from both sides. (sympy confirms all one-sided values.)

Example 7 — Course Exercises 2.7, A and B (pp.48–49), answer key

A. (The printed layout puts "$\lim g=9$" under I and "$\lim g=\frac12$" under II; that is the only sensible reading.) Use the sum, product and quotient laws:

$\lim f$ $\lim g$ $\lim(f+g)$ $\lim fg$ $\lim\frac fg$
I $3$ $9$ $12$ $27$ $\tfrac13$
II $\tfrac32$ $\tfrac12$ $2$ $\tfrac34$ $3$

B. III–XVI are all continuous at the point, so substitute:

No. Limit Value
III $\lim_{x\to2}x^4$ $16$
IV $\lim_{x\to-2}x^3$ $-8$
V $\lim_{x\to-3}(3x+2)$ $-7$
VI $\lim_{x\to1}(1-x^2)$ $0$
VII $\lim_{x\to2}(-x^2+x-2)$ $-4$
IX $\lim_{x\to3}\sqrt{x+1}$ $2$
X $\lim_{x\to4}\sqrt[3]{x+4}$ $2$
XI $\lim_{x\to-3}\frac2{x+2}$ $-2$
XII $\lim_{x\to-2}\frac{3x-1}{2-x}$ $-\tfrac74$
XIII $\lim_{x\to-1}\frac{4x-5}{3-x}$ $-\tfrac94$
XIV $\lim_{x\to7}\frac{5x}{x+2}$ $\tfrac{35}9$
XV $\lim_{x\to3}\frac{\sqrt{x+1}}{x-4}$ (reading $\sqrt{x+1}$) $\frac2{-1}=-2$
XVI $\lim_{x\to-2}\frac{x^2-1}{2x}$ $\frac{3}{-4}=-\tfrac34$

(The printed list jumps from VII to IX: there is no VIII in the PDF. The denominators are non-zero in every row, so the quotient law applies. For XV, if the numerator is meant to be $\sqrt x+1$ the value is $-(\sqrt3+1)$; the scan is ambiguous. sympy verified all rows.)

Example 8 — Course Exercises XVII–XXX (p.49), answer key

Say "does not exist" only after you have checked the one-sided limits.

No. Limit Working Answer
XVII $\lim_{x\to-1}\frac{x^2-1}{x+1}$ $=x-1$ for $x\ne-1$ $-2$
XVIII $\lim_{x\to-1}\frac{2x^2-x-3}{x+1}$ $2x^2-x-3=(2x-3)(x+1)$, so $2x-3$ $-5$
XIX $\lim_{x\to2}\frac{x-2}{x^2-4x+4}$ $=\frac{x-2}{(x-2)^2}=\frac1{x-2}$; left $-\infty$, right $+\infty$ does not exist
XX $\lim_{x\to2}\frac{2-x}{x^2-4}$ $=\frac{-(x-2)}{(x-2)(x+2)}=-\frac1{x+2}$ $-\tfrac14$
XXI $\lim_{t\to5}\frac{t-5}{t^2-25}$ $=\frac1{t+5}$ $\tfrac1{10}$
XXII $\lim_{t\to1}\frac{t^2+t-2}{t^2-1}$ $=\frac{(t+2)(t-1)}{(t-1)(t+1)}=\frac{t+2}{t+1}$ $\tfrac32$
XXIII $\lim_{x\to-2}\frac{x^3-1}{x-1}$ denominator $\to-3\ne0$: substitute, $\frac{-9}{-3}$ $3$
XXIV $\lim_{x\to2}\frac{x^3+8}{x+2}$ denominator $\to4\ne0$: $\frac{16}4$ $4$
XXV $\lim_{x\to0}\frac{|x|}x$ left $-1$, right $+1$ does not exist
XXVI $\lim_{x\to2}\frac{|x-2|}{x-2}$ left $-1$, right $+1$ does not exist
XXVII $\lim_{x\to3}f$, $f=\frac13x\ (x\le3)$, $-2x+5\ (x>3)$ left $\frac33=1$; right $-6+5=-1$ does not exist
XXVIII $\lim_{\Delta x\to0}\frac{2(x+\Delta x)-2x}{\Delta x}$ $=\frac{2\Delta x}{\Delta x}=2$ $2$
XXIX $\lim_{\Delta x\to0}\frac{\sqrt{x+2+\Delta x}-\sqrt{x+2}}{\Delta x}$ conjugate: $\frac{\Delta x}{\Delta x(\sqrt{x+2+\Delta x}+\sqrt{x+2})}$ $\dfrac1{2\sqrt{x+2}}$
XXX $\lim_{\Delta t\to0}\frac{(t+\Delta t)^2-5(t+\Delta t)-(t^2-5t)}{\Delta t}$ numerator $=2t\Delta t+\Delta t^2-5\Delta t$ $2t-5$

Notes: XXIII and XXIV, as printed, are not of the form $\tfrac00$ (the points $x\to-2$ for XXIII and $x\to2$ for XXIV give a non-zero denominator). They were very probably meant to be $x\to1$ (answer $3$) and $x\to-2$ (answer $12$); I give the answers to the printed questions. In XXX the "$\to0$" under the limit is missing; $\Delta t\to0$ is the only sensible reading. In XXVII the stray "$f(x)=f(x)=f(x)$" is a copy error. (sympy verified every row, including the alternative readings.)

Example 9 — Exam-style: harder limits and parameters

(a) $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sqrt{1+x}-\sqrt{1-x}}{x}$. Conjugate: $\dfrac{(1+x)-(1-x)}{x(\sqrt{1+x}+\sqrt{1-x})}=\dfrac{2}{\sqrt{1+x}+\sqrt{1-x}}\to\dfrac22=\mathbf1$. (b) $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to4}\frac{\sqrt x-2}{x-4}$. Write $x-4=(\sqrt x-2)(\sqrt x+2)$: $\dfrac1{\sqrt x+2}\to\mathbf{\tfrac14}$. (c) $\displaystyle\lim_{h\to0}\frac{\frac1{2+h}-\frac12}h$. Common denominator: $\dfrac{2-(2+h)}{2(2+h)h}=\dfrac{-1}{2(2+h)}\to\mathbf{-\tfrac14}$. (d) Find $b$ in terms of $a$ so that $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to1}\frac{x^2+ax+b}{x-1}$ exists, and the limit. The denominator $\to0$, so the numerator must $\to0$ (otherwise the quotient is unbounded): $1+a+b=0$, $b=-1-a$. Then $x^2+ax-1-a=(x-1)(x+1+a)$, so the limit is $1+1+a=\mathbf{a+2}$. (e) Find $k$ so that $\lim_{x\to1}f(x)$ exists for $f=kx+2\ (x<1)$, $f=5-kx\ (x>1)$. Left limit $k+2$, right limit $5-k$. Equal iff $k+2=5-k$, i.e. $k=\tfrac32$; then the limit is $\tfrac72$. (sympy: all five.)

Common mistakes

  • Writing $\tfrac00=0$ (or $=1$). $\tfrac00$ is undefined, a signal to simplify first, not an answer (course Ex 2b, p.42).
  • Writing $\tfrac a0=\infty$ for $a\ne0$ without checking the sides. The two one-sided limits are usually $+\infty$ and $-\infty$ with opposite signs, so the limit does not exist (course Ex 8 and Checkpoint, p.48).
  • Saying "$\lim=\infty$ means the limit exists." It describes unbounded behaviour; the limit still does not exist (p.48 says this itself).
  • Using the quotient law when the denominator limit is $0$.
  • Believing $\lim f(x)=f(c)$ always. Only true when $f$ is continuous at $c$ (Ex 2c: the limit is $1$ but $f(1)=0$).
  • Cancelling a factor that does not cancel, e.g. cancelling $x$ in $\frac{x+1}{x}$. Only common factors cancel, and only for $x\ne c$.
  • Forgetting the conjugate must multiply top and bottom, and that the new denominator is $\sqrt{\ }+\sqrt{\ }$, never zero near the limit.
  • Sign slips when factoring $x^2+x-6$ (the course prints $+6$ in Ex 5) and $-3^2$ versus $(-3)^2$.
  • Concluding "limit does not exist" from one side only. Compute both one-sided limits.
  • Treating a hole as a vertical asymptote. $\frac{x^2-1}{x-1}$ at $1$ has a limit ($2$); $\frac{1}{x-1}$ does not.

Practice problems

(The course's own exercises are answered in Worked Examples 1–8: Checkpoints on pp.43, 45, 46, 47, 48 and Exercises I–XXX on pp.48–49. Problems 1–3 below repeat the checkpoints so you can test yourself first.)

  1. Course checkpoints, p.43: (a) $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to2}\frac{x^2-4}{x-2}$, (b) $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to2}\frac{|x-2|}{x-2}$, (c) $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to2}2x^2-x+4$.
  2. Course checkpoints, pp.45–46: (a) $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to2}\frac{x^3-8}{x-2}$, (b) $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to3}\frac{x^2+x-12}{x-3}$.
  3. Course checkpoints, pp.47–48: (a) $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sqrt{x+4}-2}{x}$, (b) $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to-2}\frac5{x+2}$.
  4. Evaluate $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to3}\frac{x^2-9}{x^2-5x+6}$.
  5. Evaluate $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to3}\frac{x^3-27}{x-3}$.
  6. Evaluate $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to1}\frac{x-1}{\sqrt x-1}$.
  7. Find $\lim_{x\to-3^-}$, $\lim_{x\to-3^+}$ and $\lim_{x\to-3}$ of $\dfrac{|x+3|}{x+3}$.
  8. Evaluate $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sqrt{x+9}-3}{x}$.
  9. Evaluate $\displaystyle\lim_{h\to0}\frac{\frac1{x+h}-\frac1x}{h}$ for $x\ne0$.
  10. Evaluate $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to1}\frac1{(x-1)^2}$ and say whether the limit exists.
  11. Find $a$ and $b$ so that $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to2}\frac{x^2+ax+b}{x-2}=5$.
  12. Let $f(x)=\dfrac{x^2-5x+6}{x^2-4}$. Find $\lim_{x\to2}f(x)$ and $\lim_{x\to-2}f(x)$ (give one-sided information if the limit does not exist).
Show answers

Answers

  1. (a) $4$ ($=x+2$); (b) does not exist (left $-1$, right $+1$); (c) $8-2+4=10$.
  2. (a) $12$; (b) $7$.
  3. (a) $\tfrac14$ (conjugate); (b) does not exist (left $-\infty$, right $+\infty$).
  4. $\dfrac{(x-3)(x+3)}{(x-3)(x-2)}=\dfrac{x+3}{x-2}\to\dfrac61=\mathbf6$.
  5. $x^3-27=(x-3)(x^2+3x+9)$, so $9+9+9=\mathbf{27}$.
  6. $x-1=(\sqrt x-1)(\sqrt x+1)$, so the quotient is $\sqrt x+1\to\mathbf2$.
  7. Left $-1$, right $+1$; the two-sided limit does not exist.
  8. $\dfrac{(x+9)-9}{x(\sqrt{x+9}+3)}=\dfrac1{\sqrt{x+9}+3}\to\dfrac16$.
  9. $\dfrac{x-(x+h)}{x(x+h)h}=\dfrac{-1}{x(x+h)}\to-\dfrac1{x^2}$.
  10. $\frac1{(x-1)^2}>0$ on both sides and grows without bound, so it tends to $+\infty$ from both sides. The limit does not exist (as a finite number); one may write "$=+\infty$" to describe the behaviour.
  11. The numerator must vanish at $x=2$: $4+2a+b=0$, so $b=-4-2a$. Then $x^2+ax-4-2a=(x-2)(x+2+a)$ and the limit is $4+a$. Setting $4+a=5$ gives $a=1$, $b=-6$. Check: $x^2+x-6=(x-2)(x+3)$, limit $5$.
  12. $f=\dfrac{(x-2)(x-3)}{(x-2)(x+2)}=\dfrac{x-3}{x+2}$ for $x\ne\pm2$. At $2$: $\frac{-1}4=\mathbf{-\tfrac14}$. At $-2$: numerator $\to-5$, denominator $\to0$: left side $+\infty$, right side $-\infty$, does not exist.

All numerical and symbolic results in the examples and problems were checked with sympy 1.14 (script at /tmp/mth102_scratch/unit2/verify2.py).

Extra practice (from outside sources)

From outside sources — not in the NOUN course text Copied from OpenStax Calculus Volume 1 (CC BY 4.0); each problem names its section and exercise number.

Not from the NOUN course text. These problems come from the free OpenStax Calculus textbooks (CC BY 4.0). Tap Source ↗ to open the original page. Answers are worked out and checked here.

  1. Use direct substitution to show that the limit leads to the indeterminate form $0/0$. Then, evaluate the limit: $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to 1/2}\frac{2x^2+3x-2}{2x-1}$. Source ↗
  2. Use direct substitution to show that the limit leads to the indeterminate form $0/0$. Then, evaluate the limit: $\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 9}\frac{t-9}{\sqrt t-3}$. Source ↗
  3. Use direct substitution to show that the limit leads to the indeterminate form $0/0$. Then, evaluate the limit: $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to -3}\frac{\sqrt{x+4}-1}{x+3}$. Source ↗
  4. Use direct substitution to show that the limit leads to the indeterminate form $0/0$. Then, evaluate the limit: $\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{\frac1{a+h}-\frac1a}{h}$, where $a$ is a non-zero real-valued constant. Source ↗
  5. Use direct substitution to obtain an undefined expression. Then simplify the function to help determine the limit. (a) $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to -2^-}\frac{2x^2+7x-4}{x^2+x-2}$ (Exercise 103); (b) $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to 1^-}\frac{2x^2+7x-4}{x^2+x-2}$ (Exercise 105). Source ↗
  6. Evaluate the limit: $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to-\infty}\frac{x^4-4x^3+1}{2-2x^2-7x^4}$. Source ↗
  7. Evaluate the limit: $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^2-2x+5}{x+2}$. Source ↗
  8. Evaluate the limit: $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to-\infty}\frac{\sqrt{4x^2-1}}{x+2}$. Source ↗
Show answers (extra practice)

Answers (extra practice)

  1. At $x=\frac12$: $2\cdot\frac14+\frac32-2=0$ and $2x-1=0$, so the form is $\frac00$. Factor the numerator: $2x^2+3x-2=(2x-1)(x+2)$. For $x\ne\frac12$ the quotient equals $x+2$, so the limit is $\frac12+2=\mathbf{\tfrac52}$. (OpenStax key: $\frac52$ ✓.)
  2. At $t=9$ the form is $\frac{9-9}{3-3}=\frac00$. Multiply top and bottom by the conjugate $\sqrt t+3$: $\dfrac{(t-9)(\sqrt t+3)}{(\sqrt t-3)(\sqrt t+3)}=\dfrac{(t-9)(\sqrt t+3)}{t-9}=\sqrt t+3$ for $t\ne9$. The limit is $3+3=\mathbf6$. (OpenStax key: $6$ ✓.)
  3. At $x=-3$: $\sqrt{1}-1=0$ and $x+3=0$, so the form is $\frac00$. Multiply by the conjugate $\sqrt{x+4}+1$: $\dfrac{(x+4)-1}{(x+3)(\sqrt{x+4}+1)}=\dfrac{x+3}{(x+3)(\sqrt{x+4}+1)}=\dfrac1{\sqrt{x+4}+1}$. The limit is $\dfrac1{1+1}=\mathbf{\tfrac12}$. (Even-numbered, so no printed key; checked with sympy.)
  4. As $h\to0$ both numerator and denominator tend to $0$. Combine the fractions: $\dfrac1{a+h}-\dfrac1a=\dfrac{a-(a+h)}{a(a+h)}=\dfrac{-h}{a(a+h)}$. Dividing by $h$ gives $\dfrac{-1}{a(a+h)}$ for $h\ne0$. The limit is $\mathbf{-\dfrac1{a^2}}$. (This is the difference quotient of $\frac1x$; compare Practice problem 9 above.)
  5. Factor: $f(x)=\dfrac{2x^2+7x-4}{x^2+x-2}=\dfrac{(2x-1)(x+4)}{(x+2)(x-1)}$. (a) At $x=-2$ the numerator is $(-5)(2)=-10$ and the denominator is $0$, so the limit is of the form $\frac{-10}{0}$. Write $f(x)=\dfrac1{x+2}\cdot\dfrac{(2x-1)(x+4)}{x-1}$; the second factor tends to $\dfrac{(-5)(2)}{-3}=\dfrac{10}3>0$. As $x\to-2^-$, $x+2\to0^-$ (small and negative), so $f(x)\to\mathbf{-\infty}$. (OpenStax key: $-\infty$ ✓.) (b) At $x=1$ the numerator is $(1)(5)=5$ and the denominator is $0$. Write $f(x)=\dfrac1{x-1}\cdot\dfrac{(2x-1)(x+4)}{x+2}$; the second factor tends to $\frac53>0$. As $x\to1^-$, $x-1\to0^-$, so $f(x)\to\mathbf{-\infty}$. (OpenStax key: $-\infty$ ✓.) From the right the signs flip (both limits are $+\infty$, Exercises 104 and 106), so the two-sided limits at $-2$ and at $1$ do not exist.
  6. Divide numerator and denominator by $x^4$ (the highest power): $\dfrac{1-\frac4x+\frac1{x^4}}{\frac2{x^4}-\frac2{x^2}-7}\to\dfrac{1}{-7}=\mathbf{-\tfrac17}$. (OpenStax key: $-\frac17$ ✓.)
  7. Degree of numerator ($2$) exceeds degree of denominator ($1$), so the limit is not a finite number. Divide by $x^2$: $\dfrac{1-\frac2x+\frac5{x^2}}{\frac1x+\frac2{x^2}}$; the top tends to $1$, the bottom tends to $0$ through positive values, so the quotient grows without bound: $\mathbf{+\infty}$. (Long division confirms: $\frac{x^2-2x+5}{x+2}=x-4+\frac{13}{x+2}$.) (OpenStax key: $\infty$ ✓.) Remember the course's wording: this means the limit does not exist as a number; "$\infty$" only describes how it fails.
  8. Here $x\to-\infty$, so $x<0$ and $\sqrt{4x^2-1}=\sqrt{x^2\left(4-\frac1{x^2}\right)}=|x|\sqrt{4-\frac1{x^2}}=-x\sqrt{4-\frac1{x^2}}$. Then $\dfrac{-x\sqrt{4-1/x^2}}{x+2}=\dfrac{-\sqrt{4-1/x^2}}{1+2/x}\to\dfrac{-2}{1}=\mathbf{-2}$. The minus sign is the trap: as $x\to+\infty$ the same expression tends to $+2$. (Agrees with the OpenStax answer key, which gives $-2$; also checked with sympy.)

All eight results were checked with sympy (script at /tmp/mth102_scratch/extra/verify_extra23.py); the odd-numbered ones also agree with the OpenStax answer key (https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/chapter-2 for §2.3, https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/chapter-4 for §4.6).
