Unit 3 of 8

Unit 3 — Idea of Continuity

Page convention. The course PDF has no printed page numbers on these pages. "p.N" always means the PDF page N (Unit 3 is PDF pp.60–66). The section numbers are the course's own and are inconsistent (see Errata): the contents list on p.60 says 3.5 Exercises and 3.6 References, but the body has 3.5 Continuity on a Closed Interval, 3.6 Exercises, 3.7 Summary and 3.8 References.

Likely to be examined. The unit has Objectives (p.60: definition of continuity, continuity of polynomial and rational functions, continuity on a closed interval), the Class exercise (p.63) and the 3.6 Exercises i–xvi (pp.65–66). It has no tutor-marked assignment labelled as such. The Course Guide (pp.10–12) says assessment is tutor-marked assignments (best three of four count 30%) plus a two-hour final exam worth 70% in which you answer three questions out of at least five. The PDF does not say which topics the exam covers, so treat everything below as "likely", not "known". This unit is short; it builds directly on Unit 2 (limits), so redo Unit 2's one-sided limits first.

What you must be able to do

  • State the three conditions for continuity at a point and test them.
  • Say where a polynomial or rational function is continuous (everywhere / everywhere in its domain), and give the intervals of continuity.
  • Find and classify discontinuities as removable or non-removable, and remove a removable one by (re)defining $f(c)$.
  • Check continuity on a closed interval $[a,b]$ using one-sided limits at the end points, and for radical functions find the domain from $g(x)\ge0$.
  • Check continuity of a piecewise function at the break point, and choose constants so that it is continuous.
  • Use continuity of compositions (Exercise xvi) and recognise the greatest-integer function as discontinuous at every integer.

Key ideas

1. The idea (p.60)

Intuition. A function is continuous at $c$ if its graph has no break, hole or jump there: you can draw it through $x=c$ without lifting the pencil (p.61). In other words, if $x$ values are close to $c$, then $f(x)$ is close to $f(c)$.

Definition (p.60). Let $f$ be defined on an open interval containing $c$. $f$ is continuous at $c$ if 1. $f(c)$ is defined, 2. $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)$ exists (a finite number), 3. $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)=f(c)$.

All three must hold; failing any one gives a discontinuity. $f$ is continuous on an open interval $(a,b)$ if it is continuous at every point of it. "Continuous" with no qualifier (Summary, p.66) means continuous at every point of its domain.

2. Polynomials and rational functions (p.61)

  • A polynomial is continuous at every real number (direct substitution always works: $\lim_{x\to c}p(x)=p(c)$, Unit 2).
  • A rational function $\dfrac{p(x)}{q(x)}$ is continuous at every number in its domain, i.e. wherever $q(x)\ne0$. At a zero of $q$ the function is not defined, so it is discontinuous there (and the intervals of continuity are the open intervals between the zeros).

The course (p.61) says the two types of functions for which direct substitution works are polynomials and rational functions. That is too narrow: roots, absolute values, sine, cosine, exponentials and compositions of continuous functions are also continuous on their domains. Standard facts used below (not proved in this unit): sums, products, quotients (denominator $\ne0$), and compositions of continuous functions are continuous.

3. Discontinuities (p.63)

Let $I$ be an open interval containing $c$ and $f$ defined on $I$ except possibly at $c$. If $f$ is not continuous at $c$, it has a discontinuity at $c$. - Removable: $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)$ exists (a finite number) but $f(c)$ is undefined or $\ne$ the limit. Fix: define $f(c)$ to be the limit. Course: Example 2b, $\frac{x^2-1}{x-1}$ at $x=1$ (redefine $f(1)=2$). - Non-removable: no redefinition of the single value $f(c)$ can make it continuous. Two usual kinds: jump (one-sided limits exist but differ, e.g. $\frac{|x+1|}{x+1}$ at $-1$) and infinite (a one-sided limit is $\pm\infty$, e.g. $\frac1x$ at $0$, course Example 2a).

4. Continuity on a closed interval (pp.63–64)

Let $f$ be defined on $[a,b]$. $f$ is continuous on $[a,b]$ if

$$f\text{ is continuous on }(a,b),\qquad\lim_{x\to a^+}f(x)=f(a),\qquad\lim_{x\to b^-}f(x)=f(b).$$

The last two say "continuous from the right at $a$" and "continuous from the left at $b$". Same idea for $(a,b]$, $[a,b)$ and infinite intervals such as $[0,\infty)$ for $\sqrt x$ (p.64).

Radical working tip (p.64). For $f(x)=\sqrt{g(x)}$ the domain is the solution of $g(x)\ge0$. When $g$ is continuous (e.g. a polynomial), $f$ is continuous at every point of its domain; at an end point of the domain you only have one side to check (Example 3).

5. Piecewise functions

At the point where the formula changes, compute the left and right limit and compare with the value $f(c)$ (Example 4, p.64–65). Continuous iff left limit = right limit $=f(c)$.

Formula sheet

Continuity at $c$: $f(c)$ exists, $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)$ exists, and $\lim_{x\to c}f(x)=f(c)$. Equivalent: $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to c^-}f(x)=\lim_{x\to c^+}f(x)=f(c)$. On $[a,b]$: continuous on $(a,b)$, $\lim_{x\to a^+}f=f(a)$, $\lim_{x\to b^-}f=f(b)$. Polynomial: continuous on $(-\infty,\infty)$. Rational $p/q$: continuous wherever $q\ne0$. Radical: $\sqrt{g(x)}$ has domain $\{g(x)\ge0\}$. Types: removable (limit exists $\ne f(c)$ or $f(c)$ undefined); non-removable: jump (finite, one-sided limits differ) or infinite ($\pm\infty$). Removing a removable discontinuity: set $f(c):=\lim_{x\to c}f(x)$. Factorisations: $x^2-a^2=(x-a)(x+a)$, $x^3-a^3=(x-a)(x^2+ax+a^2)$. Greatest-integer function $\lfloor x\rfloor$ (the course writes $[[x]]$): continuous on every $(n,n+1)$ ($n\in\mathbb Z$); at each integer the left limit is $n-1$, the right limit is $n$.

Worked examples

Example 1 — Polynomials (Course Example 1, pp.61–62)

Discuss the continuity of (a) $f(x)=x^2-2x+3$, (b) $f(x)=x^3-x$.

Solution. Both are polynomials, so both are continuous at every real number, i.e. on $(-\infty,\infty)$. Justification: for any $c$, $\lim_{x\to c}p(x)=p(c)$ by Unit 2's rule, which is exactly condition 3 (conditions 1 and 2 are built in: $p(c)$ is defined and the limit exists). Graph check. The graph the course draws on p.62 is labelled "$x^2-2x$" but it passes through $(0,3)$ and has its lowest point $(1,2)$: that is the graph of $x^2-2x+3$ (indeed $x^2-2x+3=(x-1)^2+2$; $x^2-2x$ would have minimum $-1$ at $(1,-1)$). See Errata. (sympy: min of $x^2-2x+3$ is $2$ at $x=1$; min of $x^2-2x$ is $-1$.)

Example 2 — Rational functions (Course Example 2, pp.62–63; Class exercise p.63)

Discuss the continuity of (a) $\dfrac1x$, (b) $\dfrac{x^2-1}{x-1}$, (c) $\dfrac1{x^2+1}$.

Solution. Each is a rational function, so it is continuous at every number in its domain. (a) Domain: $x\ne0$. Continuous on $(-\infty,0)$ and $(0,\infty)$; discontinuous at $0$ (not defined there). (b) Domain: $x\ne1$. Continuous on $(-\infty,1)$ and $(1,\infty)$. (For $x\ne1$ it equals $x+1$.) (c) $x^2+1>0$ always, so the domain is all real numbers: continuous on $(-\infty,\infty)$.

Class exercise (p.63). (a) $\frac1{x-1}$: continuous on $(-\infty,1)$ and $(1,\infty)$. (b) $\frac{x^2-4}{x-2}$: continuous on $(-\infty,2)$ and $(2,\infty)$ (the course prints the second interval as "$(2,-\infty)$": misprint). (c) $\frac1{x^2+2}$: continuous on $(-\infty,\infty)$. (sympy: continuous domains agree.)

Example 3 — Removable and non-removable (Course p.63, redone)

(a) $f(x)=\dfrac{x^2-1}{x-1}$ at $x=1$. $f(1)$ is undefined, so condition 1 fails. But $\lim_{x\to1}\frac{(x-1)(x+1)}{x-1}=\lim(x+1)=2$ exists. Removable. Define $f(1)=2$: then $f(x)=x+1$ everywhere and is continuous. (b) $f(x)=\dfrac1x$ at $x=0$. $\lim_{x\to0^-}\frac1x=-\infty$, $\lim_{x\to0^+}\frac1x=+\infty$. The limit does not exist, so no choice of $f(0)$ helps: non-removable (infinite). (c) A jump. $f(x)=\begin{cases}x+1,&x<0\\3-x,&x\ge0.\end{cases}$ At $0$: left limit $1$, right limit $3$, $f(0)=3$. The one-sided limits differ, so $\lim_{x\to0}f$ does not exist: non-removable (jump of size $2$). $f$ is continuous from the right at $0$ but not from the left. (d) Both kinds in one function. $f(x)=\dfrac{x^2-1}{x^2-3x+2}=\dfrac{(x-1)(x+1)}{(x-1)(x-2)}$. Domain: $x\ne1,2$. At $x=1$ the factor cancels: limit $\frac{1+1}{1-2}=-2$, removable (define $f(1)=-2$). At $x=2$ the denominator $\to0$ but the numerator $\to3$: infinite, non-removable. (sympy: limit at $1$ is $-2$.)

Example 4 — A radical on an unbounded interval (Course Example 3, p.64)

Discuss the continuity of $f(x)=\sqrt{3-x}$.

Solution. Domain: $3-x\ge0\iff x\le3$, so $(-\infty,3]$. For every $x<3$ the three conditions hold ($\sqrt{3-x}$ is a composition of continuous functions). At the right end point $x=3$ only the left limit makes sense: $\lim_{x\to3^-}\sqrt{3-x}=0=f(3)$, so $f$ is continuous from the left at $3$. Hence $f$ is continuous on $(-\infty,3]$. Working tip variants (sympy-checked): $\sqrt{x^2-4}$ is continuous on $(-\infty,-2]\cup[2,\infty)$; $\dfrac1{\sqrt{4-x^2}}$ needs $4-x^2>0$ (the square root is in the denominator), so it is continuous on $(-2,2)$; $\dfrac{\sqrt{x-2}}{x-5}$ is continuous on $[2,5)\cup(5,\infty)$ (domain $x\ge2$, minus the zero of the denominator).

Example 5 — A piecewise function on $[-1,3]$ (Course Example 4, pp.64–65)

Discuss the continuity of $g(x)=\begin{cases}5-x,&-1\le x\le2\\x^2-1,&2<x\le3.\end{cases}$

Solution. $5-x$ is a polynomial, so $g$ is continuous on $[-1,2)$; $x^2-1$ is a polynomial, so $g$ is continuous on $(2,3]$. Only $x=2$ needs checking: - $g(2)=5-2=3$ (the first formula covers $x=2$). - Left limit: $\lim_{x\to2^-}(5-x)=3$. - Right limit: $\lim_{x\to2^+}(x^2-1)=4-1=3$. (The course prints the right-hand sign as "$2^{\mp}$": misprint for $2^+$.)

Left limit = right limit = $g(2)=3$, so $g$ is continuous at $2$, and therefore continuous on $[-1,3]$ (right-continuous at $-1$, left-continuous at $3$ automatically since each formula is a polynomial). (sympy: both limits $3$.)

Example 6 — Course Exercises 3.6 A, i–ii (p.65), answer key

(The course says "In exercise i to iii"; only i and ii are in part A. See Errata.) - i. $f(x)=5x^3-x^2+2$. A polynomial: continuous on the whole real line. Yes. - ii. $f(x)=\dfrac1{x^2-4}$. Denominator $(x-2)(x+2)=0$ at $x=\pm2$, where $f$ is not defined. No: not continuous on the whole real line. It is continuous on $(-\infty,-2)$, $(-2,2)$, $(2,\infty)$ (infinite discontinuities at $\pm2$: numerator is $1$, denominator $\to0$).

The two graphs printed on p.65 under i and ii do not belong to them: the left one (zeros at $\pm1$, asymptote at $x=0$) is the graph of $\frac{x^2-1}x=x-\frac1x$ (exercise iv), the right one (the line $y=x-1$) is $\frac{x^2-1}{x+1}$ with a hole at $(-1,-2)$ (exercise v). Inferred from the shapes.

Example 7 — Course Exercises 3.6 B, iii–xvi (pp.65–66), answer key

(Exercises xiv and xv are missing from the PDF: the list goes xiii, then xvi.)

No. Function Where continuous Notes
iii $\dfrac1{9-x^2}$ $(-\infty,-3)\cup(-3,3)\cup(3,\infty)$ $9-x^2=0$ at $\pm3$; infinite discontinuities
iv $\dfrac{x^2-1}x$ $(-\infty,0)\cup(0,\infty)$ $x=0$ infinite
v $\dfrac{x^2-1}{x+1}$ $(-\infty,-1)\cup(-1,\infty)$ $x=-1$: removable (equals $x-1$, limit $-2$)
vi $x^2-2x+1$ $(-\infty,\infty)$ polynomial, $=(x-1)^2$
vii $\dfrac x{x-1}$ $(-\infty,1)\cup(1,\infty)$ $x=1$ infinite
viii $\dfrac x{x^2+1}$ $(-\infty,\infty)$ denominator $\ge1$
ix $\dfrac{x-5}{x^2-9x+20}$ $(-\infty,4)\cup(4,5)\cup(5,\infty)$ $x^2-9x+20=(x-4)(x-5)$; $x=5$ removable (limit $1$, function $\frac1{x-4}$); $x=4$ infinite
x $\begin{cases}-2x+3,&x<1\\x^2,&x>1\end{cases}$ $(-\infty,1)\cup(1,\infty)$ $f(1)$ is not defined; one-sided limits both $1$, so the discontinuity at $1$ is removable (define $f(1)=1$)
xi $\begin{cases}3+x,&x\le2\\x^2+1,&x>2\end{cases}$ $(-\infty,\infty)$ at $2$: left $5$, right $4+1=5$, $f(2)=5$
xii $\dfrac{\lvert x+1\rvert}{x+1}$ $(-\infty,-1)\cup(-1,\infty)$ $=1$ for $x>-1$, $=-1$ for $x<-1$: jump, non-removable
xiii $[[x-1]]$ (greatest integer $\le x-1$) each interval $[n,n+1)$, $n\in\mathbb Z$ discontinuous at every integer $n$: left limit $n-2$, right limit $n-1=f(n)$; continuous from the right, jump of $1$
xvi $h=f(g(x))$, $f=\frac1{\sqrt x}$, $g=x-1$, $x>1$ $(1,\infty)$ $h(x)=\frac1{\sqrt{x-1}}$, composition of continuous functions on $x-1>0$

(The statement for x: the formula leaves $x=1$ unassigned, so "discontinuous at $1$" is because $f(1)$ is undefined. Notation $[[\cdot]]$ is read as greatest-integer, the usual meaning in this notation; the course does not define it. sympy verified every row.)

Example 8 — Exam-style: constants and removing discontinuities

(a) $f(x)=\dfrac{x^2-9}{x-3}$ for $x\ne3$. Choose $f(3)$ so that $f$ is continuous at $3$. For $x\ne3$, $f(x)=x+3\to6$. By condition 3, $f(3)=\mathbf6$. (b) Find $a,b$ so that $f(x)=\begin{cases}-2,&x\le-1\\ax+b,&-1<x<1\\3,&x\ge1\end{cases}$ is continuous everywhere. Each piece is a polynomial; check the two break points. - At $-1$: left $-2$, right $-a+b$. Need $-a+b=-2$. - At $1$: left $a+b$, right $3$. Need $a+b=3$. Add: $2b=1$, $b=\tfrac12$; then $a=\tfrac52$. So $f=\tfrac52x+\tfrac12$ in the middle. (Check: $-\frac52+\frac12=-2$ ✓, $\frac52+\frac12=3$ ✓.) (c) Find $a$ so that $f(x)=\begin{cases}ax+1,&x\le2\\x^2-a,&x>2\end{cases}$ is continuous at $2$. Left $=f(2)=2a+1$; right $=4-a$. Equal iff $3a=3$, $a=\mathbf1$ (then both are $3$). (d) Where is $\dfrac{\sqrt{x-2}}{x-5}$ continuous? Numerator needs $x\ge2$; denominator is $0$ at $5$. So continuous on $[2,5)\cup(5,\infty)$ (at $2$ only from the right). (sympy: all four.)

Common mistakes

  • Checking only one or two of the three conditions. For a removable discontinuity (limit exists, $f(c)$ missing or different) the limit part is fine and the value part fails.
  • Saying a rational function is continuous "everywhere". It is continuous only where the denominator is non-zero. $\frac{x^2-1}{x-1}$ is not continuous at $1$ (it is not defined there), even though a hole can be filled.
  • Treating a hole as an asymptote. $\frac{x^2-1}{x-1}$ at $1$ is removable (limit $2$); $\frac1{x-1}$ is not.
  • Equating "discontinuous at $c$" with "$c$ in the domain". Course definition (p.63) says $f$ is defined on the interval except possibly at $c$: points outside the domain are discontinuities, but a function is called continuous (Summary, p.66) when it is continuous at every point of its domain, so $\frac1x$ is a "continuous function" whose domain misses $0$. Say which sense you mean.
  • Piecewise functions: forgetting $f(c)$. Left limit and right limit can agree and still differ from $f(c)$ (then removable).
  • Closed intervals: using two-sided limits at the end points. At $a$ only the right-hand limit exists, at $b$ only the left-hand limit (Example 4).
  • Radicals: solving $g(x)>0$ instead of $g(x)\ge0$, or forgetting that a root in the denominator needs $g(x)>0$.
  • Greatest-integer function: it is continuous between integers and right-continuous (not continuous) at each integer.
  • Wrong sign of a limit at an infinite discontinuity. Check each side separately (Unit 2).

Practice problems

(The course's own exercises are answered in Worked Examples 2, 6 and 7: Class exercise p.63, Exercises i–xvi pp.65–66. Do them first, then try the problems below.)

  1. Course Class exercise (p.63): discuss the continuity of (a) $\frac1{x-1}$, (b) $\frac{x^2-4}{x-2}$, (c) $\frac1{x^2+2}$.
  2. Find all points where $f(x)=\dfrac1{x^2-x-6}$ is discontinuous and give the intervals of continuity.
  3. Let $f(x)=\dfrac{x^2-16}{x-4}$ for $x\ne4$. Find $f(4)$ so that $f$ is continuous at $4$.
  4. Classify the discontinuities of $f(x)=\dfrac{x^2-1}{x^2-3x+2}$ and say how to remove the removable one.
  5. Show that $f(x)=\begin{cases}x+1,&x<0\\3-x,&x\ge0\end{cases}$ is not continuous at $0$. Is it continuous from the left or from the right there?
  6. Find $a,b$ so that $f(x)=\begin{cases}x^2,&x<1\\ax+b,&1\le x\le3\\6,&x>3\end{cases}$ is continuous on $(-\infty,\infty)$.
  7. Where is $\sqrt{x^2-4}$ continuous? Is it continuous at $x=2$ in the sense of the course's definition on $[2,\infty)$?
  8. Show that $f(x)=\sqrt{x+5}$ is continuous on $[-5,\infty)$.
  9. Find $a$ so that $f(x)=\begin{cases}ax+1,&x\le2\\x^2-a,&x>2\end{cases}$ is continuous at $2$.
  10. Using the greatest-integer function $\lfloor x\rfloor$: find the left limit, right limit and value at $x=2$, and state where $\lfloor x\rfloor$ is discontinuous.
  11. State where $\dfrac{|x|}x$ is continuous, and whether the discontinuity can be removed.
  12. Let $h(x)=\dfrac{\sqrt{x-2}}{x-5}$. Give the largest set on which $h$ is continuous.
Show answers

Answers

  1. (a) $(-\infty,1)$ and $(1,\infty)$; (b) $(-\infty,2)$ and $(2,\infty)$; (c) $(-\infty,\infty)$.
  2. $x^2-x-6=(x-3)(x+2)$: discontinuous at $x=-2$ and $x=3$; continuous on $(-\infty,-2)$, $(-2,3)$, $(3,\infty)$.
  3. For $x\ne4$, $f=x+4\to8$; so $f(4)=\mathbf8$.
  4. At $x=1$: removable (limit $-2$; set $f(1)=-2$). At $x=2$: infinite, non-removable.
  5. Left limit $1$, right limit $3$; $f(0)=3$. The limits differ, so $f$ is not continuous at $0$ (jump of $2$). It is continuous from the right (right limit $=f(0)$) and not from the left.
  6. $x<1$ piece tends to $1$ at $x=1$, so $a+b=1$; at $x=3$: $3a+b=6$. Subtract: $2a=5$, $a=\tfrac52$, $b=-\tfrac32$. Check: $\tfrac52-\tfrac32=1$ ✓; $\tfrac{15}2-\tfrac32=6$ ✓.
  7. Domain $x^2\ge4$: $(-\infty,-2]\cup[2,\infty)$. At $x=2$ only the right-hand side belongs to the domain; $\lim_{x\to2^+}\sqrt{x^2-4}=0=f(2)$, so it is continuous from the right at $2$ (and from the left at $-2$).
  8. For $x>-5$ it is a composition of continuous functions. At $-5$: $\lim_{x\to-5^+}\sqrt{x+5}=0=f(-5)$. So continuous on $[-5,\infty)$.
  9. $2a+1=4-a\Rightarrow a=1$.
  10. Left limit $1$, right limit $2$, $\lfloor2\rfloor=2$: continuous from the right, not from the left. Discontinuous at every integer.
  11. Domain $x\ne0$, so it is continuous on $(-\infty,0)$ and $(0,\infty)$. At $0$ the one-sided limits are $-1$ and $+1$ (a jump), so the discontinuity is not removable.
  12. $[2,5)\cup(5,\infty)$.

All results in the examples and problems were checked with sympy 1.14 (script at /tmp/mth102_scratch/unit3/verify3.py).

Extra practice (from outside sources)

From outside sources — not in the NOUN course text Copied from OpenStax Calculus Volume 1 (CC BY 4.0); each problem names its section and exercise number.

Not from the NOUN course text. These problems come from the free OpenStax Calculus textbooks (CC BY 4.0). Tap Source ↗ to open the original page. Answers are worked out and checked here.

  1. Determine the point(s), if any, at which the function is discontinuous. Classify any discontinuity as jump, removable, infinite, or other: $f(x)=\dfrac{x}{x^2-x}$. Source ↗
  2. Determine the point(s), if any, at which the function is discontinuous. Classify any discontinuity as jump, removable, infinite, or other: $f(t)=\dfrac{t+3}{t^2+5t+6}$. Source ↗
  3. Decide if the function is continuous at the given point. If it is discontinuous, what type of discontinuity is it? $f(x)=\dfrac{2x^2-5x+3}{x-1}$ at $x=1$. Source ↗
  4. Decide if the function is continuous at the given point. If it is discontinuous, what type of discontinuity is it? $g(u)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{6u^2+u-2}{2u-1}&\text{if }u\ne\frac12\\[2mm]\dfrac72&\text{if }u=\frac12\end{cases}$ at $u=\frac12$. Source ↗
  5. Decide if the function is continuous at the given point. If it is discontinuous, what type of discontinuity is it? $f(x)=\begin{cases}x^2-e^x&\text{if }x<0\\x-1&\text{if }x\ge0\end{cases}$ at $x=0$. Source ↗
  6. Find the value(s) of $k$ that makes the function continuous over the given interval: $f(x)=\begin{cases}3x+2,&x<k\\2x-3,&k\le x\le8\end{cases}$. Source ↗
  7. Find the value(s) of $k$ that makes the function continuous over the given interval: $f(x)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{x^2+3x+2}{x+2},&x\ne-2\\[2mm]k,&x=-2\end{cases}$. Source ↗
  8. Find the value(s) of $k$ that makes the function continuous over the given interval: $f(x)=\begin{cases}\sqrt{kx},&0\le x\le3\\x+1,&3<x\le10\end{cases}$. Source ↗
Show answers (extra practice)

Answers (extra practice)

  1. $x^2-x=x(x-1)$, so $f$ is undefined at $x=0$ and $x=1$ and continuous everywhere else (a rational function). For $x\ne0,1$: $f(x)=\dfrac{x}{x(x-1)}=\dfrac1{x-1}$. At $x=0$: $\lim_{x\to0}f(x)=\dfrac1{0-1}=-1$ exists but $f(0)$ is undefined: removable (define $f(0)=-1$). At $x=1$: $\dfrac1{x-1}\to-\infty$ from the left and $+\infty$ from the right: infinite discontinuity. (OpenStax key: removable at $x=0$, infinite at $x=1$ ✓.)
  2. $t^2+5t+6=(t+2)(t+3)$, so $f$ is undefined at $t=-2$ and $t=-3$. For $t\ne-2,-3$: $f(t)=\dfrac{t+3}{(t+2)(t+3)}=\dfrac1{t+2}$. At $t=-3$: the limit is $\dfrac1{-3+2}=-1$ and $f(-3)$ is undefined: removable (define $f(-3)=-1$). At $t=-2$: $\dfrac1{t+2}\to-\infty$ from the left, $+\infty$ from the right: infinite. (Even-numbered, so no printed key; checked with sympy.)
  3. $f(1)$ is undefined (denominator $0$), so condition 1 of continuity fails: not continuous at $x=1$. Factor $2x^2-5x+3=(2x-3)(x-1)$, so $f(x)=2x-3$ for $x\ne1$ and $\lim_{x\to1}f(x)=-1$ exists. The limit exists, so the discontinuity is removable; define $f(1)=-1$ to repair it. (OpenStax key: "No. It is a removable discontinuity." ✓)
  4. Check the three conditions. (i) $g\!\left(\frac12\right)=\frac72$ is defined. (ii) For $u\ne\frac12$: $6u^2+u-2=(2u-1)(3u+2)$, so $g(u)=3u+2$ and $\lim_{u\to1/2}g(u)=\frac32+2=\frac72$. (iii) The limit equals the value. So $g$ is continuous at $u=\frac12$. (OpenStax key: "Yes. It is continuous." ✓)
  5. (i) $f(0)=0-1=-1$. (ii) From the left: $\lim_{x\to0^-}(x^2-e^x)=0-1=-1$. From the right: $\lim_{x\to0^+}(x-1)=-1$. The two one-sided limits agree, so $\lim_{x\to0}f(x)=-1$. (iii) The limit equals $f(0)$. So $f$ is continuous at $0$. (OpenStax key: "Yes. It is continuous." ✓)
  6. Each piece is a polynomial, hence continuous on its own interval, so the only possible break is at $x=k$. Here $f(k)=2k-3$ and $\lim_{x\to k^+}f(x)=2k-3$, while $\lim_{x\to k^-}f(x)=3k+2$. Continuity requires $3k+2=2k-3$, so $\mathbf{k=-5}$. Check: $3(-5)+2=-13=2(-5)-3$ ✓; and $k=-5$ lies where the formulas apply. (OpenStax key: $k=-5$ ✓.)
  7. For $x\ne-2$: $x^2+3x+2=(x+1)(x+2)$, so $f(x)=x+1$ and $\lim_{x\to-2}f(x)=-1$. For continuity at $-2$ we need $f(-2)=k$ equal to this limit: $\mathbf{k=-1}$. (OpenStax key: $k=-1$ ✓.)
  8. On $[0,3]$ we need $\sqrt{kx}$ defined, so $k\ge0$ (then it is continuous there). The only break is at $x=3$: $f(3)=\sqrt{3k}=\lim_{x\to3^-}\sqrt{kx}$ and $\lim_{x\to3^+}(x+1)=4$. Set $\sqrt{3k}=4$: $3k=16$, $\mathbf{k=\tfrac{16}3}$ (which satisfies $k\ge0$). (OpenStax key: $k=\frac{16}3$ ✓.)

All eight results were checked with sympy (script at /tmp/mth102_scratch/extra/verify_extra23.py); the odd-numbered ones also agree with the OpenStax answer key (https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/chapter-2, "Section 2.4 Exercises").

Unit 4 of 8

Unit 4 — The Derivative as Limit of Rate of Change

Page convention. "p.N" means the PDF page number of MTH102.pdf. The pages of this unit carry no printed page numbers (checked on renders of pp.67–78), so PDF numbers are the only reference. The unit is PDF pp.67–78. In the course it is headed "Module Two, Unit 1" and its sections are numbered 4.1–4.9 (this is why it is called Unit 4 here). Equation numbers such as (1), (2) are the course's own and restart in every derivation.

Likely to be examined. The course's own objective list (p.67–68, section 4.2) is: differentiation from first principle (4.3), derivative of $x^n$ (4.4), differentiation of polynomials (4.5), standard derivatives (4.6). There are no Self-Assessment Exercises and no Tutor-Marked Assignment printed in this unit. The only questions are the Exercises 4.7 on pp.77–78: A (first principle: $x^3$, $5x^2+2$, $6x^2-1$, $4x^3$) and B (three polynomials). The Course Guide (pp.10–12) says TMAs are in a separate assignment file (30 % of the grade) and the final examination is 70 %, two hours. It gives no questions for this unit. Expect: differentiate from first principles, differentiate polynomials and evaluate at a point, recall the standard derivatives, and use the derivative as slope/rate of change.

What you must be able to do

  • Explain the gradient of a line, the gradient of a chord (secant) of a curve, and how the tangent gradient is the limit of chord gradients.
  • Differentiate a polynomial, $\sin x$ or $\cos x$ from first principles: write $y+\delta y$, subtract $y$, divide by $\delta x$, let $\delta x\to0$, and show every step.
  • Prove $\frac{d}{dx}x^n=nx^{n-1}$ for a positive integer $n$ with the binomial theorem.
  • Differentiate any polynomial term by term and evaluate the derivative at a given $x$.
  • Quote the table of standard derivatives (p.77) from memory, with the conditions under which each holds.
  • Read $\frac{dy}{dx}$ as a slope and as an instantaneous rate of change; find the equation of a tangent and a normal line, and a velocity from a distance function.

Key ideas

1. Gradient of a line, chord and tangent

Intuition. A straight line rises by the same amount for every unit moved to the right. That constant ratio is the gradient

$$m=\frac{\Delta y}{\Delta x}=\frac{y_2-y_1}{x_2-x_1}\qquad(\text{p.68; the course prints }\Delta x/\Delta y\text{ in the middle, see Errata}).$$

A curve has no single gradient, it bends. Take a fixed point $P(x,y)$ and a neighbouring point $Q(x+\delta x,\,y+\delta y)$ on the curve (p.68–69). The line $PQ$ is a chord (secant) with gradient $\delta y/\delta x$. As $Q$ slides along the curve towards $P$, the chord turns and settles on the tangent at $P$. The tangent's gradient is the gradient of the curve at $P$.

2. The derivative (first principle)

The gradient of the tangent is the limit of the chord gradients:

$$\frac{dy}{dx}=\lim_{\delta x\to0}\frac{\delta y}{\delta x}=\lim_{\delta x\to0}\frac{f(x+\delta x)-f(x)}{\delta x}.$$

This is the first principle (definition) of differentiation. Points to hold on to:

  • $\delta x,\ \delta y$ are small increments (changes), not "differentials". The course (p.69) calls them differentials; strictly, $dx,\ dy$ are the differentials. See Errata.
  • Notation. $\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}$ is a chord gradient and depends on $\delta x$. $\dfrac{dy}{dx}$ is the derivative: the limit. The course writes "$\delta y/\delta x=6x$" at the end of its derivations; the right-hand side is the limit, so the left-hand side must be $dy/dx$.
  • $dy/dx$ is not "$0/0$". The ratio $\delta y/\delta x$ is simplified first (cancel $\delta x\ne0$), and only then is $\delta x$ set to $0$.
  • The derivative exists at $x$ only if the limit exists as a finite number. A corner, like $|x|$ at $0$, has no derivative there.
  • Derivative of $f$ at a point: $f'(a)=\dfrac{dy}{dx}\Big|_{x=a}$. The course writes "the value of $\delta y/\delta x$ at $x=2$" (p.73).

3. Derivative as a rate of change

$\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}$ is the average rate of change of $y$ per unit of $x$ over an interval. $\dfrac{dy}{dx}$ is the instantaneous rate of change at one value of $x$ (p.67 intro). The sign matters: if $dy/dx>0$ for every $x$ in an interval, $y$ is increasing on that interval; if $dy/dx<0$ there, decreasing. $dy/dx=0$ at a single point only says the tangent is horizontal there (e.g. $y=x^3$ at $x=0$ is still increasing). Units are (units of $y$)/(units of $x$): if $s$ is distance in metres and $t$ time in seconds, $ds/dt$ is velocity in m/s. The course text states this idea in the Introduction (p.67) and Summary (p.78) but gives no worked rate problems; Examples 9 and 10 below are supplementary.

4. Differentiation rules used in this unit

  • Constant: $\dfrac d{dx}(c)=0$. A horizontal line has gradient $0$.
  • Power: $\dfrac d{dx}(x^n)=nx^{n-1}$. The course proves it with the binomial theorem (p.71–72), which is a finite expansion only when $n$ is a positive integer. The table (p.77) quotes the same rule for every $n$ (negative, fractional) without proof. It is true for all real $n$ (for $x>0$ when $n$ is not an integer), see Examples 5(b),(c) for $1/x$ and $\sqrt x$.
  • Constant multiple and sum: $(c\,f)'=c\,f'$, $(f\pm g)'=f'\pm g'$. So a polynomial is differentiated term by term (p.72, 78).

5. Derivatives of $\sin x$, $\cos x$, $\tan x$

The first-principle proof for $\sin x$ rests on one limit:

$$\lim_{\theta\to0}\frac{\sin\theta}{\theta}=1\qquad(\theta\text{ in radians}).$$

Why it is true: for $0<\theta<\frac\pi2$, comparing areas in the unit circle gives $\sin\theta<\theta<\tan\theta$; divide by $\sin\theta$ to get $\cos\theta<\frac{\sin\theta}{\theta}<1$, and $\cos\theta\to1$, so the quotient is squeezed to $1$. $\frac{\sin\theta}\theta$ is even in $\theta$, so the left-hand limit is the same. The course never states this limit: it just puts $\delta x=0$ into $\frac{\sin(\delta x/2)}{\delta x/2}$, which is $\frac00$ (p.74–75). Angles must be in radians, otherwise $\sin\theta/\theta\not\to1$.

$\tan x$ is differentiated with the quotient rule (p.76), which the course only teaches in Unit 2 (PDF p.79 onward). The result $\sec^2x$ holds where $\cos x\ne0$.

6. Tangent and normal

At the point $(a,f(a))$ the tangent has gradient $m=f'(a)$ and equation $y-f(a)=f'(a)(x-a)$. The normal is the line through the same point perpendicular to the tangent: gradient $-\dfrac1{f'(a)}$ when $f'(a)\ne0$. If $f'(a)=0$ the tangent is horizontal and the normal is the vertical line $x=a$. (Supplementary: not in the course text, but follows directly from "derivative = tangent gradient".)

Formula sheet

First principle

$$\frac{dy}{dx}=\lim_{\delta x\to0}\frac{f(x+\delta x)-f(x)}{\delta x},$$
$$m=\frac{\Delta y}{\Delta x}=\frac{y_2-y_1}{x_2-x_1}.$$

Rules

$$\frac d{dx}(c)=0,\qquad \frac d{dx}(ax^n)=a\,n\,x^{n-1},$$
$$\frac d{dx}\big(f\pm g\big)=f'\pm g'.$$

Binomial expansion (positive integer $n$, used on p.71–72)

$$(x+\delta x)^n=x^n+nx^{n-1}\delta x+\frac{n(n-1)}{2!}x^{n-2}(\delta x)^2+\frac{n(n-1)(n-2)}{3!}x^{n-3}(\delta x)^3+\cdots+(\delta x)^n.$$

Trig identities used in the proofs

$$\sin A-\sin B=2\cos\frac{A+B}2\sin\frac{A-B}2,$$
$$\cos A-\cos B=-2\sin\frac{A+B}2\sin\frac{A-B}2,$$
$$\sin^2x+\cos^2x=1.$$
$$\lim_{\theta\to0}\frac{\sin\theta}{\theta}=1.$$

Standard derivatives (course table p.77, with conditions added)

$y$ $\dfrac{dy}{dx}$ Condition
$c$ (constant) $0$
$x^n$ $nx^{n-1}$ any real $n$ (proved in the course only for $n\in\mathbb Z^+$)
$a\,x^n$ $a\,n\,x^{n-1}$ $a$ constant
$\sin x$ $\cos x$ $x$ in radians
$\cos x$ $-\sin x$ $x$ in radians
$\tan x$ $\sec^2x$ radians; $\cos x\ne0$
$\cot x$ $-\operatorname{cosec}^2x$ radians; $\sin x\ne0$
$\sec x$ $\sec x\tan x$ radians; $\cos x\ne0$
$\operatorname{cosec}x$ $-\operatorname{cosec}x\cot x$ radians; $\sin x\ne0$
$e^x$ $e^x$
$e^{kx}$ $k\,e^{kx}$ $k$ constant
$a^x$ $a^x\ln a$ $a>0$ (the course lists this row twice)
$\ln x$ $\dfrac1x$ $x>0$
$\sinh x$ $\cosh x$
$\cosh x$ $\sinh x$

Tangent and normal at $x=a$: tangent $y-f(a)=f'(a)(x-a)$; normal $y-f(a)=-\dfrac{1}{f'(a)}(x-a)$ ($f'(a)\ne0$).

Worked examples

All results below were checked with sympy (limit, diff, expand).

Example 1 — From chord to tangent (Course p.68–69, numerical)

Curve $y=3x^2+6$ (the course's curve, p.69). Find the gradient of the chord from $P(2,18)$ to $Q$ with $x=2+h$, and then the tangent gradient at $P$.

Solution. $\delta y=3(2+h)^2+6-18=12h+3h^2$, so the chord gradient is $\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}=\dfrac{12h+3h^2}h=12+3h$ ($h\ne0$).

$h$ 1 0.1 0.01 0.001
chord gradient $12+3h$ 15 12.3 12.03 12.003

The values close in on $12$; as $h\to0$ the limit is $\mathbf{12}$. So the tangent at $P$ has gradient $12$, i.e. $\frac{dy}{dx}=6x=12$ at $x=2$ (Example 2 gets $6x$ for every $x$).

Example 2 — First principle for $y=3x^2+6$ (Course p.69–70, redone correctly)

Solution. 1. At $Q$: $y+\delta y=3(x+\delta x)^2+6=3x^2+6x\,\delta x+3(\delta x)^2+6$. (The course prints "$2x-\delta x$" for "$2x\,\delta x$" here.) 2. Subtract $y=3x^2+6$ (the course subtracts $3x^2+6x$ by mistake and silently repairs it later):

$$\delta y=6x\,\delta x+3(\delta x)^2 .$$
  1. Divide by $\delta x\ne0$: $\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}=6x+3\,\delta x$. (The course writes $3|\delta x|$; $(\delta x)^2/\delta x$ is $\delta x$, not $|\delta x|$.)
  2. Let $\delta x\to0$: $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=\mathbf{6x}$.

Tangent at $x=1$: $y=9$, gradient $6$, so $y-9=6(x-1)$, i.e. $y=6x+3$.

Example 3 — Course Example 2, $y=x^2+3x$ (p.70–71, redone)

Solution. $y+\delta y=(x+\delta x)^2+3(x+\delta x)=x^2+2x\,\delta x+(\delta x)^2+3x+3\,\delta x$ (the course has a stray extra "$+\delta x$" in this line). Subtract $y=x^2+3x$: $\delta y=2x\,\delta x+(\delta x)^2+3\,\delta x$ (the course prints "$8y$" for $\delta y$). Divide by $\delta x$: $\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}=2x+\delta x+3$ (course: $2x+|\delta x|+3$). Let $\delta x\to0$: $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=\mathbf{2x+3}$. At $x=2$ the gradient is $7$.

Example 4 — Course Exercises 4.7 A, first principle (p.77)

For each, $\delta y=f(x+\delta x)-f(x)$, divide by $\delta x$, then let $\delta x\to0$.

(i) $y=x^3$: $(x+\delta x)^3-x^3=3x^2\delta x+3x(\delta x)^2+(\delta x)^3$. Divide: $3x^2+3x\,\delta x+(\delta x)^2\to\mathbf{3x^2}$. (ii) $y=5x^2+2$: $\delta y=5(2x\,\delta x+(\delta x)^2)$ (the constant cancels). Divide: $10x+5\delta x\to\mathbf{10x}$. (iii) $y=6x^2-1$: $\delta y=6(2x\,\delta x+(\delta x)^2)$. Divide: $12x+6\delta x\to\mathbf{12x}$. (iv) $y=4x^3$: $\delta y=4(3x^2\delta x+3x(\delta x)^2+(\delta x)^3)$. Divide: $12x^2+12x\,\delta x+4(\delta x)^2\to\mathbf{12x^2}$. Each agrees with $\frac d{dx}(ax^n)=anx^{n-1}$. The constants $+2$ and $-1$ disappear because a constant has $\delta y=0$.

Example 5 — The power rule (Course p.71–72)

(a) $y=x^n$, $n$ a positive integer (course proof). By the binomial theorem,

$$\delta y=(x+\delta x)^n-x^n=nx^{n-1}\delta x+\frac{n(n-1)}{2!}x^{n-2}(\delta x)^2+\cdots+(\delta x)^n .$$

Divide by $\delta x$: $\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}=nx^{n-1}+\dfrac{n(n-1)}{2!}x^{n-2}\delta x+\cdots+(\delta x)^{n-1}$. Every term after the first has a factor $\delta x$, so it $\to0$. Hence $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=\mathbf{nx^{n-1}}$. Check, $n=4$: sympy gives $\dfrac{(x+h)^4-x^4}{h}=4x^3+6x^2h+4xh^2+h^3\to4x^3$.

(b) $y=\dfrac1x$ ($x\ne0$), beyond the course. $\dfrac{\frac1{x+h}-\frac1x}{h}=\dfrac{-h}{x(x+h)h}=-\dfrac1{x(x+h)}\to-\dfrac1{x^2}=\mathbf{-x^{-2}}$, which is $nx^{n-1}$ with $n=-1$.

(c) $y=\sqrt x$ ($x>0$), beyond the course. Rationalise: $\dfrac{\sqrt{x+h}-\sqrt x}h=\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{x+h}+\sqrt x}\to\dfrac1{2\sqrt x}=\mathbf{\tfrac12x^{-1/2}}$, which is $nx^{n-1}$ with $n=\frac12$. So the table's "$x^n$ for every $n$" is correct, but the binomial argument on p.72 does not prove it for $n=-1$ or $\frac12$.

Example 6 — Polynomials and values (Course p.72–73; Exercises 4.7 B, p.77–78)

Differentiate term by term; $\frac d{dx}(ax^n)=anx^{n-1}$.

Course Example 1 (p.72–73). $y=x^3+5x^2-4x+2$: $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=3x^2+10x-4$. (The course writes the middle terms as "$2.5x^{2-1}$" and "$1.4x^{1-1}$": the dots mean $2\times5$ and $1\times4$, not decimals.)

Course Example 2 (p.73). $y=x^4+6x^3-4x^2+7x-2$: $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=4x^3+18x^2-8x+7$. At $x=2$: $4(8)+18(4)-8(2)+7=32+72-16+7=\mathbf{95}$.

Exercises 4.7 B (p.77–78). (i) $6x^3+4x^2-7x+2\ \Rightarrow\ 18x^2+8x-7$. (ii) $15x^3-6x^2+10\ \Rightarrow\ 45x^2-12x$. (iii) $10x^5+7x^3+2x\ \Rightarrow\ 50x^4+21x^2+2$.

Example 7 — $\sin x$ and $\cos x$ from first principles (Course p.73–76, redone)

(a) $y=\sin x$. $\delta y=\sin(x+\delta x)-\sin x$. By $\sin A-\sin B=2\cos\frac{A+B}2\sin\frac{A-B}2$ with $A=x+\delta x$, $B=x$:

$$\delta y=2\cos\!\Big(x+\frac{\delta x}2\Big)\sin\frac{\delta x}2 .$$

Divide by $\delta x$ and write $\delta x=2\cdot\frac{\delta x}2$ (a product, not the "$+$" the course prints):

$$\frac{\delta y}{\delta x}=\cos\!\Big(x+\frac{\delta x}2\Big)\cdot\frac{\sin(\delta x/2)}{\delta x/2}.$$

As $\delta x\to0$, put $\theta=\delta x/2\to0$: $\frac{\sin\theta}\theta\to1$ (Key ideas §5) and $\cos(x+\theta)\to\cos x$ because $\cos$ is continuous. So $\dfrac{d}{dx}\sin x=\mathbf{\cos x}$.

(b) $y=\cos x$. $\delta y=\cos(x+\delta x)-\cos x=-2\sin\!\big(x+\tfrac{\delta x}2\big)\sin\tfrac{\delta x}2$ (by $\cos A-\cos B=-2\sin\frac{A+B}2\sin\frac{A-B}2$). Divide by $\delta x$:

$$\frac{\delta y}{\delta x}=-\sin\!\Big(x+\frac{\delta x}2\Big)\frac{\sin(\delta x/2)}{\delta x/2}\to-\sin x\cdot1 .$$

So $\dfrac d{dx}\cos x=\mathbf{-\sin x}$. (The course's line "$-2\cos(\ldots)\sin(\ldots)/\delta x$" has $\cos$ where $\sin$ belongs; its next line is right.)

Example 8 — $\tan x$ (Course p.76)

$\tan x=\dfrac{\sin x}{\cos x}$, valid where $\cos x\ne0$. Quotient rule $\Big(\frac uv\Big)'=\frac{vu'-uv'}{v^2}$ with $u=\sin x$, $v=\cos x$, $u'=\cos x$, $v'=-\sin x$:

$$\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{\cos x\cos x-\sin x(-\sin x)}{\cos^2x}=\frac{\cos^2x+\sin^2x}{\cos^2x}=\frac1{\cos^2x}=\mathbf{\sec^2x}.$$

(The course writes $\partial u/\partial x$ and $\cos^2B$ here; ordinary $d/dx$ and $\sin^2A+\cos^2A=1$ are meant.) Sympy confirms $\frac{d}{dx}\tan x-\frac1{\cos^2x}=0$.

Example 9 — Tangent and normal (supplementary)

Find the tangent and normal to $y=x^2+3x$ at $x=1$, and the points where $y=x^3-3x+1$ has a horizontal tangent.

Solution. $y'=2x+3$; at $x=1$: $y=4$, gradient $5$. Tangent: $y-4=5(x-1)\Rightarrow\mathbf{y=5x-1}$. Normal gradient $-\frac15$: $y-4=-\frac15(x-1)\Rightarrow\mathbf{y=-\frac x5+\frac{21}5}$. For $y=x^3-3x+1$: $y'=3x^2-3=0\Rightarrow x=\pm1$, giving the points $\mathbf{(1,-1)}$ and $\mathbf{(-1,3)}$.

Example 10 — Rates of change (supplementary)

(a) Velocity. A particle has distance $s=t^3-6t^2+9t$ (m, $t$ in s). Velocity $v=\dfrac{ds}{dt}=3t^2-12t+9=3(t-1)(t-3)$. It is momentarily at rest when $v=0$: $t=1$ ($s=4$) and $t=3$ ($s=0$). Acceleration $\dfrac{dv}{dt}=6t-12$. Average velocity on $[1,4]$ is $\dfrac{s(4)-s(1)}{3}=\dfrac{4-4}3=0$, even though the instantaneous velocity at $t=4$ is $v(4)=9$ m/s: average and instantaneous rates differ. (b) Area of a circle. $A=\pi r^2$; $\dfrac{dA}{dr}=2\pi r$. At $r=5$ the instantaneous rate is $10\pi$ per unit of radius. The actual increase from $r=5$ to $r=6$ is $11\pi$, the average rate over that interval; they differ because the rate keeps growing.

Common mistakes

  • Writing the derivative as $\delta y/\delta x$. $\delta y/\delta x$ is a chord gradient; the derivative is $dy/dx=\lim\delta y/\delta x$. Do not write "$\delta y/\delta x=6x$" as a final answer.
  • Letting $\delta x\to0$ too early. Putting $\delta x=0$ in $\frac{\delta y}{\delta x}$ gives $\frac00$. Expand, subtract, divide by $\delta x$ and cancel, then take the limit.
  • Forgetting to subtract $y$. $\delta y=f(x+\delta x)-f(x)$ and the original terms must cancel. The course's slip with $3x^2+6x$ shows how easily a wrong subtraction creeps in (an $x$ appears in place of $+6$).
  • Expanding $(x+\delta x)^2$ as $x^2+\delta x^2$ (dropping $2x\,\delta x$). The middle term is the whole derivative.
  • Constants: the derivative of $c$ is $0$, but the term $5x^2+2$ keeps its $5x^2$ factor: $(5x^2+2)'=10x$, not $10x+2$.
  • Evaluating $\sin\theta/\theta\to1$ in degrees. It needs radians. Without it, $(\sin x)'=\frac\pi{180}\cos x$.
  • Mixing up $\sin\to\cos$ signs: $(\cos x)'=-\sin x$ (minus), $(\sin x)'=+\cos x$. Replace the "$+$" in the sine proof by a product: $\cos(\cdot)+\sin(\cdot)$ is not $2\cos(\cdot)\sin(\cdot)$.
  • Applying the power rule to $a^x$. $\frac d{dx}x^n=nx^{n-1}$ (variable base) but $\frac d{dx}a^x=a^x\ln a$ (variable exponent); $\frac d{dx}2^x\ne x\,2^{x-1}$.
  • $\frac d{dx}e^{kx}=ke^{kx}$, not $e^{kx}$ (forgetting the factor $k$).
  • Confusing the table's $\ln x$: $\frac d{dx}\ln x=\frac1x$ needs $x>0$.
  • Normal gradient: it is $-1/m$ (negative reciprocal), not $-m$ and not $1/m$.
  • Average versus instantaneous rate: $\frac{s(b)-s(a)}{b-a}$ is not the velocity at $a$ or at $b$.
  • Value at a point: differentiate first, then substitute $x=2$. Substituting first gives the derivative of a constant, $0$.

Practice problems

  1. (Course Ex. 4.7 A.) Differentiate from first principle: (i) $y=x^3$ (ii) $y=5x^2+2$ (iii) $y=6x^2-1$ (iv) $y=4x^3$.
  2. (Course Ex. 4.7 B.) Find the derivative: (i) $y=6x^3+4x^2-7x+2$ (ii) $y=15x^3-6x^2+10$ (iii) $y=10x^5+7x^3+2x$.
  3. From first principle find $\dfrac{dy}{dx}$ for (a) $y=2x^2-x+4$ (b) $y=x^3-2x$.
  4. From first principle differentiate $y=\sqrt{x+3}$ and state for which $x$ it holds.
  5. For $y=x^3-2x^2+5x-1$ find $\dfrac{dy}{dx}$ and its value at $x=-1$.
  6. Differentiate: (a) $3\sin x+2\cos x$ (b) $4\tan x-5e^x$ (c) $7e^{2x}+\ln x$.
  7. Find the equations of the tangent and the normal to $y=3x^2+6$ at $x=2$.
  8. For $y=x^3-3x$, find the gradient at $x=2$ and the values of $x$ where the tangent is horizontal.
  9. A body moves so that $s=5t^2+2t$ (metres, seconds). Find its velocity at $t=3$ and its average velocity over $1\le t\le3$.
  10. Find the point on $y=x^2+3x$ where the tangent is parallel to the line $y=7x+1$.
  11. Prove from first principle that $\dfrac d{dx}\cos x=-\sin x$, naming the standard limit you use.
  12. Show that the course's step "$\delta y/\delta x=6x+3|\delta x|$" (p.70) should read $6x+3\,\delta x$, and say why $\lim=6x$ either way.
Show answers

Answers

  1. (i) $3x^2$: $\frac{(x+h)^3-x^3}h=3x^2+3xh+h^2\to3x^2$. (ii) $10x$: $\frac{5(2xh+h^2)}h=10x+5h$. (iii) $12x$: $12x+6h$. (iv) $12x^2$: $12x^2+12xh+4h^2$. Same as Example 4. (sympy-verified)
  2. (i) $18x^2+8x-7$. (ii) $45x^2-12x$. (iii) $50x^4+21x^2+2$.
  3. (a) $\dfrac{2(x+h)^2-(x+h)+4-(2x^2-x+4)}h=4x+2h-1\to\mathbf{4x-1}$. (b) $\dfrac{(x+h)^3-2(x+h)-x^3+2x}h=3x^2+3xh+h^2-2\to\mathbf{3x^2-2}$.
  4. $\dfrac{\sqrt{x+3+h}-\sqrt{x+3}}h=\dfrac1{\sqrt{x+3+h}+\sqrt{x+3}}\to\mathbf{\dfrac1{2\sqrt{x+3}}}$, valid for $x>-3$.
  5. $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=3x^2-4x+5$; at $x=-1$: $3+4+5=\mathbf{12}$.
  6. (a) $3\cos x-2\sin x$. (b) $4\sec^2x-5e^x$ (sympy gives the equal form $4\tan^2x+4-5e^x$), $x\ne\frac\pi2+k\pi$. (c) $14e^{2x}+\dfrac1x$, $x>0$.
  7. $y'=6x=12$ at $x=2$, $y=18$. Tangent: $y-18=12(x-2)$, i.e. $\mathbf{y=12x-6}$. Normal gradient $-\frac1{12}$: $y-18=-\frac1{12}(x-2)$, i.e. $\mathbf{y=-\frac{x}{12}+\frac{109}6}$.
  8. $y'=3x^2-3$; at $x=2$: $\mathbf9$. $y'=0\Rightarrow x=\mathbf{\pm1}$.
  9. $v=\dfrac{ds}{dt}=10t+2$; at $t=3$: $\mathbf{32}$ m/s. Average over $[1,3]$: $\dfrac{s(3)-s(1)}2=\dfrac{51-7}2=\mathbf{22}$ m/s.
  10. $y'=2x+3=7\Rightarrow x=2$, $y=10$: the point $\mathbf{(2,10)}$.
  11. $\cos(x+h)-\cos x=-2\sin\!\big(x+\frac h2\big)\sin\frac h2$. So $\frac{\delta y}h=-\sin\!\big(x+\frac h2\big)\cdot\frac{\sin(h/2)}{h/2}$. As $h\to0$: $\sin(x+\frac h2)\to\sin x$ (continuity) and $\frac{\sin\theta}\theta\to1$ ($\theta=\frac h2$, radians). Limit $=-\sin x$. ∎
  12. $\delta y=6x\,\delta x+3(\delta x)^2$; dividing by $\delta x$ gives $6x+3(\delta x)^2/\delta x=6x+3\,\delta x$ (for $\delta x<0$, $3|\delta x|=-3\delta x\ne3\delta x$). Both $3\delta x$ and $3|\delta x|$ tend to $0$ as $\delta x\to0$ (from either side), so the limit is $6x$ in either case; but the correct line is $6x+3\delta x$.

All numerical and symbolic results in the examples and problems were checked with sympy.

Extra practice (from outside sources)

From outside sources — not in the NOUN course text Copied from OpenStax Calculus Volume 1 (CC BY 4.0); each problem names its section and exercise number.

Not from the NOUN course text. These problems come from the free OpenStax Calculus textbooks (CC BY 4.0). Tap Source ↗ to open the original page. Answers are worked out and checked here.

  1. Use Equation 3.1 to find the slope of the secant line between the values $x_1$ and $x_2$ for $y=f(x)=x^{2}+2x+1$; $x_1=3$, $x_2=3.5$. Source ↗
  2. Use Equation 3.4 to find the slope of the tangent line $m_{tan}=f'(a)$, and find an equation of the tangent line to $f$ at $x=a$: $f(x)=x^{2}+x$, $a=1$. Source ↗
  3. Use Equation 3.4 to find the slope of the tangent line $m_{tan}=f'(a)$, and find an equation of the tangent line to $f$ at $x=a$: $f(x)=2-3x^{2}$, $a=-2$. Source ↗
  4. Use Equation 3.4 to find the slope of the tangent line $m_{tan}=f'(a)$, and find an equation of the tangent line to $f$ at $x=a$: $f(x)=\dfrac{2}{x+3}$, $a=-4$. Source ↗
  5. For the function $y=f(x)=\sqrt{x}$, find $f'(a)$ using Equation 3.5, with $a=4$. Source ↗
  6. For the position function $y=s(t)=2t^{3}+3$, an object is moving along a straight line, where $t$ is in seconds and $s$ is in meters. (a) Find the simplified expression for the average velocity from $t=2$ to $t=2+h$. (b) Find the average velocity between $t=2$ and $t=2+h$, where (i) $h=0.1$, (ii) $h=0.01$, (iii) $h=0.001$, and (iv) $h=0.0001$. (c) Use the answer from (a) to estimate the instantaneous velocity at $t=2$ second. Source ↗
  7. Use the limit definition of derivative to show that the derivative does not exist at $x=a$ for $f(x)=x^{1/3}$, $x=0$. Source ↗
  8. Suppose that $N(x)$ computes the number of gallons of gas used by a vehicle traveling $x$ miles. Suppose the vehicle gets $30$ mpg. (a) Find a mathematical expression for $N(x)$. (b) What is $N(100)$? Explain the physical meaning. (c) What is $N'(100)$? Explain the physical meaning. Source ↗
Show answers (extra practice)

Answers (extra practice)

All eight exercises are odd-numbered, and each answer below agrees with the OpenStax answer key (Chapter 3).

  1. $f(3)=16$, $f(3.5)=12.25+7+1=20.25$. Slope $=\dfrac{20.25-16}{3.5-3}=\dfrac{4.25}{0.5}=\mathbf{8.5}$. (Check: $f(x)=(x+1)^2$, so the chord slope between $x_1,x_2$ is $x_1+x_2+2=8.5$.)
  2. $f(1)=2$. $\dfrac{f(1+h)-f(1)}h=\dfrac{(1+h)^2+(1+h)-2}h=\dfrac{3h+h^2}h=3+h\to3$. So $m_{tan}=\mathbf3$; tangent through $(1,2)$: $y-2=3(x-1)$, i.e. $\mathbf{y=3x-1}$.
  3. $f(-2)=2-12=-10$. $\dfrac{f(-2+h)-f(-2)}h=\dfrac{2-3(4-4h+h^2)+10}h=\dfrac{12h-3h^2}h=12-3h\to12$. $m_{tan}=\mathbf{12}$; $y+10=12(x+2)$, i.e. $\mathbf{y=12x+14}$.
  4. $f(-4)=\dfrac2{-1}=-2$. $\dfrac{f(-4+h)-f(-4)}h=\dfrac1h\left(\dfrac2{h-1}+2\right)=\dfrac1h\cdot\dfrac{2+2(h-1)}{h-1}=\dfrac1h\cdot\dfrac{2h}{h-1}=\dfrac2{h-1}\to-2$. $m_{tan}=\mathbf{-2}$; $y+2=-2(x+4)$, i.e. $\mathbf{y=-2x-10}$.
  5. $\dfrac{f(4+h)-f(4)}h=\dfrac{\sqrt{4+h}-2}h=\dfrac{(4+h)-4}{h\left(\sqrt{4+h}+2\right)}=\dfrac1{\sqrt{4+h}+2}\to\dfrac1{2+2}=\mathbf{\dfrac14}$. (Same method as the course's $\sqrt{x+3}$ question; it agrees with the general rule $\frac1{2\sqrt x}$ at $x=4$.)
  6. (a) $\dfrac{s(2+h)-s(2)}h=\dfrac{2(8+12h+6h^2+h^3)+3-19}h=\dfrac{24h+12h^2+2h^3}h=\mathbf{24+12h+2h^2}=2(h^2+6h+12)$. (b) (i) $25.22$ m/s; (ii) $24.1202$ m/s ($\approx24.12$); (iii) $24.012002$ m/s ($\approx24.01$); (iv) $24.00120002$ m/s ($\approx24.00$). (c) As $h\to0$ the expression $\to\mathbf{24}$ m/s, the instantaneous velocity at $t=2$.
  7. For $f(x)=x^{1/3}$, $f(0)=0$ and $\dfrac{f(0+h)-f(0)}h=\dfrac{h^{1/3}}h=\dfrac1{h^{2/3}}$. Since $h^{2/3}=\left(h^{1/3}\right)^2>0$ for $h\ne0$ of either sign, the quotient is positive and grows without bound: $\lim_{h\to0^+}=\lim_{h\to0^-}=+\infty$. The limit is not a finite number, so $f'(0)$ does not exist (the graph has a vertical tangent at the origin).
  8. (a) $N(x)=\dfrac{x}{30}$ gallons. (b) $N(100)=\dfrac{100}{30}=\dfrac{10}3\approx3.3$: after driving $100$ miles the vehicle has used about $3.3$ gallons. (c) $N'(x)=\dfrac1{30}$ for every $x$, so $N'(100)=\dfrac1{30}\approx0.033$ gallons per mile: the rate at which gas is being used after $100$ miles (constant, because the car's fuel economy is constant).

All results above were checked with sympy (limits, difference quotients, tangent lines) and compared with the OpenStax answer key.

Unit 5 of 8

Unit 5 — Differentiation Techniques

Page convention. The course PDF (122 pages) has no printed page numbers in this unit. "p.N" below means the PDF page N. Unit 5 is PDF pp.79–96 (the PDF calls it "Module Two, Unit 2: Differentiation Techniques"; its section numbers are mixed up: 5.x, 3.5, 1.7 — see Errata). Layout: product rule pp.79–84, quotient rule pp.84–88, chain rule pp.88–90, implicit functions pp.91–94, exercises p.95, summary and references pp.95–96. The course writes $\delta y/\delta x$ where it means the derivative $dy/dx$.

Likely to be examined (objectives p.79; Checkpoints p.82, 88, 90, 94; Exercises A–D p.95). This unit has no Self-Assessment Exercises or Tutor-Marked Assignment of its own; the Course Guide (p.11) says the TMAs come "in a separate file". The final exam lasts two hours, you answer three questions out of at least five (Guide, p.12). Expect: product, quotient and chain rule drills, $dy/dx$ of implicit functions, $d^2y/dx^2$ of an implicit function at a point, and the tangent line at a point of a curve.

What you must be able to do

  • Differentiate a product $uv$ with the product rule, and a quotient $u/v$ with the quotient rule.
  • Differentiate a composite function (function of a function) with the chain rule, also when the rules are combined (product with chain, quotient with chain).
  • Simplify the result and leave it in factors, as the course asks (p.95, Exercise A).
  • Differentiate an implicit function: find $dy/dx$ from an equation in $x$ and $y$ that cannot be solved for $y$.
  • Find $d^2y/dx^2$ of an implicit function at a given point.
  • Find the equation of the tangent (and normal) to a curve at a point, including when $x$ is given and the $y$-values must be found first.
  • Prove the product and quotient rules from first principles.

Key ideas

1. Product rule

Intuition. A rectangle with sides $u$ and $v$: when $x$ changes, both sides change. The area $uv$ grows by a thin strip along each side ($u\,\delta v$ and $v\,\delta u$) plus a tiny corner $\delta u\,\delta v$, which vanishes faster than the rest.

Statement. If $u$ and $v$ are differentiable at $x$, then $uv$ is differentiable at $x$ and

$$\frac{d}{dx}(uv)=u\frac{dv}{dx}+v\frac{du}{dx}.$$

Proof (course p.79–80, tidied). $\delta y=(u+\delta u)(v+\delta v)-uv=u\,\delta v+v\,\delta u+\delta u\,\delta v$. Divide by $\delta x$: $\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}=u\dfrac{\delta v}{\delta x}+v\dfrac{\delta u}{\delta x}+\dfrac{\delta u}{\delta x}\,\delta v$. As $\delta x\to0$, $\delta v\to0$ (because $v$ is differentiable, hence continuous), so the last term $\to \dfrac{du}{dx}\cdot0=0$. The course writes the result with $\delta$'s; the rule is about the limit, $dy/dx$.

Habit: write down $u$, $v$, $u'$, $v'$ first, then assemble. Take out common factors at the end.

2. Quotient rule

Statement. If $u,v$ are differentiable at $x$ and $v(x)\ne0$, then

$$\frac{d}{dx}\!\left(\frac uv\right)=\frac{v\,\dfrac{du}{dx}-u\,\dfrac{dv}{dx}}{v^2}.$$

Order matters: bottom times derivative of top, minus top times derivative of bottom, over bottom squared. Swapping the two terms flips the sign. A quotient may often be simpler as a product: $\dfrac{u}{v}=u\,v^{-1}$ (but the quotient rule is the course's method).

Proof (corrected; the course p.84–85 has sign slips).

$$\delta y=\frac{u+\delta u}{v+\delta v}-\frac uv=\frac{v(u+\delta u)-u(v+\delta v)}{v(v+\delta v)}=\frac{v\,\delta u-u\,\delta v}{v(v+\delta v)}.$$

Divide by $\delta x$ and let $\delta x\to0$ ($\delta v\to0$, $v\ne0$): $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=\dfrac{v\,u'-u\,v'}{v^2}$.

3. Chain rule (function of a function)

Intuition. If $y$ depends on $u$ and $u$ depends on $x$, rates multiply: $y$ changes $\frac{dy}{du}$ times as fast as $u$, and $u$ changes $\frac{du}{dx}$ times as fast as $x$.

Statement. If $u=g(x)$ is differentiable at $x$ and $y=f(u)$ is differentiable at $u=g(x)$, then

$$\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{dy}{du}\cdot\frac{du}{dx}.$$

Procedure. Name the inside function $u$; differentiate the outside with respect to $u$; multiply by $du/dx$; replace $u$ by its expression in $x$ at the end.

Note on the course's argument (p.88): "cancel $\delta u$" is only a heuristic, since $\delta u$ may be $0$ for some $\delta x$. The theorem is true; the one-line cancellation is not a proof.

Special case used all the time: $\dfrac{d}{dx}(ax+b)^n=na\,(ax+b)^{n-1}$; $\dfrac d{dx}\sin(ax+b)=a\cos(ax+b)$; $\dfrac d{dx}e^{ax+b}=a\,e^{ax+b}$.

4. Implicit functions

A relation like $x^3+y^3=2xy$ defines $y$ as a function of $x$ (near a point) without solving for $y$. To find $dy/dx$: 1. Differentiate both sides with respect to $x$. 2. Every time you differentiate an expression in $y$, multiply by $dy/dx$ (chain rule): $\dfrac d{dx}y^2=2y\dfrac{dy}{dx}$. Products like $xy$ need the product rule: $\dfrac d{dx}(xy)=y+x\dfrac{dy}{dx}$. 3. Constants differentiate to $0$. 4. Collect all terms containing $dy/dx$ on one side, factor it out and divide.

The answer usually contains both $x$ and $y$. The point must lie on the curve before you use it: check that its coordinates satisfy the equation.

Second derivative. Differentiate $dy/dx$ (a function of $x$ and $y$) once more with respect to $x$, treating $y$ as a function of $x$, then replace $dy/dx$ by its expression or value.

Tangent and normal at $(x_1,y_1)$ on the curve with slope $m=dy/dx$ there: tangent $y-y_1=m(x-x_1)$; normal (if $m\ne0$) slope $-1/m$.

Formula sheet

$$\frac d{dx}(uv)=uv'+vu',$$
$$\frac d{dx}\frac uv=\frac{vu'-uv'}{v^2}\ (v\ne0),$$
$$\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{dy}{du}\frac{du}{dx}.$$
$$\frac d{dx}(ax+b)^n=na(ax+b)^{n-1},$$
$$\frac d{dx}\sqrt{ax+b}=\frac a{2\sqrt{ax+b}},$$
$$\frac d{dx}\frac1{f(x)}=-\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)^2}.$$
$$(\sin x)'=\cos x,\qquad (\cos x)'=-\sin x,$$
$$(\tan x)'=\sec^2x,\qquad (\cot x)'=-\operatorname{cosec}^2x,$$
$$(e^x)'=e^x,$$
$$(e^{ax+b})'=ae^{ax+b},\qquad (x^n)'=nx^{n-1}.$$
$$\text{Implicit: }\ \frac d{dx}f(y)=f'(y)\frac{dy}{dx},$$
$$\frac d{dx}(xy)=y+x\frac{dy}{dx}.$$

Shortcut (not in the course; fine for checking answers): if the curve is $F(x,y)=0$, then $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=-\dfrac{F_x}{F_y}$ where $F_x,F_y$ are the partial derivatives.

$$\text{Tangent: }y-y_1=m(x-x_1),$$
$$\text{normal slope }=-\frac1m.$$

Worked examples

All results below were checked with sympy 1.14 (script under /tmp/mth102_scratch/unit5/).

Example 1 — Product rule (Course Examples 1–2, p.80–81)

(a) $y=x^3\sin x$. $u=x^3$, $v=\sin x$; $u'=3x^2$, $v'=\cos x$.

$$y'=u v'+v u'=x^3\cos x+3x^2\sin x=x^2\,(x\cos x+3\sin x).$$

The course (p.80) writes $\sin x\cdot3x$ instead of $\sin x\cdot3x^2$ and so gets $x(x^2\cos x+3\sin x)$: wrong. Check at $x=\pi/2$: the correct value is $y'(\pi/2)=3\pi^2/4\approx7.40$, the course's expression gives $3\pi/2\approx4.71$.

(b) $y=(3x-2)(x^2+3)$. $u=3x-2$, $v=x^2+3$: $y'=(3x-2)(2x)+(x^2+3)(3)=6x^2-4x+3x^2+9=\mathbf{9x^2-4x+9}$ (course p.81 ✓). Check by expanding first: $y=3x^3-2x^2+9x-6$, $y'=9x^2-4x+9$ ✓.

Example 2 — Product rule with powers and chain (Course Examples 3–5, p.81–82)

(a) $y=x^5e^x$: $y'=x^5e^x+e^x\cdot5x^4=x^4e^x(x+5)$ ✓.

(b) $y=x^2(2x-5)^4$. $u=x^2$, $u'=2x$. For $v=(2x-5)^4$ use the chain rule with $w=2x-5$: $v'=4w^3\cdot2=8(2x-5)^3$.

$$y'=x^2\cdot8(2x-5)^3+(2x-5)^4\cdot2x=2x(2x-5)^3\,[4x+(2x-5)]=\mathbf{2x(2x-5)^3(6x-5)}.$$

(The course reuses the letters $u,v,y$ for two different things on p.81, which is confusing; the result is right.)

(c) $y=(3x-1)^3(x^2+5)$. $u'=9(3x-1)^2$ (chain), $v'=2x$:

$$y'=(3x-1)^3\,2x+(x^2+5)\,9(3x-1)^2=(3x-1)^2\,[2x(3x-1)+9(x^2+5)]=(3x-1)^2(15x^2-2x+45).$$

✓ (course p.82).

Example 3 — Product checkpoints (Course p.82, solutions p.83–84)

Problem Derivative
1. $(x^2-1)(x^3+1)$ $(x^2-1)(3x^2)+(x^3+1)(2x)=5x^4-3x^2+2x=x(5x^3-3x+2)$
2. $x(x^2-1)^3$ $x\cdot6x(x^2-1)^2+(x^2-1)^3=(x^2-1)^2(7x^2-1)$
3. $e^x\sin x$ $e^x(\sin x+\cos x)$
4. $4x^3\sin x$ $4x^3\cos x+12x^2\sin x=4x^2(x\cos x+3\sin x)$
5. $3x^3e^x$ $3x^3e^x+9x^2e^x=3x^2e^x(x+3)$

The course's printed problem 5 reads "$3x^3e^3$", but its solution uses $e^x$; see Errata. Course solution 1 has the slip "$du/dx=ex$" for $2x$; the answer is right. Course solution 4 labels $dv/dx$ as $dy/dx$.

Example 4 — Quotient rule (Course Examples 1–2, p.85–87)

(a) $y=\dfrac{x^2}{\sqrt{x+1}}$. $u=x^2$, $v=(x+1)^{1/2}$, $u'=2x$, $v'=\dfrac1{2\sqrt{x+1}}$.

$$y'=\frac{\sqrt{x+1}\,(2x)-\dfrac{x^2}{2\sqrt{x+1}}}{x+1}.$$

Multiply top and bottom by $2\sqrt{x+1}$: numerator $4x(x+1)-x^2=3x^2+4x$, denominator $2(x+1)^{3/2}$:

$$y'=\frac{x(3x+4)}{2(x+1)^{3/2}}\quad(x>-1)\qquad\text{✔ course p.86.}$$

Check by product form: $y=x^2(x+1)^{-1/2}$, $y'=2x(x+1)^{-1/2}-\tfrac12x^2(x+1)^{-3/2}=\dfrac{4x(x+1)-x^2}{2(x+1)^{3/2}}$ ✓.

(b) $y=\dfrac{\sin x}{x^2}$. $y'=\dfrac{x^2\cos x-\sin x\cdot2x}{x^4}=\dfrac{x\cos x-2\sin x}{x^3}$ ($x\ne0$) ✓ (course p.86–87, cancelling one $x$).

Example 5 — Quotient rule, more (Course Examples 3–4, p.87–88)

(a) Course Example 3. The problem is printed as $y=\dfrac{5e^x}{\cos x}$, but the solution works with $\dfrac{4e^x}{\cos x}$. The two disagree; for the printed problem:

$$y'=\frac{\cos x\cdot5e^x-5e^x(-\sin x)}{\cos^2x}=\frac{5e^x(\cos x+\sin x)}{\cos^2x},$$

and the course's $\dfrac{4e^x(\cos x+\sin x)}{\cos^2x}$ is the correct answer for $4e^x/\cos x$.

(b) Course Example 4. $\dfrac d{dx}\tan x=\dfrac{\cos x\cos x-\sin x(-\sin x)}{\cos^2x}=\dfrac{\cos^2x+\sin^2x}{\cos^2x}=\dfrac1{\cos^2x}=\sec^2x$ ✓.

(c) Course checkpoint (p.88), corrected. - $y=\dfrac{4x+1}{7x-4}$: $y'=\dfrac{4(7x-4)-7(4x+1)}{(7x-4)^2}=\dfrac{-23}{(7x-4)^2}$. The course gives "$-a/(7x-4)$" (garbled and the square is missing). - $y=\dfrac{x^2-1}{x^3}$: $y'=\dfrac{x^3\cdot2x-3x^2(x^2-1)}{x^6}=\dfrac{-x^4+3x^2}{x^6}=\dfrac{3-x^2}{x^4}$. The course answer "$x(-x^2+3x+2)/(x^2)^2$" simplifies to $\dfrac{-x^2+3x+2}{x^3}$, which is not equal to $\dfrac{3-x^2}{x^4}$. - $y=\dfrac{\cos x}{\sin x}$: $y'=\dfrac{-\sin^2x-\cos^2x}{\sin^2x}=-\operatorname{cosec}^2x$ ✓.

Example 6 — Chain rule (Course Examples 1–4, p.89–90, corrected)

(a) $y=\dfrac1{(6x-5)^2}=(6x-5)^{-2}$. Put $u=6x-5$, so $y=u^{-2}$ (the course writes "$y=u^2$"; its next line $dy/du=-2u^{-3}$ is for $u^{-2}$). $\dfrac{du}{dx}=6$, $\dfrac{dy}{du}=-2u^{-3}$. $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=-12u^{-3}=\mathbf{-\dfrac{12}{(6x-5)^3}}$ ✓.

(b) $y=(2x+8)^2$. $u=2x+8$, so $y=u^2$ (the course writes $y=u^3$: wrong). $\dfrac{du}{dx}=2$, $\dfrac{dy}{du}=2u$, so $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=4u=\mathbf{4(2x+8)=8x+32}$. The course's answer $6(2x+8)^2$ is wrong. Check by expanding: $y=4x^2+32x+64$, $y'=8x+32$ ✓.

(c) $y=\sin(5x-6)$: $u=5x-6$; $y'=\cos u\cdot5=5\cos(5x-6)$ ✓. (d) $y=\tan(3x+2)$: $y'=3\sec^2(3x+2)$ ✓ (course prints "$u=3x=2$" and "$3x=2$": typos for $3x+2$). (e) Course checkpoint ii (p.90): $y=\sqrt{4x-3}=(4x-3)^{1/2}$, $y'=\tfrac12(4x-3)^{-1/2}\cdot4=\dfrac2{\sqrt{4x-3}}$ for $x>\tfrac34$. The course prints $\dfrac{2}{(4x-3)^{3/2}}$: wrong (the exponent is $\tfrac12$, not $\tfrac32$). Checkpoints i, iii are right: $6(2x+5)^2$, $4\cos(4x+3)$.

(f) Two layers (new). $y=\sin^2(3x+1)$: outside $w^2$, middle $\sin$, inside $3x+1$. $y'=2\sin(3x+1)\cdot\cos(3x+1)\cdot3=3\sin(6x+2)$.

Example 7 — Mixed rules (new, exam level)

(a) $y=\dfrac{(3x-1)^3}{x^2+5}$. Quotient with chain inside: $u'=9(3x-1)^2$, $v'=2x$.

$$y'=\frac{(x^2+5)\cdot9(3x-1)^2-(3x-1)^3\cdot2x}{(x^2+5)^2}=\frac{(3x-1)^2\,[9x^2+45-6x^2+2x]}{(x^2+5)^2}=\frac{(3x-1)^2(3x^2+2x+45)}{(x^2+5)^2}.$$

(b) $y=e^{2x-3}\cos(7x+3)$. Product; $u'=2e^{2x-3}$, $v'=-7\sin(7x+3)$:

$$y'=e^{2x-3}\big[2\cos(7x+3)-7\sin(7x+3)\big].$$

(c) $y=\dfrac{\sqrt{x^2+1}}{x+2}$. $u'=\dfrac x{\sqrt{x^2+1}}$:

$$y'=\frac{\dfrac{x(x+2)}{\sqrt{x^2+1}}-\sqrt{x^2+1}}{(x+2)^2}=\frac{x(x+2)-(x^2+1)}{(x+2)^2\sqrt{x^2+1}}=\frac{2x-1}{(x+2)^2\sqrt{x^2+1}}.$$

Example 8 — Implicit differentiation (Course Examples 1 and 3, p.91 and p.93; Checkpoint p.94)

(a) $2x^2+3y^2=16$. Differentiate: $4x+6y\,y'=0$, so $y'=-\dfrac{4x}{6y}=-\dfrac{2x}{3y}$ ($y\ne0$) ✓ (course leaves it as $-4x/6y$).

(b) $x^3+y^3=2xy$. (The course says "differentiating with respect to $y$"; it means $x$.) Differentiate with respect to $x$, product rule on $2xy$:

$$3x^2+3y^2y'=2y+2xy'\ \Longrightarrow\ y'(3y^2-2x)=2y-3x^2\ \Longrightarrow\ y'=\frac{2y-3x^2}{3y^2-2x}\quad\text{✔ course p.93.}$$

(c) Checkpoint I, $x^2+2xy+3y^2=4$: $2x+2y+2xy'+6yy'=0$, so $y'=-\dfrac{x+y}{x+3y}$ ✓. (d) Checkpoint II, $x^3+y^3+3xy^2=8$: $3x^2+3y^2y'+3y^2+6xy\,y'=0$, so $y'(3y^2+6xy)=-(3x^2+3y^2)$:

$$y'=-\frac{x^2+y^2}{y^2+2xy}.$$

The course prints the denominator as $y^2+2y$: the $x$ is missing (wrong).

Example 9 — Second derivative of an implicit function (Course Example 2 p.91–93, Exercise D I p.95)

(a) The course's Example 2 has a defect. "Find $dy/dx$ and $d^2y/dx^2$ for $2x^2+3y^2-4x-3y+6=0$ at $x=3$, $y=2$." The point $(3,2)$ is not on the curve: $2(9)+3(4)-12-6+6=18\ne0$. In fact the curve has no real points at all: $2(x-1)^2+3(y-\tfrac12)^2+\tfrac{13}4=0$ has no real solution (the left side is $\ge\frac{13}4$). The course's method is fine, so redo it with the constant changed so that $(3,2)$ lies on the curve: $2x^2+3y^2-4x-3y-12=0$ (check: $18+12-12-6-12=0$ ✓).

Differentiate: $4x+6yy'-4-3y'=0\Rightarrow y'=\dfrac{4-4x}{6y-3}=\dfrac{4(1-x)}{3(2y-1)}$. (The course p.92 prints "$(-4-4x)/(6y-3)$" once: typo, the line before has the correct $4-4x$.) At $(3,2)$: $y'=\dfrac{4-12}{12-3}=-\dfrac89$.

Second derivative, quotient rule on $y'=\dfrac{4-4x}{6y-3}$ ($u=4-4x$, $u'=-4$; $v=6y-3$, $v'=6y'$):

$$y''=\frac{(6y-3)(-4)-(4-4x)(6y')}{(6y-3)^2}.$$

At $(3,2)$, $y'=-\frac89$: numerator $=9(-4)-(-8)\cdot6\cdot(-\tfrac89)=-36-\tfrac{128}3=-\tfrac{236}3$, denominator $81$, so

$$y''=-\frac{236}{243}\approx-0.971.$$

The course's arithmetic (p.92–93) gives the same $-236/243$; the value does not depend on the constant in the equation, but the problem as printed has no point to evaluate at.

(b) Exercise D I: $x^2+y^2-2x+2y=23$ at $(-2,3)$. On the curve: $4+9+4+6=23$ ✓. $2x+2yy'-2+2y'=0\Rightarrow y'=\dfrac{1-x}{y+1}$; at $(-2,3)$: $y'=\dfrac34$. $y''=\dfrac{-(y+1)-(1-x)y'}{(y+1)^2}$, which (using the curve, $(x-1)^2+(y+1)^2=25$) equals $-\dfrac{25}{(y+1)^3}$. At $(-2,3)$: $-\dfrac{25}{64}$. (Direct: $\dfrac{-4-3\cdot\frac34}{16}=\dfrac{-25/4}{16}=-\dfrac{25}{64}$ ✓.)

Example 10 — Tangent to an implicit curve (Course Example 4, p.93–94, redone)

Find the tangents where $x=2$ on $x^2+y^2-2x+y=6$ (the course prints it as "$x^2+y^2=2x+y=6$"; its solution uses $x^2+y^2-2x+y=6$).

Slope. $2x+2yy'-2+y'=0\Rightarrow y'=\dfrac{2-2x}{2y+1}$. Points with $x=2$. $4+y^2-4+y=6\Rightarrow y^2+y-6=0\Rightarrow(y+3)(y-2)=0$, so $y=-3$ or $y=2$. The points are $(2,-3)$ and $(2,2)$ (the course prints "$(2,-2)$" for the second point: typo). - At $(2,-3)$: $m=\dfrac{2-4}{-6+1}=\dfrac25$. Tangent: $y+3=\tfrac25(x-2)\Rightarrow5y+15=2x-4\Rightarrow\mathbf{2x-5y-19=0}$ (course: $5y-2x+19=0$, same line). - At $(2,2)$: $m=\dfrac{2-4}{4+1}=-\dfrac25$. Tangent: $y-2=-\tfrac25(x-2)\Rightarrow5y-10=-2x+4\Rightarrow\mathbf{2x+5y-14=0}$ ✓ (the course's arithmetic is right; it labels the point $(2,-2)$ in the heading by mistake, and $(2,-2)$ is not on the curve).

Extra (new): the normal at $(2,2)$ has slope $\tfrac52$: $y-2=\tfrac52(x-2)$, i.e. $5x-2y-6=0$.

Example 11 — Proving the quotient rule (exam proof, corrected course p.84–85)

Let $y=u/v$ with $u,v$ differentiable and $v(x)\ne0$. Then

$$\frac{\delta y}{\delta x}=\frac1{\delta x}\left[\frac{u+\delta u}{v+\delta v}-\frac uv\right]=\frac{v\,\delta u-u\,\delta v}{v(v+\delta v)\,\delta x}=\frac{v\dfrac{\delta u}{\delta x}-u\dfrac{\delta v}{\delta x}}{v(v+\delta v)}.$$

Let $\delta x\to0$. Then $\delta v\to0$ (differentiable implies continuous), so $v(v+\delta v)\to v^2\ne0$ and

$$\frac{dy}{dx}=\frac{v\,u'-u\,v'}{v^2}.\ \blacksquare$$

The course (p.84–85) writes $v\delta u-v\delta u$, then $v\delta u+u\delta v$, then "$-u\delta v$" again, and says "$\delta u\to0$ and $\delta x\to0$" when it means $\delta v\to0$; only its last line (4) has the right sign.

Common mistakes

  • Treating the derivative of a product as the product of derivatives: $(uv)'\ne u'v'$. Same for quotients: $(u/v)'\ne u'/v'$.
  • Quotient rule in the wrong order. It is $vu'-uv'$, not $uv'-vu'$. Test with $u=1$, $v=x$: the answer must be $-1/x^2$.
  • Forgetting the square in the denominator, or squaring $v$ and then expanding it when it is not needed.
  • Chain rule: forgetting the inner derivative. $\dfrac d{dx}\sin(5x-6)=5\cos(5x-6)$, not $\cos(5x-6)$. And $\dfrac d{dx}(2x+8)^2=4(2x+8)$, not $2(2x+8)$.
  • Writing the wrong power for $y$ in terms of $u$. $(6x-5)^{-2}$ is $u^{-2}$, not $u^2$; $(2x+8)^2$ is $u^2$, not $u^3$ (both slips are in the course).
  • Square-root exponents. $\dfrac d{dx}\sqrt{4x-3}=\dfrac2{\sqrt{4x-3}}$: the exponent after differentiating is $-\tfrac12$, not $-\tfrac32$.
  • Implicit differentiation: forgetting $dy/dx$ after differentiating a term in $y$ ($y^2\to2y\,y'$), or forgetting the product rule on $xy$.
  • Not collecting the $dy/dx$ terms before dividing; dividing term-by-term.
  • Using a point that is not on the curve. Substitute into the original equation first.
  • Losing a point when finding the tangent at "x = 2". Solve for all $y$ with that $x$ (here two points, two tangents).
  • Second derivative of an implicit function: differentiating $y'$ but forgetting that $y$ inside it is a function of $x$ (you need $y'$ again).
  • Not simplifying fully. The course asks for the result left in factors (p.82, p.95).
  • Mixing up $\delta y/\delta x$ and $dy/dx$. The course uses $\delta$ for the derivative; in an answer write $\dfrac{dy}{dx}$.

Practice problems

  1. Exercise A (p.95): differentiate, leaving the answer in factors: (I) $(2x-1)(x+4)^2$; (II) $3x^3(x^2+4)^2$; (III) $\sqrt x\,(x+3)^2$; (IV) $5x^3\sin x$; (V) $\cos x\sin x$; (VI) $e^x\cos x$; (VII) $2x^5\cos x$.
  2. Exercise B (p.95): (I) $\dfrac{5x^2}{\cos x}$; (II) $\dfrac{6e^x}{\sin x}$; (III) $\dfrac{\cos x}{x^5}$; (IV) $\dfrac{x^3+1}{x-1}$; (V) $\dfrac{2x^2-x+3}{2x-5}$.
  3. Exercise C (p.95): (I) $(7x-2)^7$; (II) $5x^3-2$ (as printed; see Errata); (III) $\dfrac1{x^2+2x-3}$; (IV) $\cos(7x+3)$; (V) $e^{2x-3}$.
  4. Exercise D I (p.95): If $x^2+y^2-2x+2y=23$, find $\dfrac{dy}{dx}$ and $\dfrac{d^2y}{dx^2}$ at $x=-2$, $y=3$. Also find the tangent line there.
  5. Exercise D II (p.95): find $dy/dx$: (a) $xy-x^3=6$; (b) $x^2+y^2=8$; (c) $x^2+6xy=4y$; (d) $x^3+y^3-x-y=3$.
  6. Checkpoints: (p.82) 1–5 as in Example 3; (p.88) a–c; (p.90) i–iii; (p.94) I, II. State the correct answers (several are misprinted in the course).
  7. Differentiate $y=\dfrac{(3x-1)^3}{x^2+5}$ and simplify.
  8. Differentiate $y=e^{2x-3}\cos(7x+3)$.
  9. Find the tangent and the normal to $x^2+y^2=8$ at $(2,2)$.
  10. For $x^3+y^3=2xy$ (Course Example 3, p.93) find $dy/dx$ and $d^2y/dx^2$ at the point $(1,1)$.
  11. Find the points on $x^2+y^2-2x+2y=23$ where the tangent is horizontal.
  12. Prove the quotient rule from first principles (state the hypotheses).
Show answers

Answers

  1. (I) $6(x+1)(x+4)$ [product: $2(x+4)^2+(2x-1)2(x+4)=2(x+4)(x+4+2x-1)=2(x+4)(3x+3)$]. (II) $3x^2(x^2+4)(7x^2+12)$. (III) $\dfrac{(x+3)(5x+3)}{2\sqrt x}$ for $x>0$. (IV) $5x^2(x\cos x+3\sin x)$. (V) $\cos^2x-\sin^2x=\cos2x$. (VI) $e^x(\cos x-\sin x)$. (VII) $2x^4(5\cos x-x\sin x)$.
  2. (I) $\dfrac{10x\cos x+5x^2\sin x}{\cos^2x}=\dfrac{5x(2\cos x+x\sin x)}{\cos^2x}$. (II) $\dfrac{6e^x(\sin x-\cos x)}{\sin^2x}$. (III) $-\dfrac{x\sin x+5\cos x}{x^6}$. (IV) $\dfrac{2x^3-3x^2-1}{(x-1)^2}$. (V) $\dfrac{4x^2-20x-1}{(2x-5)^2}$.
  3. (I) $49(7x-2)^6$. (II) $15x^2$. (III) $-\dfrac{2(x+1)}{(x^2+2x-3)^2}$ (i.e. $-\dfrac{2x+2}{(x-1)^2(x+3)^2}$). (IV) $-7\sin(7x+3)$. (V) $2e^{2x-3}$.
  4. The point is on the curve. $y'=\dfrac{1-x}{y+1}=\dfrac34$ at $(-2,3)$; $y''=-\dfrac{25}{(y+1)^3}=-\dfrac{25}{64}$. Tangent: $y-3=\tfrac34(x+2)$, i.e. $3x-4y+18=0$.
  5. (a) $y'=\dfrac{3x^2-y}{x}$. (b) $y'=-\dfrac xy$. (c) $y'=-\dfrac{x+3y}{3x-2}$. (d) $y'=\dfrac{1-3x^2}{3y^2-1}$.
  6. (p.82) $5x^4-3x^2+2x$; $(x^2-1)^2(7x^2-1)$; $e^x(\sin x+\cos x)$; $4x^2(x\cos x+3\sin x)$; $3x^2e^x(x+3)$. (p.88) $\dfrac{-23}{(7x-4)^2}$; $\dfrac{3-x^2}{x^4}$; $-\operatorname{cosec}^2x$. (p.90) $6(2x+5)^2$; $\dfrac2{\sqrt{4x-3}}$; $4\cos(4x+3)$. (p.94) I: $-\dfrac{x+y}{x+3y}$; II: $-\dfrac{x^2+y^2}{y^2+2xy}$.
  7. $\dfrac{(3x-1)^2(3x^2+2x+45)}{(x^2+5)^2}$.
  8. $e^{2x-3}\big[2\cos(7x+3)-7\sin(7x+3)\big]$.
  9. $y'=-x/y=-1$ at $(2,2)$ (on the curve: $4+4=8$ ✓). Tangent: $y-2=-(x-2)$, i.e. $x+y=4$. Normal: slope $1$, $y=x$.
  10. $y'=\dfrac{2y-3x^2}{3y^2-2x}$; at $(1,1)$ (on the curve: $1+1=2$ ✓): $y'=\dfrac{2-3}{3-2}=-1$. $y''=\dfrac{(2y'-6x)(3y^2-2x)-(2y-3x^2)(6yy'-2)}{(3y^2-2x)^2}$; at $(1,1)$: $\dfrac{(-2-6)(1)-(-1)(-6-2)}{1}=-8-8=-16$.
  11. $y'=0\iff x=1$. Then $(1-1)^2+(y+1)^2=25$ (the curve is the circle $(x-1)^2+(y+1)^2=25$), so $y=4$ or $y=-6$. Points $(1,4)$ and $(1,-6)$.
  12. See Example 11: needs $u,v$ differentiable at $x$ and $v(x)\ne0$; uses $\delta v\to0$ because differentiable implies continuous.

All symbolic and numerical results in the examples and problems were checked with sympy 1.14.

Extra practice (from outside sources)

From outside sources — not in the NOUN course text Copied from OpenStax Calculus Volume 1 (CC BY 4.0); each problem names its section and exercise number.

Not from the NOUN course text. These problems come from the free OpenStax Calculus textbooks (CC BY 4.0). Tap Source ↗ to open the original page. Answers are worked out and checked here.

  1. Find $f'(x)$ for $f(x)=(x+2)(2x^2-3)$. Source ↗
  2. Find $f'(x)$ for $f(x)=\dfrac{x+9}{x^2-7x+1}$. Source ↗
  3. Find $f'(x)$ for $f(x)=\dfrac{e^x-e^{-x}}{e^x+e^{-x}}$. Source ↗
  4. Find $\dfrac{dy}{dx}$ for $y=x^2\cos^4x$. Source ↗
  5. Find an equation of the tangent line to the graph of $x^2y^2+5xy=14$ at the point $(2,1)$. Source ↗
  6. Find $y'$ and $y''$ for $x^2+6xy-2y^2=3$. Source ↗
  7. Find an equation of the line passing through the point $P(3,3)$ and tangent to the graph of $f(x)=\dfrac{6}{x-1}$. Source ↗
  8. Find an equation of the tangent line to the graph of $x^3-x\ln y+y^3=2x+5$ at the point $(2,1)$. (Hint: use implicit differentiation to find $\dfrac{dy}{dx}$.) Source ↗
Show answers (extra practice)

Answers (extra practice)

  1. Product rule with $u=x+2$, $v=2x^2-3$: $f'=1\cdot(2x^2-3)+(x+2)(4x)=2x^2-3+4x^2+8x=\mathbf{6x^2+8x-3}$. (Check: expand $f=2x^3+4x^2-3x-6$, differentiate: same.)
  2. Quotient rule: $f'=\dfrac{1\cdot(x^2-7x+1)-(x+9)(2x-7)}{(x^2-7x+1)^2}$. Numerator: $x^2-7x+1-(2x^2+11x-63)=-x^2-18x+64$. So $f'(x)=\mathbf{\dfrac{-x^2-18x+64}{(x^2-7x+1)^2}}$. (Agrees with OpenStax's odd-answer key.)
  3. Quotient rule with $u=e^x-e^{-x}$, $u'=e^x+e^{-x}$, $v=e^x+e^{-x}$, $v'=e^x-e^{-x}$: $f'=\dfrac{(e^x+e^{-x})^2-(e^x-e^{-x})^2}{(e^x+e^{-x})^2}$. The numerator is $4e^xe^{-x}=4$, so $f'(x)=\mathbf{\dfrac{4}{(e^x+e^{-x})^2}}$ $(=\operatorname{sech}^2x)$. (Agrees with the key.)
  4. Product rule, chain rule on $\cos^4x$: $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=2x\cos^4x+x^2\cdot4\cos^3x\,(-\sin x)=\mathbf{2x\cos^3x\,(\cos x-2x\sin x)}$.
  5. Check the point: $4\cdot1+5\cdot2=14$ ✓. Differentiate: $2xy^2+2x^2yy'+5y+5xy'=0$, so $y'=-\dfrac{2xy^2+5y}{2x^2y+5x}=-\dfrac{y(2xy+5)}{x(2xy+5)}=-\dfrac yx$. At $(2,1)$: $y'=-\tfrac12$. Tangent: $y-1=-\tfrac12(x-2)$, i.e. $\mathbf{y=-\tfrac12x+2}$. (Agrees with the key.)
  6. Differentiate: $2x+6y+6xy'-4yy'=0\Rightarrow y'=\mathbf{-\dfrac{x+3y}{3x-2y}}$. Differentiate again (quotient rule): $y''=-\dfrac{(1+3y')(3x-2y)-(x+3y)(3-2y')}{(3x-2y)^2}$. The numerator expands to $-11y+11xy'$; substitute $y'$: $-11\dfrac{y(3x-2y)+x(x+3y)}{3x-2y}=-11\dfrac{x^2+6xy-2y^2}{3x-2y}=-\dfrac{33}{3x-2y}$ (using the curve, $x^2+6xy-2y^2=3$). Hence $y''=\mathbf{\dfrac{33}{(3x-2y)^3}}$. (Sympy: numerical check at $x=1$, $y=\frac{3+\sqrt5}2$ on the curve gives $-2.9516$ both ways.)
  7. The point $(3,3)$ is on the curve ($6/2=3$), but do not assume it is the point of tangency. Tangent at $x=a$: $y=\dfrac6{a-1}-\dfrac6{(a-1)^2}(x-a)$. Put $x=3,\ y=3$ and $u=a-1$: $3=\dfrac6u-\dfrac{6(2-u)}{u^2}\Rightarrow3u^2=12u-12\Rightarrow(u-2)^2=0$, so $a=3$ (a double root: only one tangent). Slope $f'(3)=-\dfrac6{4}=-\dfrac32$. Line: $y-3=-\tfrac32(x-3)$, i.e. $\mathbf{y=-\tfrac32x+\tfrac{15}2}$. (Agrees with the key.)
  8. Check: $8-2\ln1+1=9=2\cdot2+5$ ✓. Differentiate: $3x^2-\ln y-\dfrac xy\,y'+3y^2y'=2$, so $y'=\dfrac{3x^2-\ln y-2}{x/y-3y^2}=\dfrac{y(3x^2-\ln y-2)}{x-3y^3}$. At $(2,1)$: $\dfrac{12-0-2}{2-3}=-10$. Tangent: $y-1=-10(x-2)$, i.e. $\mathbf{y=-10x+21}$.

Every result above was checked with sympy (python3 -I); items 2, 3, 5, 7 also agree with the OpenStax odd-answer key (https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/chapter-3).
