Unit 6 of 8

Unit 6 — Integration: indefinite integrals and techniques

Page convention. The course PDF (122 pages) has no printed page numbers on these pages, so "p.N" means the PDF page number. This unit is PDF pp.97–114. The course calls it "Module Two, Unit 3: Integration" (contents, p.9) and numbers its sections 6.1–6.8 (a leftover from an older edition); this study guide calls it Unit 6. Page map: p.97 introduction and objectives; p.98 6.3 Integration and 6.4 Notation, Example 1; p.99 standard integrals; pp.100–102 substitution for a linear function of $x$; pp.102–104 the $f'/f$ form; pp.104–106 integration by parts; pp.106–112 partial fractions (distinct factors, repeated factors, irreducible quadratic); pp.112–113 exercises 6.6 A–D; pp.113–114 summary and references. The course writes $\delta x$ where the usual notation is $dx$; this chapter uses $dx$.

Where are definite integrals? Not in these pages. Pages 97–114 contain only indefinite integrals (no limits, no area, no fundamental theorem). The contents page (p.9) lists "Module Three, Unit 1: Definite integrals (application to areas under curve and volumes of solids)", but the body has no such unit: the only definite-integral material is Module 8, "Volume of solids of revolution by definite integral", PDF pp.115–122, which uses $\int_a^b$ with no explanation and has no section on area under a curve. See Unit 7.

Likely to be examined. The course gives no named SAEs or TMA for this unit. The only questions are 6.6 Exercises A–D (pp.112–113): powers of $x$, integrals of a linear function of $x$, the $f'/f$ form and partial-fraction integrals, and four integrals by parts. The objectives (p.97) list the topics 6.3 Integration and 6.4 Notation and are cut off at the page end. The course guide (PDF p.11) says tutor-marked assignments are four selected by your tutor and count for 30% of the final grade; the rest is a final examination. I have no exam paper, so nothing here claims what the exam contains.

What you must be able to do

  • Say what $\int f(x)\,dx$ means (the family of antiderivatives) and why the constant $+c$ is needed.
  • Integrate powers of $x$ (including negative and fractional powers), sums and constant multiples, and expand brackets first when the integrand is a product.
  • Use the table of standard integrals, including $e^{kx}$, $a^x$, $\sin$, $\cos$, $\sec^2$, $\sinh$, $\cosh$ and the inverse-function results.
  • Integrate $f(ax+b)$ by the linear-substitution rule.
  • Recognise and integrate $\dfrac{f'(x)}{f(x)}$ (including $\tan x$ and $\cot x$) with the correct $\ln|\cdot|$.
  • Integrate a product by parts, choosing $u$ and $dv$ sensibly, repeating when needed (including the "cyclic" case $e^{ax}\sin bx$).
  • Resolve a rational function into partial fractions (distinct linear factors, a repeated linear factor, an irreducible quadratic factor) and integrate each piece; divide first when the fraction is improper.
  • Check any answer by differentiating it.

Key ideas

1. Integration as the reverse of differentiation (p.98)

Intuition. Differentiation turns a function into its slope function. Integration asks the reverse question: which function has this slope function? Since $\frac{d}{dx}\,\frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1}=x^n$, the antiderivative of $x^n$ is $\frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1}$.

Precise statement. $F$ is an antiderivative of $f$ on an interval if $F'(x)=f(x)$ there. Then

$$\int f(x)\,dx=F(x)+c,\qquad c\ \text{an arbitrary constant}.$$

Any two antiderivatives on an interval differ by a constant (that is why the constant is needed). Linearity: $\int(af+bg)\,dx=a\int f\,dx+b\int g\,dx$.

Power rule. $\displaystyle\int x^n\,dx=\frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1}+c$ for $n\ne-1$ (the course writes "$n\ne1$": wrong, see Errata). For $n=-1$: $\int\frac1x\,dx=\ln|x|+c$.

Why the constant. $x^3-x$, $x^3-x+5$ and $x^3-x-\sqrt2$ all have derivative $3x^2-1$ (the course's own example, p.98). Integrating cannot tell them apart, so we write $+c$.

2. Notation (p.98)

In $\int f(x)\,dx$ the sign $\int$ is the integral sign, $f(x)$ is the integrand, and $dx$ names the variable of integration; both $\int$ and $dx$ must be written. If $\frac{dy}{dx}=f(x)$ then $y=\int f(x)\,dx+c$.

3. Substitution for a linear function (pp.100–102)

Idea. If the inside of a function is $ax+b$, put $z=ax+b$; then $dz=a\,dx$, so $dx=\frac1a\,dz$. The course writes this as $\int f(z)\,\frac{dx}{dz}\,dz$ with $\frac{dx}{dz}=\frac1a$.

$$\int f(ax+b)\,dx=\frac1a\,F(ax+b)+c\quad(a\ne0)\qquad\text{where }F'=f.$$

Always return to $x$ at the end. General substitution (not in the course, but you will need it): if $z=g(x)$ then $\int f(g(x))\,g'(x)\,dx=\int f(z)\,dz$.

4. The form $\dfrac{f'(x)}{f(x)}$ (pp.102–104)

If the numerator is the derivative of the denominator, put $z=f(x)$, $dz=f'(x)\,dx$:

$$\int\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)}\,dx=\int\frac{dz}{z}=\ln|f(x)|+c .$$

The absolute value is required: $\ln$ is defined only for positive numbers and $f$ may be negative. If the numerator is only a constant multiple of $f'$ (as in $\frac{4x-8}{x^2-4x+5}$) take the constant out first. Consequences: $\int\tan x\,dx=-\ln|\cos x|+c$ and $\int\cot x\,dx=\ln|\sin x|+c$ (the course does the second on p.104). The course's heading also names $\int f(x)f'(x)\,dx$ but never treats it; the result is $\frac12f(x)^2+c$.

5. Integration by parts (pp.104–106)

From the product rule $\frac{d}{dx}(uv)=u\frac{dv}{dx}+v\frac{du}{dx}$, integrate both sides:

$$\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du,\qquad\text{i.e. }\int u\,v'\,dx=uv-\int u'v\,dx.$$

How to choose. Take $u$ = the factor that gets simpler when differentiated ($\ln x$, a polynomial, $\tan^{-1}x$); take $dv$ = the factor you can integrate ($e^{kx}$, $\sin$, $\cos$, a power). When $dv$ is integrated, omit the constant (the course says so on p.105: it would cancel). For $x^n e^{kx}$ you repeat the process $n$ times. For $e^{ax}\sin bx$ you get back the original integral after two rounds: solve for it (the "cyclic" case).

6. Partial fractions (pp.106–112)

A rational function $\frac{P(x)}{Q(x)}$ is integrated by splitting it into simpler fractions. First, if $\deg P\ge\deg Q$ (improper), divide $P$ by $Q$ (long division) to get a polynomial plus a proper fraction. Then factorise $Q$ over the real numbers and use the table:

Factor in $Q$ Terms in the decomposition Integral of each term
$(x-a)$ $\dfrac{A}{x-a}$ $A\ln\lvert x-a\rvert$
$(x-a)^2$ $\dfrac{A}{x-a}+\dfrac{B}{(x-a)^2}$ $A\ln\lvert x-a\rvert-\dfrac{B}{x-a}$
$(x-a)^3$ $\dfrac{A}{x-a}+\dfrac{B}{(x-a)^2}+\dfrac{C}{(x-a)^3}$ $\dots-\dfrac{C}{2(x-a)^2}$
$x^2+px+q$ (irreducible: $p^2-4q<0$) $\dfrac{Bx+C}{x^2+px+q}$ split into $\dfrac B2\ln(x^2+px+q)$ plus an $\tan^{-1}$ term

Finding the constants. Multiply through by $Q$ to get an identity in $x$; then (i) substitute the roots of $Q$ (cover-up method, quick for distinct linear factors and for the highest power of a repeated factor), and (ii) compare coefficients of powers of $x$ for what remains. An irreducible quadratic needs a linear numerator $Bx+C$. Do not call a denominator "irreducible" if it factors (the course does on p.107; see Errata).

Formula sheet

Basic rules

$$\int x^n\,dx=\frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1}+c\ (n\ne-1),$$
$$\int\frac1x\,dx=\ln|x|+c,$$
$$\int k\,f\,dx=k\int f\,dx,$$
$$\int(f\pm g)\,dx=\int f\pm\int g .$$

Course table (p.99), with corrected conditions

$$\int e^x\,dx=e^x,\qquad \int e^{kx}\,dx=\frac{e^{kx}}k\ (k\ne0),$$
$$\int a^x\,dx=\frac{a^x}{\ln a}\ (a>0,\ a\ne1),$$
$$\int\sin x\,dx=-\cos x,\qquad \int\cos x\,dx=\sin x,$$
$$\int\sec^2x\,dx=\tan x,$$
$$\int\sinh x\,dx=\cosh x,\qquad \int\cosh x\,dx=\sinh x,$$
$$\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{1-x^2}}=\sin^{-1}x\ (|x|<1),$$
$$\int\frac{-dx}{\sqrt{1-x^2}}=\cos^{-1}x\ (|x|<1),$$
$$\int\frac{dx}{1+x^2}=\tan^{-1}x,$$
$$\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{x^2+1}}=\sinh^{-1}x,$$
$$\int\frac{dx}{\sqrt{x^2-1}}=\cosh^{-1}x\ (x>1),$$
$$\int\frac{dx}{1-x^2}=\tanh^{-1}x\ (|x|<1)$$
$$\Big(=\tfrac12\ln\Big|\tfrac{1+x}{1-x}\Big|\ \text{for all }x\ne\pm1\Big).$$

(Add $+c$ to every line.)

Not in the course's table, but used in this chapter

$$\int\tan x\,dx=-\ln|\cos x|,$$
$$\int\cot x\,dx=\ln|\sin x|,$$
$$\int\frac{dx}{x^2+a^2}=\frac1a\tan^{-1}\frac xa,\qquad \int f f'\,dx=\tfrac12f^2,$$
$$\int\ln x\,dx=x\ln x-x .$$

Techniques

$$\int f(ax+b)\,dx=\frac{F(ax+b)}{a},$$
$$\int\frac{f'}{f}\,dx=\ln|f|,$$
$$\int u\,dv=uv-\int v\,du,$$
$$\int e^{ax}\sin bx\,dx=\frac{e^{ax}(a\sin bx-b\cos bx)}{a^2+b^2},$$
$$\int e^{ax}\cos bx\,dx=\frac{e^{ax}(a\cos bx+b\sin bx)}{a^2+b^2}.$$

Worked examples

All results below were checked with sympy (differentiating each answer gives back the integrand).

Example 1 — Powers of $x$ (Course Example 1, pp.98–99)

Integrate (a) $\int(x^3+3x^2+2x+4)\,dx$, (b) $\int(s^3+4s)\,ds$, (c) $\int(t^3+4t^2-2)\,dt$.

Solution. Use $\int x^n\,dx=\frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1}$ term by term, and $4=4x^0$ so $\int4\,dx=4x$. (a) $\dfrac{x^4}{4}+\dfrac{3x^3}{3}+\dfrac{2x^2}{2}+4x+c=\dfrac{x^4}4+x^3+x^2+4x+c$. (b) $\dfrac{s^4}{4}+\dfrac{4s^2}{2}+c=\dfrac{s^4}4+2s^2+c$. (c) $\dfrac{t^4}{4}+\dfrac{4t^3}{3}-2t+c$. Check (b): $\frac{d}{ds}\big(\frac{s^4}4+2s^2\big)=s^3+4s$ ✓. The letter of the variable does not matter, only that $ds$ matches it.

Example 2 — Linear substitution (Course Examples 1–3, pp.100–102)

Integrate (a) $\int(3x-2)^6\,dx$, (b) $\int\cos(6x+4)\,dx$, (c) $\int\sec^28x\,dx$.

Solution. (a) Let $z=3x-2$; $\frac{dz}{dx}=3$, so $dx=\frac13dz$. $\displaystyle\int z^6\cdot\frac13\,dz=\frac13\cdot\frac{z^7}7=\frac{(3x-2)^7}{21}+c$. (b) $z=6x+4$, $dx=\frac16dz$: $\frac16\int\cos z\,dz=\frac16\sin z=\dfrac{\sin(6x+4)}6+c$. (c) $z=8x$, $dx=\frac18dz$: $\frac18\int\sec^2z\,dz=\dfrac{\tan8x}8+c$. Shortcut: integrate as if the inside were $x$, then divide by the coefficient of $x$. The same rule gives Exercise B: $\int\frac{dx}{5x+4}=\frac15\ln|5x+4|+c$ and $\int4^{3x}dx=\dfrac{4^{3x}}{3\ln4}+c$.

Example 3 — The form $f'/f$ (Course pp.102–104, redone with absolute values)

Integrate (a) $\int\dfrac{2x-5}{x^2-5x+6}dx$, (b) $\int\dfrac{3x^2}{x^3-6}dx$, (c) $\int\dfrac{4x-8}{x^2-4x+5}dx$, (d) $\int\cot x\,dx$, (e) $\int\tan x\,dx$.

Solution. (a) The numerator is exactly the derivative of the denominator. $\ln|x^2-5x+6|+c$. (Note $x^2-5x+6=(x-2)(x-3)<0$ on $(2,3)$: the course's $\ln(x^2-5x+6)$ is undefined there.) (b) $\frac{d}{dx}(x^3-6)=3x^2$. $\ln|x^3-6|+c$. (c) $4x-8=2(2x-4)$ and $\frac{d}{dx}(x^2-4x+5)=2x-4$. So the integral is $2\ln(x^2-4x+5)+c$. Here no absolute value is needed: $x^2-4x+5=(x-2)^2+1>0$. (d) $\cot x=\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}$, $z=\sin x$, $dz=\cos x\,dx$: $\ln|\sin x|+c$. (e) $\tan x=\frac{\sin x}{\cos x}=-\frac{(\cos x)'}{\cos x}$: $-\ln|\cos x|+c$ (also $=\ln|\sec x|+c$).

Example 4 — Integration by parts, one round (Course Example 1, pp.104–105)

Find $\int x^2\ln x\,dx$ and $\int\ln x\,dx$.

Solution. $\ln x$ is not in the table, so take $u=\ln x$ (it simplifies on differentiating), $dv=x^2dx$. Then $du=\frac1x\,dx$ and $v=\frac{x^3}3$ (no constant).

$$\int x^2\ln x\,dx=\frac{x^3}3\ln x-\int\frac{x^3}3\cdot\frac1x\,dx=\frac{x^3}3\ln x-\frac13\int x^2dx=\frac{x^3}3\ln x-\frac{x^3}9+c=\frac{x^3}3\Big(\ln x-\frac13\Big)+c .$$

For $\int\ln x\,dx$ write the integrand as $\ln x\cdot1$: $u=\ln x$, $dv=dx$, $v=x$: $x\ln x-\int x\cdot\frac1x\,dx=x\ln x-x+c$. (Exercise D(ii) $\int x\ln x\,dx$ is the same pattern: $\frac{x^2}2\ln x-\frac{x^2}4+c$.)

Example 5 — Parts repeated (Course Example 2, pp.105–106; Exercise D iv)

Find $\int x^2e^{3x}\,dx$ and $\int x^3e^{2x}\,dx$.

Solution. Take $u$ = the power, $dv=e^{kx}dx$, $v=\frac{e^{kx}}k$.

$$\int x^2e^{3x}dx=\frac{x^2e^{3x}}3-\frac23\int xe^{3x}dx .$$

Again with $u=x$, $dv=e^{3x}dx$: $\int xe^{3x}dx=\frac{xe^{3x}}3-\frac13\int e^{3x}dx=\frac{xe^{3x}}3-\frac{e^{3x}}9$. Therefore

$$\int x^2e^{3x}dx=\frac{x^2e^{3x}}3-\frac{2xe^{3x}}9+\frac{2e^{3x}}{27}+c=\frac{e^{3x}}3\Big(x^2-\frac{2x}3+\frac29\Big)+c ,$$

which agrees with the course's final line. For $x^3e^{2x}$ use the same steps three times (or "tabular": differentiate $x^3$ down to $0$, integrate $e^{2x}$ down, alternate signs $+,-,+,-$):

$$\int x^3e^{2x}dx=e^{2x}\Big(\frac{x^3}2-\frac{3x^2}4+\frac{3x}4-\frac38\Big)+c .$$

Example 6 — The cyclic case (Exercise D i and iii)

Find $I=\int e^{3x}\sin x\,dx$ and $J=\int e^{5x}\sin3x\,dx$.

Solution. Take $u=\sin x$, $dv=e^{3x}dx$, $v=\frac{e^{3x}}3$:

$$I=\frac{e^{3x}\sin x}3-\frac13\int e^{3x}\cos x\,dx .$$

Parts again, $u=\cos x$, $dv=e^{3x}dx$: $\int e^{3x}\cos x\,dx=\frac{e^{3x}\cos x}3+\frac13\int e^{3x}\sin x\,dx=\frac{e^{3x}\cos x}3+\frac I3$. So

$$I=\frac{e^{3x}\sin x}3-\frac{e^{3x}\cos x}9-\frac I9\ \Longrightarrow\ \frac{10}9I=\frac{e^{3x}(3\sin x-\cos x)}9\ \Longrightarrow\ I=\frac{e^{3x}(3\sin x-\cos x)}{10}+c .$$

Same method (or the formula sheet with $a=5$, $b=3$): $J=\dfrac{e^{5x}(5\sin3x-3\cos3x)}{34}+c$.

Example 7 — Partial fractions, distinct linear factors (Course Example 1, pp.107–108)

Find $\int\dfrac{x+1}{x^2-3x+2}dx$.

Solution. $x^2-3x+2=(x-2)(x-1)$ (the course calls this denominator "irreducible"; it is not, it factors). Write $\frac{x+1}{(x-2)(x-1)}=\frac A{x-2}+\frac B{x-1}$, so $x+1=A(x-1)+B(x-2)$. Put $x=2$: $3=A$. Put $x=1$: $2=-B$, $B=-2$. Hence

$$\int\Big(\frac3{x-2}-\frac2{x-1}\Big)dx=3\ln|x-2|-2\ln|x-1|+c .$$

New, same type: $\int\frac{3x+5}{(x-1)(x+3)}dx$: $A=2$ (at $x=1$: $8=4A$), $B=1$ (at $x=-3$: $-4=-4B$), result $2\ln|x-1|+\ln|x+3|+c$.

Example 8 — Repeated factors (Course Examples 2 and 3, pp.108–111, corrected)

(a) $\displaystyle\int\frac{x^2}{(x+1)(x-1)^2}dx$. Write $\frac{x^2}{(x+1)(x-1)^2}=\frac A{x+1}+\frac B{x-1}+\frac C{(x-1)^2}$, so

$$x^2=A(x-1)^2+B(x+1)(x-1)+C(x+1).$$

$x=1$: $1=2C$, $C=\frac12$. $x=-1$: $1=4A$, $A=\frac14$. Coefficient of $x^2$: $1=A+B$, $B=\frac34$. Then

$$\int\Big(\frac{1/4}{x+1}+\frac{3/4}{x-1}+\frac{1/2}{(x-1)^2}\Big)dx=\frac14\ln|x+1|+\frac34\ln|x-1|-\frac1{2(x-1)}+c .$$

The last term is minus: $\int(x-1)^{-2}dx=-(x-1)^{-1}$. (The course prints $+\frac1{2(x-1)}$ and has $(x+1)^2$ in a line; see Errata.)

(b) $\displaystyle\int\frac{x^2+1}{(x+2)^3}dx$. Write $\frac{x^2+1}{(x+2)^3}=\frac A{x+2}+\frac B{(x+2)^2}+\frac C{(x+2)^3}$, so $x^2+1=A(x+2)^2+B(x+2)+C$. $x=-2$: $C=5$. $x^2$: $A=1$. Constant term: $1=4A+2B+C=4+2B+5$, $B=-4$. Then

$$\int\Big(\frac1{x+2}-\frac4{(x+2)^2}+\frac5{(x+2)^3}\Big)dx=\ln|x+2|+\frac4{x+2}-\frac5{2(x+2)^2}+c .$$

(Course final line has $(x+3)^2$ in the last term: a misprint. Quick alternative: substitute $z=x+2$, $x^2+1=z^2-4z+5$.)

Example 9 — Irreducible quadratic and improper fractions (Course Example 4, pp.111–112; plus two new)

(a) $\displaystyle\int\frac{x^2}{(x-2)(x^2+1)}dx$. $x^2+1$ has no real roots, so the numerator over it is linear:

$$\frac{x^2}{(x-2)(x^2+1)}=\frac A{x-2}+\frac{Bx+C}{x^2+1},\qquad x^2=A(x^2+1)+(Bx+C)(x-2).$$

$x=2$: $4=5A$, $A=\frac45$. $x^2$: $1=A+B$, $B=\frac15$. Constant: $0=A-2C$, $C=\frac25$. So

$$\int\Big[\frac{4/5}{x-2}+\frac15\cdot\frac{x}{x^2+1}+\frac25\cdot\frac1{x^2+1}\Big]dx=\frac45\ln|x-2|+\frac1{10}\ln(x^2+1)+\frac25\tan^{-1}x+c ,$$

using $\int\frac{x}{x^2+1}dx=\frac12\ln(x^2+1)$ (the $f'/f$ form with $f'=2x$). Matches the course.

(b) Improper (new). $\displaystyle\int\frac{x^3+2}{x^2-1}dx$. The degree of the top is larger, so divide: $x^3+2=x(x^2-1)+x+2$, giving $x+\frac{x+2}{x^2-1}$. Then $\frac{x+2}{(x-1)(x+1)}=\frac{3/2}{x-1}-\frac{1/2}{x+1}$ ($x=1$: $3=2A$; $x=-1$: $1=-2B$). Result

$$\frac{x^2}2+\frac32\ln|x-1|-\frac12\ln|x+1|+c .$$

(c) Complete the square (new). $\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x^2+4x+13}$: $x^2+4x+13=(x+2)^2+9$, so with $z=x+2$ this is $\int\frac{dz}{z^2+9}=\frac13\tan^{-1}\frac{x+2}3+c$.

Common mistakes

  • Forgetting $+c$ on an indefinite integral. (Also: a different-looking answer may be right, $c$ can absorb constants. Differentiate to check.)
  • Using the power rule at $n=-1$. $\int x^{-1}dx$ is $\ln|x|$, not $x^0/0$. The course prints the exception as $n\ne1$ (wrong).
  • Dropping the absolute value in $\ln|f(x)|$, in $\ln|x-a|$ and in $\ln|\sin x|$.
  • Not dividing by the inner coefficient: $\int\cos(6x+4)\,dx=\frac16\sin(6x+4)$, not $\sin(6x+4)$. And not multiplying: $\int e^{kx}dx=\frac1ke^{kx}$.
  • $\int f'/f$ with the wrong constant. $\int\frac{4x-8}{x^2-4x+5}dx$ is $2\ln(\ldots)$; $\int\frac{x}{x^2+1}dx$ is $\frac12\ln(x^2+1)$, not $\ln(x^2+1)$.
  • Splitting a quotient term by term wrongly. $\int\frac{1}{x^2+1}\,dx\ne\int\frac1{x^2}dx+\int\frac1{1}dx$: no rule splits a denominator. Use partial fractions only for factors.
  • Parts: choosing $u=x^2$, $dv=\ln x\,dx$ (harder); forgetting the minus sign in $uv-\int v\,du$; adding a constant to $v$ and carrying it wrongly; forgetting that each repeat of parts can flip a sign.
  • Cyclic parts: stopping after the integral comes back instead of solving for it; mistake in the sign of $\int e^{ax}\cos bx$.
  • Partial fractions, repeated factor: writing only $\frac B{(x-a)^2}$ and omitting $\frac A{x-a}$. A factor $(x-a)^2$ needs two terms.
  • Irreducible quadratic with constant numerator: use $\frac{Bx+C}{x^2+px+q}$, not $\frac C{x^2+px+q}$.
  • Improper fraction: applying partial fractions without dividing first ($\deg$ top $\ge\deg$ bottom).
  • $\int\frac{dx}{(x-1)^2}=-\frac{1}{x-1}$, not $+\frac1{x-1}$ and not $\ln(x-1)^2$.

Practice problems

(Problems 1–4 are the course's Exercises 6.6 A–D, pp.112–113; there are no answers in the course. Add $+c$ to every indefinite integral.)

  1. Exercise A. (i) $\int(3x-1)\,dx$; (ii) $\int(3t+4t^2)\,dt$; (iii) $\int(x+1)(x-2)\,dx$; (iv) $\int\frac{2(x+3)}{x^3}\,dx$.
  2. Exercise B. (i) $\int(2x-7)^3dx$; (ii) $\int\cos(7x+2)\,dx$; (iii) $\int\sec^2(3x+1)\,dx$; (iv) $\int(5x-4)^6dx$; (v) $\int\sin(3x+8)\,dx$; (vi) $\int\sec^24x\,dx$; (vii) $\int\frac{dx}{5x+4}$; (viii) $\int4^{3x}dx$.
  3. Exercise C. (i) $\int\frac{3x^2}{x^3-4}dx$; (ii) $\int\frac{2x+3}{x^2+3x-5}dx$; (iii) $\int\frac{2x+4}{x^2+4x-1}dx$; (iv) $\int\frac{x-3}{x^2-6x+2}dx$; (v) $\int\frac{\sec^2x}{\tan x}dx$; (vi) $\int\tan x\,dx$; (vii) $\int\frac{2x-3}{x^2+3-7}dx$ (read literally, $x^2-4$); (viii) $\int\frac{9x^2}{x^3-7}dx$.
  4. Exercise D (by parts). (i) $\int e^{3x}\sin x\,dx$; (ii) $\int x\ln x\,dx$; (iii) $\int e^{5x}\sin3x\,dx$; (iv) $\int x^3e^{2x}dx$.
  5. Integrate: (a) $\int xe^{x^2}dx$; (b) $\int\sin^3x\cos x\,dx$; (c) $\int\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+x^2}}dx$.
  6. By parts: (a) $\int\ln x\,dx$; (b) $\int\tan^{-1}x\,dx$.
  7. Partial fractions: (a) $\int\frac{dx}{x^2(x+1)}$; (b) $\int\frac{x+3}{(x-1)^2(x+2)}dx$.
  8. Improper fraction: $\int\frac{x^3+2}{x^2-1}\,dx$.
  9. (a) $\int\frac{dx}{x^2+4x+13}$; (b) $\int\frac{x+1}{x^2+1}dx$.
  10. Find $f$ if $f'(x)=6x^2-4x+1$ and $f(1)=3$.
Show answers

Answers

  1. (i) $\frac{3x^2}2-x$. (ii) $\frac{3t^2}2+\frac{4t^3}3$. (iii) expand to $x^2-x-2$: $\frac{x^3}3-\frac{x^2}2-2x$. (iv) $\frac{2x+6}{x^3}=2x^{-2}+6x^{-3}$: $-\frac2x-\frac3{x^2}$.
  2. (i) $\frac{(2x-7)^4}8$. (ii) $\frac{\sin(7x+2)}7$. (iii) $\frac{\tan(3x+1)}3$. (iv) $\frac{(5x-4)^7}{35}$. (v) $-\frac{\cos(3x+8)}3$. (vi) $\frac{\tan4x}4$. (vii) $\frac15\ln\lvert5x+4\rvert$. (viii) $\frac{4^{3x}}{3\ln4}$.
  3. (i) $\ln\lvert x^3-4\rvert$. (ii) $\ln\lvert x^2+3x-5\rvert$. (iii) $\ln\lvert x^2+4x-1\rvert$. (iv) $x-3=\frac12(2x-6)$: $\frac12\ln\lvert x^2-6x+2\rvert$. (v) $z=\tan x$: $\ln\lvert\tan x\rvert$. (vi) $-\ln\lvert\cos x\rvert$. (vii) $\frac{2x-3}{(x-2)(x+2)}=\frac{1/4}{x-2}+\frac{7/4}{x+2}$: $\frac14\ln\lvert x-2\rvert+\frac74\ln\lvert x+2\rvert$. (viii) $3\ln\lvert x^3-7\rvert$.
  4. (i) $\frac{e^{3x}(3\sin x-\cos x)}{10}$. (ii) $\frac{x^2}2\ln x-\frac{x^2}4$. (iii) $\frac{e^{5x}(5\sin3x-3\cos3x)}{34}$. (iv) $e^{2x}\big(\frac{x^3}2-\frac{3x^2}4+\frac{3x}4-\frac38\big)$.
  5. (a) $z=x^2$: $\frac12e^{x^2}$. (b) $z=\sin x$: $\frac{\sin^4x}4$. (c) $z=1+x^2$, $dz=2x\,dx$: $\sqrt{1+x^2}$.
  6. (a) $x\ln x-x$. (b) $u=\tan^{-1}x$, $dv=dx$: $x\tan^{-1}x-\int\frac x{1+x^2}dx=x\tan^{-1}x-\frac12\ln(1+x^2)$.
  7. (a) $\frac1{x^2(x+1)}=\frac1{x^2}-\frac1x+\frac1{x+1}$: $-\frac1x-\ln\lvert x\rvert+\ln\lvert x+1\rvert$. (b) $\frac{x+3}{(x-1)^2(x+2)}=\frac{4/3}{(x-1)^2}-\frac{1/9}{x-1}+\frac{1/9}{x+2}$: $-\frac4{3(x-1)}-\frac19\ln\lvert x-1\rvert+\frac19\ln\lvert x+2\rvert$.
  8. $\frac{x^2}2+\frac32\ln\lvert x-1\rvert-\frac12\ln\lvert x+1\rvert$ (Example 9b).
  9. (a) $\frac13\tan^{-1}\frac{x+2}3$. (b) $\frac12\ln(x^2+1)+\tan^{-1}x$.
  10. $f=2x^3-2x^2+x+c$; $f(1)=1+c=3$, $c=2$, so $f(x)=2x^3-2x^2+x+2$.

All results above (course examples, exercises and new problems) were checked with sympy 1.14 by differentiating each answer.

Extra practice (from outside sources)

From outside sources — not in the NOUN course text Copied from OpenStax Calculus Volume 1 (CC BY 4.0); each problem names its section and exercise number.

Not from the NOUN course text. These problems come from the free OpenStax Calculus textbooks (CC BY 4.0). Tap Source ↗ to open the original page. Answers are worked out and checked here.

  1. Evaluate the integral $\displaystyle\int\frac{14x^3+2x+1}{x^3}\,dx$. Source ↗
  2. Use a suitable change of variables to determine the indefinite integral $\displaystyle\int(11x-7)^{-3}\,dx$. Source ↗
  3. Use the method of partial fractions to evaluate $\displaystyle\int\frac{x}{x^2-4}\,dx$. Source ↗
  4. Use a suitable change of variables to determine the indefinite integral $\displaystyle\int\frac{y^5}{(1-y^3)^{3/2}}\,dy$. Source ↗
  5. Find the integral by using the simplest method: $\displaystyle\int x^3\sin x\,dx$. Source ↗
  6. Find the integral by using the simplest method: $\displaystyle\int e^x\sin x\,dx$. Source ↗
  7. Use the method of partial fractions to evaluate $\displaystyle\int\frac{dx}{x^3-2x^2-4x+8}$. Source ↗
  8. Evaluate the integral $\displaystyle\int\frac{x^3+6x^2+3x+6}{x^3+2x^2}\,dx$. (The book lists it under "Evaluate the following integrals, which have irreducible quadratic factors"; this denominator has only linear factors, one of them repeated.) Source ↗
Show answers (extra practice)

Answers (extra practice)

  1. Split the fraction into powers of $x$: $\dfrac{14x^3+2x+1}{x^3}=14+2x^{-2}+x^{-3}$, so $\displaystyle\int=14x-\frac2x-\frac1{2x^2}+c$. OpenStax's answer key agrees.
  2. Linear substitution: $z=11x-7$, $dz=11\,dx$, so $\displaystyle\int(11x-7)^{-3}dx=\frac1{11}\int z^{-3}dz=-\frac1{22z^2}=-\frac1{22(11x-7)^2}+c$. OpenStax's key agrees.
  3. The numerator is half the derivative of the denominator ($f'/f$ form, $f=x^2-4$): $\displaystyle\int\frac{x}{x^2-4}dx=\frac12\ln\lvert x^2-4\rvert+c$. Via partial fractions: $\dfrac x{(x-2)(x+2)}=\dfrac{1/2}{x-2}+\dfrac{1/2}{x+2}$, which integrates to $\frac12\ln\lvert x-2\rvert+\frac12\ln\lvert x+2\rvert=\frac12\ln\lvert x^2-4\rvert$. OpenStax's key gives $\frac12\ln\lvert4-x^2\rvert+c$, the same up to the constant.
  4. Let $z=1-y^3$, so $dz=-3y^2dy$ and $y^5dy=y^3\cdot y^2dy=(1-z)\left(-\tfrac13dz\right)$. Then
$$\int\frac{y^5}{(1-y^3)^{3/2}}dy=-\frac13\int(1-z)z^{-3/2}dz=-\frac13\int\big(z^{-3/2}-z^{-1/2}\big)dz=-\frac13\big(-2z^{-1/2}-2z^{1/2}\big)=\frac{2(1+z)}{3\sqrt z}.$$

Back-substituting $z=1-y^3$: $\dfrac{2(2-y^3)}{3\sqrt{1-y^3}}+c$. OpenStax's key, $-\dfrac{2(y^3-2)}{3\sqrt{1-y^3}}+c$, is the same function. 5. By parts three times, differentiating the power each time ($u=x^3$, then $x^2$, then $x$): $\displaystyle\int x^3\sin x\,dx=-x^3\cos x+3\int x^2\cos x\,dx$; $\displaystyle\int x^2\cos x\,dx=x^2\sin x-2\int x\sin x\,dx$; $\displaystyle\int x\sin x\,dx=-x\cos x+\sin x$. Assembling:

$$\int x^3\sin x\,dx=-x^3\cos x+3x^2\sin x+6x\cos x-6\sin x+c.$$

OpenStax's key, $-x(x^2-6)\cos x+3(x^2-2)\sin x+c$, is the same. 6. Cyclic case. Let $I=\int e^x\sin x\,dx$. By parts ($u=\sin x$, $dv=e^xdx$): $I=e^x\sin x-\int e^x\cos x\,dx$, and again ($u=\cos x$): $\int e^x\cos x\,dx=e^x\cos x+\int e^x\sin x\,dx=e^x\cos x+I$. So $I=e^x\sin x-e^x\cos x-I$, i.e. $2I=e^x(\sin x-\cos x)$:

$$\int e^x\sin x\,dx=\tfrac12e^x(\sin x-\cos x)+c.$$

OpenStax's key, $\frac12e^x(-\cos x+\sin x)+c$, agrees. 7. Factor: $x^3-2x^2-4x+8=x^2(x-2)-4(x-2)=(x-2)(x^2-4)=(x-2)^2(x+2)$, a repeated linear factor. Write $\dfrac1{(x-2)^2(x+2)}=\dfrac A{x-2}+\dfrac B{(x-2)^2}+\dfrac C{x+2}$, so $1=A(x-2)(x+2)+B(x+2)+C(x-2)^2$. Put $x=2$: $1=4B$, $B=\frac14$. Put $x=-2$: $1=16C$, $C=\frac1{16}$. Coefficient of $x^2$: $0=A+C$, so $A=-\frac1{16}$. Integrating ($\int(x-2)^{-2}dx=-(x-2)^{-1}$):

$$\int\frac{dx}{x^3-2x^2-4x+8}=-\frac1{16}\ln\lvert x-2\rvert-\frac1{4(x-2)}+\frac1{16}\ln\lvert x+2\rvert+c.$$

OpenStax's key, $\frac1{16}\left(-\frac4{x-2}-\ln\lvert x-2\rvert+\ln\lvert x+2\rvert\right)+c$, agrees. 8. The numerator and denominator both have degree 3, so divide first: $x^3+6x^2+3x+6=1\cdot(x^3+2x^2)+(4x^2+3x+6)$. The denominator is $x^2(x+2)$ (repeated factor $x$), so $\dfrac{4x^2+3x+6}{x^2(x+2)}=\dfrac Ax+\dfrac B{x^2}+\dfrac C{x+2}$ and $4x^2+3x+6=Ax(x+2)+B(x+2)+Cx^2$. Put $x=0$: $6=2B$, $B=3$. Put $x=-2$: $16=4C$, $C=4$. Coefficient of $x^2$: $4=A+C$, so $A=0$. Hence the integrand is $1+\dfrac3{x^2}+\dfrac4{x+2}$ and

$$\int\frac{x^3+6x^2+3x+6}{x^3+2x^2}dx=x-\frac3x+4\ln\lvert x+2\rvert+c.$$

OpenStax's key agrees.

Every answer above was checked with sympy (differentiate the antiderivative and compare with the integrand); the odd-numbered exercises (497, 273, 283, 33, 19, 199, 205, 209) also agree with the OpenStax answer keys.

Unit 7 of 8

Unit 7 — Definite integrals: FTC, evaluation, substitution with limits, area

The course text is thin here. Read this first. The contents page (PDF p.9) lists "Module Three, Unit 1: Definite integrals (Application to areas under curve and Volumes of solids)", but the course has no such unit. Pages 97–114 (Unit 6) are indefinite integrals only. The only place where definite integrals appear is Module 8, "Volume of solids of revolution by definite integral", PDF pp.115–122: it writes $\pi\int_a^b y^2\,\delta x$ and evaluates it with the bracket notation $\big[\ \big]_a^b$, but it never defines the definite integral, never states the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, never treats substitution with limits and never discusses area under a curve or between curves (the "areas under curve" promised on p.9 is missing). Everything in the sections below on the definition, the Fundamental Theorem, substitution with limits, and area is therefore standard first-year calculus, written by me, not taken from the course. Where the course does say something, it is cited as "Course p.N" (PDF page).

Page convention. The course PDF has no printed page numbers, so "p.N" is the PDF page number. The course's definite-integral material is pp.115–122 only (Module 8). The course writes $\delta x$ for $dx$; this chapter uses $dx$.

Likely to be examined. The course gives no objectives for definite integrals as such. Its own questions that use them are the Module 8 worked examples (pp.117–120) and 8.7 TMA, pp.120–121 (volumes of revolution: Unit 8). The techniques of Unit 6 are what you need to evaluate the integrals. Nothing here claims what the exam contains.

What you must be able to do

  • Explain a definite integral as a limit of sums of thin slices (area under a curve) and state its basic properties.
  • State the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus (both parts) with its hypotheses, and use it to evaluate $\int_a^b f(x)\,dx$ and to differentiate $\int_a^{g(x)}f(t)\,dt$.
  • Evaluate definite integrals using every Unit 6 technique: powers, substitution with the limits changed, parts with the bracket term evaluated at the limits, partial fractions, $f'/f$.
  • Use symmetry (even/odd functions) to shorten a calculation.
  • Find the area between a curve and the $x$-axis (taking care of signs: signed integral versus total area) and the area between two curves (top minus bottom, find the intersection points first).
  • Recognise when the Fundamental Theorem does not apply (the integrand is not continuous on $[a,b]$).
  • Set up and evaluate $V=\pi\int_a^b y^2\,dx$ (the link to Unit 8).

Key ideas

1. What a definite integral is

Intuition. To find the area under $y=f(x)$ from $x=a$ to $x=b$, cut $[a,b]$ into $n$ thin strips of width $\Delta x=\frac{b-a}n$, treat each strip as a rectangle of height $f(x_i^*)$ and add up the areas. Making the strips thinner gives better and better approximations. The limit is the definite integral:

$$\int_a^b f(x)\,dx=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^nf(x_i^*)\,\Delta x .$$

It is a number (not a function of $x$; the letter $x$ is a dummy variable: $\int_a^bf(x)\,dx=\int_a^bf(t)\,dt$). The sign $\int$ is a stretched "S" for "sum". If $f$ is continuous on $[a,b]$, the limit exists. Where $f\ge0$ the number is the area under the graph; where $f<0$ the contribution is negative (the signed area). The course's volume formula (p.116) is the same idea, slicing a solid into thin discs, each of volume $\pi y^2\,dx$.

Properties (for $f,g$ continuous on the intervals involved): - Linearity: $\int_a^b(\alpha f+\beta g)\,dx=\alpha\int_a^bf\,dx+\beta\int_a^bg\,dx$. - Reversing limits: $\int_b^af\,dx=-\int_a^bf\,dx$; and $\int_a^af\,dx=0$. - Splitting the interval: $\int_a^bf\,dx=\int_a^cf\,dx+\int_c^bf\,dx$ for any $c$ (inside or outside $[a,b]$). - Comparison: if $f\le g$ on $[a,b]$ ($a<b$) then $\int_a^bf\,dx\le\int_a^bg\,dx$; and $\big|\int_a^bf\,dx\big|\le\int_a^b|f|\,dx$. - Symmetry: if $f$ is even, $\int_{-a}^af\,dx=2\int_0^af\,dx$; if $f$ is odd, $\int_{-a}^af\,dx=0$. - Mean value: for $a<b$, the average value of $f$ on $[a,b]$ is $\dfrac1{b-a}\displaystyle\int_a^bf(x)\,dx$.

2. The Fundamental Theorem of Calculus (FTC)

Part 1 (differentiating an integral). If $f$ is continuous on $[a,b]$ and $F(x)=\int_a^xf(t)\,dt$, then $F$ is differentiable on $(a,b)$ and $F'(x)=f(x)$ there (one-sided derivatives at $a$ and $b$). With a variable upper limit use the chain rule:

$$\frac d{dx}\int_a^{g(x)}f(t)\,dt=f(g(x))\,g'(x).$$

Part 2 (evaluating). If $f$ is continuous on $[a,b]$ and $F$ is any antiderivative of $f$ on $[a,b]$ ($F'=f$), then

$$\int_a^bf(x)\,dx=F(b)-F(a)=\Big[F(x)\Big]_a^b .$$

No constant $+c$ is needed: it cancels in $F(b)-F(a)$. This is why the course's Unit 6 (finding $F$) matters: every Unit 6 technique evaluates a definite integral once you plug in the limits. (This is exactly what the course does on pp.117–120 without naming the theorem.)

The continuity hypothesis is real. $\int_{-1}^1\frac{dx}{x^2}$ is not $\big[-\frac1x\big]_{-1}^1=-2$: the integrand is not defined at $0$ and is unbounded near it; the integral diverges (it is an improper integral). A negative answer for the integral of a positive function is the warning sign.

3. Substitution with limits

If $z=g(x)$ with $g'$ continuous on $[a,b]$ and $f$ continuous on the range of $g$:

$$\int_a^bf(g(x))\,g'(x)\,dx=\int_{g(a)}^{g(b)}f(z)\,dz .$$

Change the limits together with the variable and then do not return to $x$. (The alternative is to find the indefinite integral, go back to $x$, and then use the original limits. Never mix the two: new variable with old limits is wrong.)

4. Parts with limits

$$\int_a^bu\,dv=\Big[uv\Big]_a^b-\int_a^bv\,du .$$

The bracket term is evaluated at the limits straight away (here you do not drop it).

5. Area

  • Area under $y=f(x)$ above the $x$-axis, $a\le x\le b$, $f\ge0$: $A=\int_a^bf(x)\,dx$.
  • If $f$ changes sign, $\int_a^bf\,dx$ is the signed area (positive parts minus negative parts). The total (geometric) area is $\int_a^b|f(x)|\,dx$: find where $f=0$, split the interval there and take absolute values of each piece.
  • Between two curves $y=f(x)$ (top) and $y=g(x)$ (bottom) for $a\le x\le b$: $A=\int_a^b\big(f(x)-g(x)\big)\,dx$. Method: (i) sketch; (ii) solve $f(x)=g(x)$ for the intersection points, which give the limits (when no limits are given); (iii) decide which curve is on top between those points (test a point); (iv) integrate top minus bottom; if the curves cross inside the range, split there. Sometimes it is easier to integrate in $y$ with horizontal strips: $A=\int_c^d\big(x_{\text{right}}-x_{\text{left}}\big)\,dy$.

6. Volume of a solid of revolution (the link to Unit 8)

Rotating the region under $y=f(x)$, $a\le x\le b$, about the $x$-axis gives discs of radius $y$ and thickness $dx$:

$$V=\pi\int_a^b\big[f(x)\big]^2\,dx\ \ (\text{course p.116}),\qquad\text{about the }y\text{-axis (curve }x=g(y),\ c\le y\le d):\ V=\pi\int_c^d\big[g(y)\big]^2\,dy .$$

The limits are the $x$-values (or $y$-values) where the region starts and stops. See Unit 8 for the full treatment.

Formula sheet

$$\int_a^bf\,dx=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{i=1}^nf(x_i^*)\Delta x,$$
$$\Delta x=\frac{b-a}n;\qquad \sum_{i=1}^ni=\frac{n(n+1)}2,$$
$$\sum_{i=1}^ni^2=\frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}6 .$$
$$\textbf{FTC 1: }\ \frac d{dx}\int_a^xf(t)\,dt=f(x);$$
$$\frac d{dx}\int_a^{g(x)}f(t)\,dt=f(g(x))g'(x).$$
$$\textbf{FTC 2: }\ \int_a^bf\,dx=F(b)-F(a).$$
$$\int_a^bf\,dx=-\int_b^af\,dx,$$
$$\int_a^bf\,dx=\int_a^cf\,dx+\int_c^bf\,dx,$$
$$\int_{-a}^af\,dx=\begin{cases}2\int_0^af\,dx&f\text{ even}\\0&f\text{ odd}\end{cases}$$
$$\int_a^bf(g(x))g'(x)\,dx=\int_{g(a)}^{g(b)}f(z)\,dz,$$
$$\int_a^bu\,dv=\Big[uv\Big]_a^b-\int_a^bv\,du .$$
$$\text{Average value}=\frac1{b-a}\int_a^bf\,dx;$$
$$\text{Area between curves}=\int_a^b(f_{\text{top}}-f_{\text{bottom}})\,dx;$$
$$\text{Total area}=\int_a^b|f|\,dx .$$
$$\text{Volume about the }x\text{-axis: }V=\pi\int_a^by^2\,dx .$$

Worked examples

All numerical results were checked with sympy.

Example 1 — A definite integral as a limit of sums

Find $\int_0^1x^2\,dx$ from the definition (right-hand end points).

Solution. $\Delta x=\frac1n$, $x_i=\frac in$, so

$$\sum_{i=1}^n\Big(\frac in\Big)^2\frac1n=\frac1{n^3}\sum_{i=1}^ni^2=\frac1{n^3}\cdot\frac{n(n+1)(2n+1)}6=\frac{(n+1)(2n+1)}{6n^2}\ \xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\ \frac26=\frac13 .$$

Check with FTC: $\big[\frac{x^3}3\big]_0^1=\frac13$ ✓.

Example 2 — Evaluating with the FTC

(a) $\int_1^3(3x^2-2x+1)\,dx$. An antiderivative is $x^3-x^2+x$ (no $+c$). $F(3)=27-9+3=21$, $F(1)=1-1+1=1$, so the value is $21-1=\mathbf{20}$. (b) $\int_0^\pi\sin x\,dx=\big[-\cos x\big]_0^\pi=1-(-1)=\mathbf2$. But $\int_0^\pi\cos x\,dx=\big[\sin x\big]_0^\pi=0$ (signed: the positive and negative halves cancel), while $\int_0^\pi|\cos x|\,dx=2$. (c) Symmetry. $\int_{-2}^2(x^3+x^2)\,dx$: $x^3$ is odd (contributes $0$), $x^2$ is even: $2\int_0^2x^2dx=2\cdot\frac83=\frac{\mathbf{16}}{\mathbf3}$.

Example 3 — The FTC, Part 1 (derivative of an integral)

(a) $\frac d{dx}\int_0^x\sqrt{1+t^4}\,dt=\sqrt{1+x^4}$ (the integrand is continuous). No need to evaluate the integral. (b) $\frac d{dx}\int_1^{x^2}\ln t\,dt$ ($x\ne0$): here $g(x)=x^2$, so the answer is $\ln(x^2)\cdot2x=\mathbf{2x\ln(x^2)}$ ($=4x\ln|x|$).

Example 4 — Substitution with the limits changed

(a) $\int_0^1 2x(x^2+1)^3\,dx$. Let $z=x^2+1$, $dz=2x\,dx$. When $x=0$, $z=1$; when $x=1$, $z=2$. So

$$\int_1^2z^3\,dz=\Big[\frac{z^4}4\Big]_1^2=\frac{16-1}4=\frac{\mathbf{15}}{\mathbf4}.$$

(b) $\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin x\cos^2x\,dx$. $z=\cos x$, $dz=-\sin x\,dx$; $x=0\Rightarrow z=1$, $x=\frac\pi2\Rightarrow z=0$:

$$\int_1^0-z^2\,dz=\int_0^1z^2\,dz=\frac{\mathbf1}{\mathbf3}.$$

(c) $\int_0^4\dfrac{dx}{1+\sqrt x}$. Let $z=\sqrt x$, $x=z^2$, $dx=2z\,dz$; $x:0\to4$ gives $z:0\to2$:

$$\int_0^2\frac{2z}{1+z}dz=2\int_0^2\Big(1-\frac1{1+z}\Big)dz=2\Big[z-\ln(1+z)\Big]_0^2=\mathbf{4-2\ln3}.$$

Mistake to avoid: in (a), writing $\int_0^1z^3\,dz$ (new variable, old limits) gives $\frac14$, wrong.

Example 5 — Parts and partial fractions with limits (uses Unit 6)

(a) $\int_0^1xe^x\,dx$: $u=x$, $dv=e^xdx$, $v=e^x$: $\big[xe^x\big]_0^1-\int_0^1e^xdx=e-(e-1)=\mathbf1$. (b) $\int_1^e\ln x\,dx=\big[x\ln x-x\big]_1^e=(e-e)-(0-1)=\mathbf1$. (c) $\int_1^4\dfrac{dx}{x(x+1)}$: $\frac1{x(x+1)}=\frac1x-\frac1{x+1}$, so $\big[\ln x-\ln(x+1)\big]_1^4=\ln\frac45-\ln\frac12=\ln\frac{8}{5}$. (On $[1,4]$ everything is positive, so no absolute values are needed.) (d) $\int_0^2\frac{x}{x^2+1}\,dx=\big[\frac12\ln(x^2+1)\big]_0^2=\frac12\ln5$ (the $f'/f$ form).

Example 6 — Area under a curve: signed versus total

Find the area between $y=x^2-4x+3=(x-1)(x-3)$ and the $x$-axis for $0\le x\le3$.

Solution. The curve is above the axis on $[0,1)$ and below it on $(1,3)$. $F(x)=\frac{x^3}3-2x^2+3x$. Signed: $\int_0^3=F(3)-F(0)=9-18+9=0$. This is not the area. Total area: $\int_0^1=F(1)-F(0)=\frac13-2+3=\frac43$; $\int_1^3=F(3)-F(1)=0-\frac43=-\frac43$, taken as $\frac43$. Total area $=\frac43+\frac43=\frac{\mathbf8}{\mathbf3}$.

Example 7 — Area between two curves

(a) Between $y=x+2$ and $y=x^2$. Intersections: $x+2=x^2\Rightarrow x^2-x-2=0\Rightarrow x=-1,2$. At $x=0$, $x+2=2>0=x^2$, so the line is on top.

$$A=\int_{-1}^2(x+2-x^2)\,dx=\Big[\frac{x^2}2+2x-\frac{x^3}3\Big]_{-1}^2=\Big(2+4-\frac83\Big)-\Big(\frac12-2+\frac13\Big)=\frac{10}3+\frac76=\frac{\mathbf9}{\mathbf2}.$$

(b) Between $y=x$ and $y=x^2$: intersections $x=0,1$; line on top: $\int_0^1(x-x^2)\,dx=\frac12-\frac13=\frac{\mathbf1}{\mathbf6}$. (c) Horizontal strips. Between $y^2=x$ and $y=x-2$: eliminate $x$: $x=y^2$ and $x=y+2$ give $y^2=y+2$, $y=-1,2$. For $-1<y<2$, $y+2>y^2$ (line on the right):

$$A=\int_{-1}^2(y+2-y^2)\,dy=\frac{\mathbf9}{\mathbf2}\quad(\text{one integral; with }dx\text{ it would need two}).$$

Example 8 — Average value

Average value of $f(x)=x^2$ on $[0,3]$: $\frac13\int_0^3x^2dx=\frac13\cdot9=\mathbf3$. (This is the height of the rectangle on $[0,3]$ with the same area as the region under the parabola.)

Example 9 — Volumes by discs (link to Unit 8; checks the course's own results)

(a) Cone (Course Example 1, p.119): the line $y=\frac rhx$, $0\le x\le h$, rotated about the $x$-axis: $V=\pi\int_0^h\frac{r^2}{h^2}x^2dx=\frac{\pi r^2}{h^2}\cdot\frac{h^3}3=\frac13\pi r^2h$ ✓. (b) Sphere (Course p.117): $y=\sqrt{r^2-x^2}$, $-r\le x\le r$: $V=\pi\int_{-r}^r(r^2-x^2)\,dx=\pi\Big[r^2x-\frac{x^3}3\Big]_{-r}^r=\pi\Big(\frac{2r^3}3+\frac{2r^3}3\Big)=\frac43\pi r^3$ ✓. (c) Course Example 2, p.120: $y=-x^2+3x$ meets the $x$-axis at $x=0,3$. $V=\pi\int_0^3(x^4-6x^3+9x^2)\,dx=\pi\big[\frac{x^5}5-\frac{3x^4}2+3x^3\big]_0^3=\pi\big(\frac{243}5-\frac{243}2+81\big)=\frac{81\pi}{10}$ ✓ (the course's value is right). (d) TMA 1, p.120 ($y=x^2$, $0\le x\le2$, about the $x$-axis): $\pi\int_0^2x^4dx=\pi\frac{32}5=\frac{32\pi}5$. (Unit 8 treats the other TMA questions.)

Common mistakes

  • Keeping $+c$ in a definite integral, or forgetting that it cancels. Write the bracket $\big[F\big]_a^b$ and plug in the limits.
  • Substituting but leaving the old limits ($x$-limits with a $z$-integral), or changing variable and then returning to $x$ but still using the $z$-limits.
  • Evaluating $F(a)-F(b)$ (order wrong). It is upper minus lower. Swapping limits flips the sign.
  • Treating the signed integral as the area. If the curve crosses the axis, split and take absolute values. A negative "area" means a region below the axis (or limits in the wrong order).
  • Between two curves: subtracting bottom minus top; not checking which curve is on top; using limits that are not the intersection points; not splitting where the curves cross.
  • Using FTC on a discontinuous integrand ($\int_{-1}^1\frac{dx}{x^2}$, $\int_0^{\pi}\sec^2x\,dx$ passes through $x=\frac\pi2$ where $\tan x$ blows up).
  • Dropping the bracket term in parts ($[uv]_a^b$) or evaluating it only at the upper limit.
  • Squaring wrongly in volumes: $\pi\int y^2dx$ means $\pi\int[f(x)]^2dx$, so $(-x^2+3x)^2\ne x^4+9x^2$; expand fully (the cross term is $-6x^3$).
  • Wrong integral notation: the integral of a sum is not the product of integrals; $\int_a^b f g\,dx\ne\int_a^bf\,dx\cdot\int_a^bg\,dx$.
  • Forgetting the $\pi$ in volume formulas, or using $y$ instead of $y^2$.

Practice problems

  1. From the definition (right-hand sums), compute $\int_0^2(x+1)\,dx$. Check with the FTC.
  2. Evaluate (a) $\int_0^2(x^3-2x+1)\,dx$; (b) $\int_1^4\Big(\frac2{\sqrt x}+x\Big)dx$.
  3. Find $\dfrac d{dx}\displaystyle\int_0^{x^2}e^{-t^2}\,dt$.
  4. Evaluate by substitution (change the limits): (a) $\int_0^{\pi/4}\tan x\sec^2x\,dx$; (b) $\int_1^e\frac{\ln x}x\,dx$; (c) $\int_0^4x\sqrt{x^2+9}\,dx$; (d) $\int_0^2x^2\sqrt{1+x^3}\,dx$.
  5. Evaluate by parts: (a) $\int_0^1x^2e^x\,dx$; (b) $\int_1^2x\ln x\,dx$.
  6. (a) $\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}x^3\cos x\,dx$ (use symmetry); (b) $\int_{-1}^3|x-1|\,dx$.
  7. Find the area enclosed by $y=x^2$ and $y=8-x^2$.
  8. Find the area between $y=\sin x$ and $y=\cos x$ for $0\le x\le\frac\pi2$ (they cross inside the interval).
  9. (a) Area under $y=\dfrac1{x^2+1}$ from $x=0$ to $x=1$. (b) $\int_0^{\ln3}\dfrac{e^x}{1+e^x}\,dx$.
  10. Find the volume formed by rotating about the $x$-axis (a) $y=\sqrt x$, $0\le x\le4$; (b) $y=2x$, $0\le x\le3$. What solid is (b)?
  11. Explain why $\int_{-1}^1\frac{dx}{x^2}=\big[-\frac1x\big]_{-1}^1=-2$ is wrong. What is the correct statement?
  12. Find the average value of $f(x)=x^2$ on $[0,3]$ and the point(s) $c$ in $[0,3]$ where $f(c)$ equals it.
Show answers

Answers

  1. $\Delta x=\frac2n$, $x_i=\frac{2i}n$: $\frac2n\sum\big(1+\frac{2i}n\big)=2+\frac4{n^2}\cdot\frac{n(n+1)}2=2+\frac{2(n+1)}n\to4$. FTC: $\big[\frac{x^2}2+x\big]_0^2=2+2=4$ ✓.
  2. (a) $\big[\frac{x^4}4-x^2+x\big]_0^2=4-4+2=\mathbf2$. (b) $\big[4\sqrt x+\frac{x^2}2\big]_1^4=(8+8)-(4+\frac12)=\frac{\mathbf{23}}{\mathbf2}$.
  3. FTC 1 with $g(x)=x^2$: $e^{-(x^2)^2}\cdot2x=\mathbf{2x\,e^{-x^4}}$.
  4. (a) $z=\tan x$: $\int_0^1z\,dz=\frac12$. (b) $z=\ln x$: $\int_0^1z\,dz=\frac12$. (c) $z=x^2+9$, $x\,dx=\frac12dz$: $\frac12\int_9^{25}\sqrt z\,dz=\frac13\big(125-27\big)=\frac{\mathbf{98}}{\mathbf3}$. (d) $z=1+x^3$, $x^2dx=\frac13dz$: $\frac13\int_1^9\sqrt z\,dz=\frac29(27-1)=\frac{\mathbf{52}}{\mathbf9}$.
  5. (a) $\big[x^2e^x\big]_0^1-2\int_0^1xe^x=e-2\cdot1=\mathbf{e-2}$ (using Example 5a). (b) $\big[\frac{x^2}2\ln x\big]_1^2-\int_1^2\frac x2dx=2\ln2-\frac34=\mathbf{2\ln2-\frac34}$.
  6. (a) $x^3\cos x$ is odd (odd times even), so $\mathbf0$. (b) $\int_{-1}^1(1-x)\,dx+\int_1^3(x-1)\,dx=2+2=\mathbf4$.
  7. Intersections: $x^2=8-x^2$, $x=\pm2$; top $8-x^2$: $\int_{-2}^2(8-2x^2)\,dx=2\big(16-\frac{16}3\big)=\frac{\mathbf{64}}{\mathbf3}$.
  8. They cross at $x=\frac\pi4$. $\int_0^{\pi/4}(\cos x-\sin x)\,dx+\int_{\pi/4}^{\pi/2}(\sin x-\cos x)\,dx=(\sqrt2-1)+(\sqrt2-1)=\mathbf{2\sqrt2-2}$.
  9. (a) $\big[\tan^{-1}x\big]_0^1=\frac{\boldsymbol\pi}{\mathbf4}$. (b) $f'/f$ form: $\big[\ln(1+e^x)\big]_0^{\ln3}=\ln4-\ln2=\mathbf{\ln2}$.
  10. (a) $\pi\int_0^4x\,dx=\mathbf{8\pi}$. (b) $\pi\int_0^34x^2dx=36\pi$; it is a right circular cone of height $3$ and base radius $6$, with $V=\frac13\pi\cdot36\cdot3=\mathbf{36\pi}$ ✓.
  11. The integrand $\frac1{x^2}$ is positive, so the integral cannot be negative; it is undefined at $0$ and unbounded near it, so the continuity hypothesis of the FTC fails on $[-1,1]$. The correct statement: the integral diverges (it is $+\infty$; sympy returns $\infty$). The same antiderivative is fine on an interval not containing $0$, e.g. $\int_1^2\frac{dx}{x^2}=\frac12$.
  12. $\frac13\int_0^3x^2dx=\frac13\cdot9=\mathbf3$. $c^2=3$, $c=\sqrt3\approx1.732\in[0,3]$.

All numerical results in the examples and answers were checked with sympy 1.14.

Extra practice (from outside sources)

From outside sources — not in the NOUN course text Copied from OpenStax Calculus Volume 1 (CC BY 4.0); each problem names its section and exercise number.

Not from the NOUN course text. These problems come from the free OpenStax Calculus textbooks (CC BY 4.0). Tap Source ↗ to open the original page. Answers are worked out and checked here.

  1. Given $R_n$ as indicated, express its limit as $n\to\infty$ as a definite integral, identifying the correct interval: $R_n=\dfrac1n\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac in$. Source ↗
  2. Evaluate the definite integral using the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, Part 2: $\displaystyle\int_{-2}^{3}(x^2+3x-5)\,dx$. Source ↗
  3. Use the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, Part 1, to find the derivative: $\dfrac{d}{dx}\displaystyle\int_{\cos x}^{1}\sqrt{1-t^2}\,dt$. Source ↗
  4. Use a change of variables to evaluate the definite integral $\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi/4}\frac{\sin\theta}{\cos^4\theta}\,d\theta$. Source ↗
  5. Is the substitution $u=1-x^2$ in the definite integral $\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2}\frac{x}{1-x^2}\,dx$ okay? If not, why not? Source ↗
  6. Compute the definite integral (express the answer in exact form): $\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi/2}x^2\sin x\,dx$. Source ↗
  7. Graph the equations and shade the area of the region between the curves. If necessary, break the region into sub-regions to determine its entire area: $y=x^3$ and $y=x^2-2x$ over $x=[-1,1]$. Source ↗
  8. Find the average value $f_{\text{ave}}$ of $f$ between $a$ and $b$, and find a point $c$ where $f(c)=f_{\text{ave}}$: $f(x)=3-\lvert x\rvert$, $a=-3$, $b=3$. Source ↗
Show answers (extra practice)

Answers (extra practice)

  1. $R_n$ is a right-hand sum with $\Delta x=\frac1n$ and sample points $x_i=\frac in$, so $f(x)=x$ on $[0,1]$: $\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}R_n=\int_0^1x\,dx=\frac12$. Check directly: $\sum_{i=1}^n i=\frac{n(n+1)}2$, so $R_n=\frac{n+1}{2n}\to\frac12$. (The reading $\int_0^1x\,dx$ is the book's; any shift such as $\int_1^2(x-1)\,dx$ has the same sum.)
  2. $F(x)=\frac{x^3}3+\frac{3x^2}2-5x$. $F(3)=9+\frac{27}2-15=\frac{15}2$ and $F(-2)=-\frac83+6+10=\frac{40}3$. So $\displaystyle\int_{-2}^3=\frac{15}2-\frac{40}3=-\frac{35}6$. OpenStax's key agrees.
  3. Flip the limits so the variable limit is on top: $\displaystyle\int_{\cos x}^1=-\int_1^{\cos x}$. By FTC Part 1 with the chain rule,
$$\frac d{dx}\left(-\int_1^{\cos x}\sqrt{1-t^2}\,dt\right)=-\sqrt{1-\cos^2x}\cdot(-\sin x)=\sqrt{\sin^2x}\,\sin x=\lvert\sin x\rvert\sin x.$$

(Careful: $\sqrt{1-\cos^2x}=\lvert\sin x\rvert$, not $\sin x$.) OpenStax's key, $\lvert\sin x\rvert\sin x$, agrees. 4. Let $u=\cos\theta$, $du=-\sin\theta\,d\theta$. Limits: $\theta=0\Rightarrow u=1$; $\theta=\frac\pi4\Rightarrow u=\frac1{\sqrt2}$. Then

$$\int_0^{\pi/4}\frac{\sin\theta}{\cos^4\theta}d\theta=-\int_1^{1/\sqrt2}u^{-4}du=\int_{1/\sqrt2}^{1}u^{-4}du=\Big[-\frac1{3u^3}\Big]_{1/\sqrt2}^{1}=-\frac13+\frac{(\sqrt2)^3}3=\frac{2\sqrt2-1}3.$$

OpenStax's key agrees. 5. No. The integrand $\frac x{1-x^2}$ is not defined at $x=1$, which lies inside $[0,2]$, so it is not continuous on the interval and the Fundamental Theorem and the substitution rule do not apply. Formally, $u$ would run from $1$ to $-3$ through $0$, where $\ln\lvert u\rvert$ is undefined, and the "answer" $\big[-\frac12\ln\lvert1-x^2\rvert\big]_0^2=-\frac12\ln3$ is meaningless. In fact the integral is improper and diverges: $\int_0^{1-\varepsilon}\frac x{1-x^2}dx=-\frac12\ln\big(1-(1-\varepsilon)^2\big)\to+\infty$ as $\varepsilon\to0^+$. OpenStax's key: "No, because the integrand is discontinuous at $x=1$." 6. By parts twice: $\displaystyle\int x^2\sin x\,dx=-x^2\cos x+2\int x\cos x\,dx$ and $\int x\cos x\,dx=x\sin x-\int\sin x\,dx=x\sin x+\cos x$. So an antiderivative is $-x^2\cos x+2x\sin x+2\cos x$. Evaluate: at $\frac\pi2$: $0+\pi+0=\pi$; at $0$: $0+0+2=2$. Result: $\pi-2$. OpenStax's key agrees. 7. Intersections: $x^3=x^2-2x\iff x(x^2-x+2)=0$, and $x^2-x+2$ has discriminant $-7<0$, so the only crossing is $x=0$. On $[0,1]$, $x^3-(x^2-2x)=x(x^2-x+2)\ge0$ (cubic on top); on $[-1,0]$ it is $\le0$ (parabola on top). Area (top minus bottom on each piece):

$$\int_{-1}^{0}(x^2-2x-x^3)\,dx+\int_0^1(x^3-x^2+2x)\,dx=\Big[\tfrac{x^3}3-x^2-\tfrac{x^4}4\Big]_{-1}^0+\Big[\tfrac{x^4}4-\tfrac{x^3}3+x^2\Big]_0^1=\frac{19}{12}+\frac{11}{12}=\frac52.$$

A single integral $\int_{-1}^1(x^3-x^2+2x)\,dx=-\frac23$ would be wrong (signed area). OpenStax's key: $\frac52$. 8. $f_{\text{ave}}=\dfrac1{3-(-3)}\displaystyle\int_{-3}^{3}(3-\lvert x\rvert)\,dx=\frac16\cdot2\int_0^3(3-x)\,dx=\frac16\cdot2\cdot\frac92=\frac32$ (the integral is the area of a triangle with base $6$ and height $3$, which is $9$). Solve $f(c)=\frac32$: $3-\lvert c\rvert=\frac32$, so $c=\pm\frac32$; both lie in $[-3,3]$. OpenStax's key: $\frac32$ when $c=\pm\frac32$.

Every answer above was checked with sympy (the definite integrals and the derivative, including the divergence in problem 5); the odd-numbered exercises (65, 171, 155, 297, 305, 45, 17, 113) also agree with the OpenStax answer keys.

Unit 8 of 8

Unit 8 — Volume of Solids of Revolution by Definite Integral

Page convention. The Unit 8 pages carry no printed page numbers (checked on the rendered images). "p.N" below means the PDF page of MTH102.pdf. Unit 8 is PDF pp.115–121 (PDF p.122 is blank). The course calls it "MODULE 8"; the Course Guide (PDF p.9) lists it as Module Three, Unit 2. The course has no numbered equations and no Self-Assessment Exercises; the only exercise set is "8.7 TMA" (6 questions).

Likely to be examined (objectives p.115, the six TMA questions pp.120–121): volume of a solid of revolution about the $x$-axis and about the $y$-axis by the disc method; derive the volume of a sphere, a spherical segment and a cone; find the limits of integration from where a curve meets the axis or another curve; area bounded by a line and a curve rotated about the $x$-axis. Per the Course Guide (PDF pp.11–12): TMAs count 30 % and the final exam 70 % (2 hours, 3 questions out of at least 5). The Guide gives no unit-by-unit exam list, so nothing more specific is claimed here.

Scope note. The course teaches only the disc method (and, without naming it, the washer idea in TMA 3 and 5). Shells are not covered; they are included here as an optional cross-check and are marked "(extra)".

What you must be able to do

  • State the definition of a solid of revolution and sketch the solid from the plane region and the axis.
  • Write $V_x=\pi\int_a^b y^2\,dx$ (rotation about the $x$-axis) and $V_y=\pi\int_c^d x^2\,dy$ (rotation about the $y$-axis) with the correct variable and limits.
  • Find limits of integration by solving $y=0$ or by solving for the intersection of two curves.
  • Rewrite a curve as $x=g(y)$ when the rotation is about the $y$-axis.
  • Derive $V_{\text{sphere}}=\frac43\pi r^3$, $V_{\text{cone}}=\frac13\pi r^2h$ and the volume of a spherical segment from the integral.
  • Use the washer form $\pi\int(y_{\text{outer}}^2-y_{\text{inner}}^2)\,dx$ for a region between two curves (TMA 3, 5).
  • Check an answer by sign (volume is positive), units (cubic) and a known solid.

Key ideas

1. Solid of revolution and the disc method (p.115–116)

Intuition. Spin a flat region about a line (the axis of revolution). The region sweeps out a solid. Cut the solid into thin slices perpendicular to the axis; each slice is almost a flat disc (a very short cylinder). Volume of a cylinder = $\pi(\text{radius})^2\times(\text{thickness})$. Add up all the discs: the sum becomes an integral.

About the $x$-axis. Region between the curve $y=f(x)$, the $x$-axis and the lines $x=a$, $x=b$. A disc at position $x$ has radius $y=f(x)$ and thickness $dx$, so its volume is $\pi y^2dx$:

$$V_x=\pi\int_a^b y^2\,dx=\pi\int_a^b [f(x)]^2\,dx .$$

About the $y$-axis. Region between the curve $x=g(y)$, the $y$-axis and the lines $y=c$, $y=d$. A disc at height $y$ has radius $x=g(y)$ and thickness $dy$:

$$V_y=\pi\int_c^d x^2\,dy=\pi\int_c^d [g(y)]^2\,dy .$$

Hypotheses. $f$ continuous on $[a,b]$ (similarly $g$ on $[c,d]$); the region lies on one side of the axis (here $f\ge0$; if $f<0$ the square makes no difference); the region touches the axis along the whole interval, otherwise there is a hole: use washers.

Rule of thumb: the integration variable is the one along the axis. Rotating about the $x$-axis integrates in $x$ and needs $y^2$ in terms of $x$; rotating about the $y$-axis integrates in $y$ and needs $x^2$ in terms of $y$.

Finding limits. For the area bounded by a curve and the $x$-axis, the limits are the roots of $y=0$ (Course Example 2: $-x^2+3x=0\Rightarrow x=0,3$). For a region between two curves, the limits are the $x$-values where they meet (set the two $y$'s equal).

2. Washers (hole in the middle; TMA 3, 5)

If the region lies between an upper curve $y=f(x)$ and a lower curve $y=g(x)$ (with $f\ge g\ge0$ on $[a,b]$), each slice is a washer (disc with a hole): area $\pi(f^2-g^2)$. So

$$V_x=\pi\int_a^b\big([f(x)]^2-[g(x)]^2\big)\,dx .$$

Subtract the squares, not the square of the difference: $(f^2-g^2)\ne(f-g)^2$.

3. Standard solids from the definition (pp.116–119)

  • Sphere of radius $r$: rotate the semicircle $y=\sqrt{r^2-x^2}$, $-r\le x\le r$, about the $x$-axis: $V=\frac43\pi r^3$.
  • Spherical segment (slice of a sphere between two parallel planes): rotate $x=\sqrt{r^2-y^2}$ for $a\le y\le a+h$ about the $y$-axis (valid for $-r\le a$, $a+h\le r$): $V=\pi h\big[r^2-a^2-ah-\frac{h^2}3\big]$.
  • Cone of radius $r$ and height $h$: rotate the segment $y=\frac rh x$, $0\le x\le h$, about the $x$-axis: $V=\frac13\pi r^2h$.

4. Shells (extra, not in the course)

Rotating the region under $y=f(x)$, $a\le x\le b$ ($a\ge0$) about the $y$-axis, a vertical strip at $x$ sweeps a thin cylindrical shell of radius $x$, height $f(x)$, thickness $dx$:

$$V_y=2\pi\int_a^b x\,f(x)\,dx .$$

It gives the same volume as discs/washers in $y$ for the same solid; use it as a check.

Formula sheet

Disc / washer

$$V_x=\pi\int_a^b y^2\,dx,\qquad V_y=\pi\int_c^d x^2\,dy,$$
$$V_x^{\text{washer}}=\pi\int_a^b\big(y_{\text{top}}^2-y_{\text{bottom}}^2\big)dx .$$

Shells (extra): $V_y=2\pi\int_a^b x\,f(x)\,dx$ (region under $y=f(x)$, $x\ge0$). Shifted axis (extra): about the line $y=k$, replace $y$ by the distance to the axis: radius $=|f(x)-k|$; washer outer minus inner radii measured from the line.

Results to know (sympy-verified)

$$V_{\text{cylinder}}=\pi r^2h,\qquad V_{\text{cone}}=\tfrac13\pi r^2h,$$
$$V_{\text{sphere}}=\tfrac43\pi r^3,\qquad V_{\text{hemisphere}}=\tfrac23\pi r^3 .$$
$$V_{\text{frustum}}=\tfrac13\pi h\,(r_1^2+r_1r_2+r_2^2),$$
$$V_{\text{cap of height }h}=\tfrac13\pi h^2(3r-h),$$
$$V_{\text{ellipsoid}}\ (\tfrac{x^2}{A^2}+\tfrac{y^2}{B^2}=1\text{ about }x)=\tfrac43\pi AB^2 .$$
$$V_{\text{segment}}\ (a\le y\le a+h)=\pi h\Big(r^2-a^2-ah-\tfrac{h^2}3\Big)=\tfrac{\pi h}{3}\big(3r^2-3a^2-3ah-h^2\big),$$
$$V_{\text{torus}}\ (\text{circle radius }r,\ \text{centre at distance }b>r\text{ from the axis})=2\pi^2br^2\quad(\text{extra}).$$

Useful integrals: $\int x^n\,dx=\frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1}$; $\int\sin^2x\,dx=\frac x2-\frac{\sin2x}4$ (since $\sin^2x=\frac{1-\cos2x}2$); $(a+b)^2=a^2+2ab+b^2$.

Worked examples

All results below were checked with sympy.

Example 1 — Easy: $y=\sqrt x$, $0\le x\le4$, about the $x$-axis (new)

$y^2=x$. $V=\pi\int_0^4x\,dx=\pi\Big[\dfrac{x^2}2\Big]_0^4=\pi\cdot8=\mathbf{8\pi}$. Check: the solid is a paraboloid of height $4$ and base radius $\sqrt4=2$; a paraboloid is half of its circumscribed cylinder: $\frac12\pi\cdot2^2\cdot4=8\pi$ ✓.

Example 2 — Volume of a sphere (Course §8.4, p.116–117)

Rotate the semicircle $y=\sqrt{r^2-x^2}$ about the $x$-axis. It meets the axis at $x=\pm r$, so the limits are $-r$ and $r$. Squaring: $y^2=r^2-x^2$ (the course writes this as "$(r^2-x^2)^{1/2\times2}$").

$$V=\pi\int_{-r}^{r}(r^2-x^2)\,dx=\pi\Big[r^2x-\frac{x^3}3\Big]_{-r}^{r}=\pi\Big[\Big(r^3-\frac{r^3}3\Big)-\Big(-r^3+\frac{r^3}3\Big)\Big]=\pi\Big[\frac{2r^3}3+\frac{2r^3}3\Big]=\frac43\pi r^3 .$$

Shortcut by symmetry: $2\pi\int_0^r(r^2-x^2)dx=2\pi\cdot\frac{2r^3}3$, same. The course's working is correct.

Example 3 — Cone (Course Example 1, p.118–119, corrected)

A right circular cone: height $h$ along the $x$-axis, base radius $r$. Generating line through the origin and $(h,r)$: $y=mx$ with slope

$$m=\frac{\Delta y}{\Delta x}=\frac rh$$

(the course writes $\Delta x/\Delta y$, which would be $h/r$; its next line $y=\frac rhx$ is nevertheless correct). Then

$$V=\pi\int_0^h\Big(\frac rhx\Big)^2dx=\frac{\pi r^2}{h^2}\Big[\frac{x^3}3\Big]_0^h=\frac{\pi r^2}{h^2}\cdot\frac{h^3}3=\frac{\pi r^2h}3 .$$

Frustum (extra): the line from $(0,r_1)$ to $(h,r_2)$ is $y=r_1+\frac{r_2-r_1}hx$; integrating gives $\frac13\pi h(r_1^2+r_1r_2+r_2^2)$ (sympy: difference $0$).

Example 4 — Spherical segment (Course §8.5, p.117–118, corrected)

The portion of the right semicircle between $y=a$ and $y=a+h$ is rotated about the $y$-axis. Right semicircle: $x=\sqrt{r^2-y^2}$ (the course prints $\sqrt{r^2+y^2}$; see Errata). So $x^2=r^2-y^2$ and

$$V_y=\pi\int_a^{a+h}(r^2-y^2)\,dy=\pi\Big[r^2y-\frac{y^3}3\Big]_a^{a+h}=\pi\Big[r^2h-\frac{(a+h)^3-a^3}3\Big].$$

Since $(a+h)^3-a^3=3a^2h+3ah^2+h^3$,

$$V_y=\pi\Big[r^2h-a^2h-ah^2-\frac{h^3}3\Big]=\pi h\Big[r^2-a^2-ah-\frac{h^2}3\Big].$$

This agrees with the course's final line (sympy: difference $0$). Checks: $a=0,\ h=r$ (hemisphere): $\pi r\big[r^2-\frac{r^2}3\big]=\frac23\pi r^3$ ✓. $a=-r,\ h=2r$ (whole sphere): $2\pi r\big[r^2-r^2+2r^2-\frac{4r^2}3\big]=\frac43\pi r^3$ ✓. Cap ($a=r-h$, top of the sphere): $\frac13\pi h^2(3r-h)$; for $r=5$, $h=2$ this is $\frac{52\pi}3$. Condition: the formula needs $a\ge-r$ and $a+h\le r$ (the course does not say so).

Example 5 — Course Example 2 (p.119–120): $y=-x^2+3x$ about the $x$-axis

Limits: $-x^2+3x=0\Rightarrow x(3-x)=0\Rightarrow x=0,3$. The region lies above the axis ($y\ge0$ on $[0,3]$). $y^2=(-x^2+3x)^2=x^4-6x^3+9x^2$ (the course's expansion is right).

$$V=\pi\int_0^3(x^4-6x^3+9x^2)\,dx=\pi\Big[\frac{x^5}5-\frac{3x^4}2+3x^3\Big]_0^3=\pi\Big[\frac{243}5-\frac{243}2+81\Big]=\pi\cdot\frac{486-1215+810}{10}=\frac{81\pi}{10}\approx25.45 .$$

The course's answer $\frac{81\pi}{10}$ is correct.

Example 6 — About the $y$-axis (new): $y=4-x^2$, $0\le x\le2$, $y\ge0$

The solid is a paraboloid bowl (apex $(0,4)$, rim radius $2$ at $y=0$). About the $y$-axis integrate in $y$: $x^2=4-y$, with $y$ from $0$ to $4$.

$$V=\pi\int_0^4(4-y)\,dy=\pi\Big[4y-\frac{y^2}2\Big]_0^4=\pi(16-8)=8\pi .$$

(extra) Shells: $2\pi\int_0^2x(4-x^2)\,dx=2\pi\big[2x^2-\frac{x^4}4\big]_0^2=2\pi(8-4)=8\pi$ ✓. Check: half the cylinder $\pi\cdot2^2\cdot4=16\pi$ ✓.

Example 7 — Washers about the $x$-axis (new, same idea as TMA 3 and 5): region between $y=x^2$ and $y=\sqrt x$

Limits: $x^2=\sqrt x\Rightarrow x^4=x\Rightarrow x(x^3-1)=0\Rightarrow x=0,1$. On $(0,1)$: $\sqrt x\ge x^2$, so top $=\sqrt x$, bottom $=x^2$.

$$V=\pi\int_0^1\big(x-x^4\big)\,dx=\pi\Big[\frac{x^2}2-\frac{x^5}5\Big]_0^1=\pi\Big(\frac12-\frac15\Big)=\frac{3\pi}{10}.$$

(Not $\pi\int(\sqrt x-x^2)^2dx$, which is the wrong formula.)

Example 8 — Same region about the $y$-axis (new)

Write the curves as $x$ in terms of $y$: right curve $x=\sqrt y$ (from $y=x^2$), left curve $x=y^2$ (from $y=\sqrt x$). For $0<y<1$, $\sqrt y\ge y^2$, so outer radius $\sqrt y$, inner radius $y^2$:

$$V=\pi\int_0^1\big(y-y^4\big)\,dy=\frac{3\pi}{10}.$$

(extra) Shells: $2\pi\int_0^1x(\sqrt x-x^2)\,dx=2\pi\Big[\frac25x^{5/2}-\frac{x^4}4\Big]_0^1=2\pi\cdot\frac3{20}=\frac{3\pi}{10}$ ✓. (Both axes give $\frac{3\pi}{10}$ here because the region is symmetric about $y=x$.)

Example 9 — Axis not a coordinate axis (extra): $y=x^2$, $0\le x\le1$, about the line $y=-1$

Distance from the axis to the curve is $x^2+1$ (outer radius); the $x$-axis is at distance $1$ (inner radius, the region does not touch the line $y=-1$):

$$V=\pi\int_0^1\big[(x^2+1)^2-1^2\big]dx=\pi\int_0^1(x^4+2x^2)\,dx=\pi\Big(\frac15+\frac23\Big)=\frac{13\pi}{15}.$$

Example 10 — Generalising the sphere (new): ellipsoid

Rotate the upper half of $\dfrac{x^2}{A^2}+\dfrac{y^2}{B^2}=1$ about the $x$-axis. $y^2=B^2\Big(1-\dfrac{x^2}{A^2}\Big)$, $-A\le x\le A$.

$$V=\pi B^2\int_{-A}^{A}\Big(1-\frac{x^2}{A^2}\Big)dx=\pi B^2\Big[x-\frac{x^3}{3A^2}\Big]_{-A}^{A}=\pi B^2\cdot\frac{4A}3=\frac43\pi AB^2 .$$

With $A=B=r$ this is the sphere ✓. For $A=3$, $B=2$: $16\pi$.

Common mistakes

  • Forgetting the $\pi$ (or writing $2\pi$ for discs). Discs: $\pi\int y^2dx$; the $2\pi$ belongs only to shells.
  • Forgetting to square $y$. The integrand is $y^2$, not $y$. For $y=-x^2+3x$ expand the whole bracket: $(-x^2+3x)^2=x^4-6x^3+9x^2$, not $x^4+9x^2$.
  • Wrong variable for the axis. About the $y$-axis you need $x^2$ as a function of $y$ and limits in $y$. Do not integrate $\pi y^2dx$ and call it $V_y$.
  • Limits in the wrong variable. For TMA 2 (about the $y$-axis) the limits are $y=1$ to $y=8$, not $x=1$ to $2$ (unless you use shells).
  • Washers: squaring the difference. Use $y_{\text{top}}^2-y_{\text{bottom}}^2$, not $(y_{\text{top}}-y_{\text{bottom}})^2$.
  • Reading the printed limit letters literally. The course's $\int_a^d$ in $V_x$ and $\int_o^d$ in $V_y$ are misprints (see Errata); the limits are $a,b$ and $c,d$.
  • Wrong sign for the semicircle: $x=\sqrt{r^2+y^2}$ is a hyperbola, not a circle. Circle: $x^2+y^2=r^2$.
  • Slope upside down: gradient is $\Delta y/\Delta x$ (rise over run), $r/h$ for the cone.
  • Not finding the intersection points for TMA 3 and 5 before integrating. Solve first, then integrate between the intersections.
  • Arithmetic slips with fractions: $\frac{243}5-\frac{243}2+81$ needs the common denominator $10$.
  • Leaving $\sin^2$ unintegrated. Use $\sin^2x=\frac{1-\cos2x}2$ (TMA 6).
  • Wrong units. Volume is in cubic units; area is not.

Practice problems

Problems 1–6 are the course's tutor-marked assignment (p.120–121). There are no Self-Assessment Exercises in this unit.

  1. (Course TMA 1, p.120) The portion of the curve $y=x^2$ between $x=0$ and $x=2$ is rotated completely round the $x$-axis. Find the volume of the solid.
  2. (Course TMA 2, p.121) The part of the curve $y=x^3$ from $x=1$ to $x=2$ is rotated completely round the $y$-axis. Find the volume of the solid generated (use the figure on p.121: the solid is bounded by the $y$-axis between $y=1$ and $y=8$; also give the value for the "region under the curve" reading).
  3. (Course TMA 3, p.121) The finite area enclosed by the line $2y=x$ and the curve $y^2=2x$ is rotated completely about the $x$-axis. Calculate the volume.
  4. (Course TMA 4, p.121) Find the volume generated by rotating the curve $y=3x^2+1$ from $x=1$ to $x=2$ completely round the $x$-axis.
  5. (Course TMA 5, p.121) If the area enclosed between $y=x^2$ and the line $y=2x$ is rotated around the $x$-axis through four right angles, find the volume.
  6. (Course TMA 6, p.121) Find the volume of the solid generated by rotating $y=2\sin x$ between $x=0$ and $x=\pi/2$ around the $x$-axis.
  7. Use the disc method to show that the volume of the cylinder obtained by rotating $y=r$, $0\le x\le h$, about the $x$-axis is $\pi r^2h$. Then find the volume of the frustum of a cone from $y=r_1+\frac{r_2-r_1}{h}x$, $0\le x\le h$.
  8. The region between $y=x^2$ and $y=\sqrt x$ is rotated about the $x$-axis. Find the volume. (Then check with the $y$-axis version: same region about the $y$-axis.)
  9. Find the volume of the solid obtained by rotating $y=\sqrt x$, $0\le x\le4$, about the $y$-axis. (Hint: the region is bounded by $y=\sqrt x$, $x=4$ and the $x$-axis; use washers.)
  10. A cap of height $h=2$ is cut from the top of a sphere of radius $r=5$. Find its volume using $\pi\int(r^2-y^2)\,dy$.
  11. Rotate the region under $y=e^{x}$, $0\le x\le1$, about the $x$-axis. Find the volume.
  12. Find the volume of the ellipsoid obtained by rotating $\dfrac{x^2}{9}+\dfrac{y^2}{4}=1$ about the $x$-axis.
Show answers

Answers

  1. $V=\pi\int_0^2(x^2)^2dx=\pi\Big[\dfrac{x^5}5\Big]_0^2=\dfrac{32\pi}5\approx20.11$.
  2. Figure reading (solid between the $y$-axis and the curve, $y=1$ to $8$): $x=y^{1/3}$, $x^2=y^{2/3}$; $V=\pi\int_1^8y^{2/3}dy=\pi\cdot\dfrac35\big[y^{5/3}\big]_1^8=\dfrac{3\pi}5(32-1)=\dfrac{93\pi}5\approx58.43$. (Reading "region under the curve" with shells: $2\pi\int_1^2x\cdot x^3dx=\dfrac{62\pi}5$. The course gives no answer; the figure supports $\frac{93\pi}5$. See Errata p.121.)
  3. Intersection: $y=\dfrac x2$ and $y^2=2x$ give $\dfrac{x^2}4=2x\Rightarrow x=0$ or $x=8$. For $0<x<8$: $\sqrt{2x}\ge\dfrac x2$ (e.g. $x=2$: $2>1$), so top $y^2=2x$, bottom $y^2=\dfrac{x^2}4$. $V=\pi\int_0^8\Big(2x-\dfrac{x^2}4\Big)dx=\pi\Big[x^2-\dfrac{x^3}{12}\Big]_0^8=\pi\Big(64-\dfrac{128}3\Big)=\dfrac{64\pi}3\approx67.02$.
  4. $y^2=(3x^2+1)^2=9x^4+6x^2+1$. $V=\pi\Big[\dfrac{9x^5}5+2x^3+x\Big]_1^2=\pi\Big[\Big(\dfrac{288}5+16+2\Big)-\Big(\dfrac95+2+1\Big)\Big]=\pi\Big(\dfrac{279}5+15\Big)=\dfrac{354\pi}5\approx222.42$.
  5. Intersections: $x^2=2x\Rightarrow x=0,2$. On $(0,2)$ the line $y=2x$ is above $y=x^2$. $V=\pi\int_0^2\big(4x^2-x^4\big)dx=\pi\Big[\dfrac{4x^3}3-\dfrac{x^5}5\Big]_0^2=\pi\Big(\dfrac{32}3-\dfrac{32}5\Big)=\dfrac{64\pi}{15}\approx13.40$.
  6. $y^2=4\sin^2x=2(1-\cos2x)$. $V=\pi\int_0^{\pi/2}2(1-\cos2x)\,dx=2\pi\Big[x-\dfrac{\sin2x}2\Big]_0^{\pi/2}=2\pi\cdot\dfrac\pi2=\pi^2\approx9.87$.
  7. Cylinder: $\pi\int_0^hr^2dx=\pi r^2h$. Frustum: $\pi\int_0^h\Big(r_1+\dfrac{r_2-r_1}hx\Big)^2dx=\dfrac{\pi h}3\big(r_1^2+r_1r_2+r_2^2\big)$ (sympy: difference $0$; $r_1=0$ gives the cone).
  8. $\dfrac{3\pi}{10}$ about the $x$-axis ($\pi\int_0^1(x-x^4)dx$); about the $y$-axis also $\dfrac{3\pi}{10}$ (washers $\pi\int_0^1(y-y^4)dy$, shells $2\pi\int_0^1x(\sqrt x-x^2)dx$).
  9. Washers in $y$: outer radius $4$ (line $x=4$), inner radius $x=y^2$, $y\in[0,2]$. $V=\pi\int_0^2(16-y^4)\,dy=\pi\Big[16y-\dfrac{y^5}5\Big]_0^2=\pi\Big(32-\dfrac{32}5\Big)=\dfrac{128\pi}5\approx80.42$. (Shells: $2\pi\int_0^4x\sqrt x\,dx=\dfrac{128\pi}5$ ✓.)
  10. $V=\pi\int_{3}^{5}(25-y^2)\,dy=\pi\Big[25y-\dfrac{y^3}3\Big]_3^5=\pi\Big[\Big(125-\dfrac{125}3\Big)-(75-9)\Big]=\pi\Big(\dfrac{250}3-66\Big)=\dfrac{52\pi}3\approx54.45$. (Formula $\frac13\pi h^2(3r-h)=\frac13\pi\cdot4\cdot13$ ✓.)
  11. $V=\pi\int_0^1e^{2x}dx=\pi\Big[\dfrac{e^{2x}}2\Big]_0^1=\dfrac{\pi(e^2-1)}2\approx10.04$.
  12. $\dfrac43\pi AB^2$ with $A=3$, $B=2$: $V=\dfrac43\pi\cdot3\cdot4=16\pi\approx50.27$.

All numerical and symbolic results in the examples and problems were checked with sympy 1.14 (script: /tmp/mth102_scratch/unit8/check.py).

  3. Disk method, rotated around the $x$-axis: region bounded by $y=e^x+1$, $x=0$, $x=1$ and $y=0$. Source ↗
  4. Rotated around the $y$-axis: region bounded by $y=\sqrt{4-x^2}$, $y=0$ and $x=0$. Source ↗
  5. Rotated around the $x$-axis: region bounded by $y=x^2$ and $y=x+2$. Source ↗
  6. Rotated around the $x$-axis: region bounded by $y=\sin x$, $y=5\sin x$, $x=0$ and $x=\pi$. Source ↗
  7. Washer method, rotated around the $y$-axis: region bounded by $y=x+2$, $y=2x-1$ and $x=0$. Source ↗
  8. Washer method, rotated around the $y$-axis: region bounded by $x=e^{2y}$, $x=y^2$, $y=0$ and $y=\ln 2$. Source ↗
Show answers (extra practice)

Answers (extra practice)

  1. Rotate the line $y=\dfrac rhx$, $0\le x\le h$, about the $x$-axis (the slice at $x$ is a disc of radius $\dfrac{rx}h$): $V=\pi\displaystyle\int_0^h\dfrac{r^2x^2}{h^2}\,dx=\pi\dfrac{r^2}{h^2}\cdot\dfrac{h^3}3=\mathbf{\dfrac13\pi r^2h}$.
  2. $V=\pi\displaystyle\int_0^4(2x^2)^2dx=4\pi\Big[\dfrac{x^5}5\Big]_0^4=\mathbf{\dfrac{4096\pi}5}\approx2573.6$. (Agrees with OpenStax's odd-answer key.)
  3. $V=\pi\displaystyle\int_0^1(e^x+1)^2dx=\pi\int_0^1\big(e^{2x}+2e^x+1\big)dx=\pi\Big[\dfrac{e^{2x}}2+2e^x+x\Big]_0^1=\mathbf{\dfrac{\pi(e^2+4e-3)}2}\approx23.97$.
  4. Solve for $x$: $x^2=4-y^2$, $0\le y\le2$. Discs about the $y$-axis: $V=\pi\displaystyle\int_0^2(4-y^2)\,dy=\pi\Big[4y-\dfrac{y^3}3\Big]_0^2=\mathbf{\dfrac{16\pi}3}$ (a hemisphere of radius 2 ✓). (Agrees with the key.)
  5. Intersections: $x^2=x+2\Rightarrow x=-1,\ 2$. On $(-1,2)$, $x+2\ge x^2$ (at $x=0$: $2>0$), so outer radius $x+2$, inner $x^2$. $V=\pi\displaystyle\int_{-1}^2\big[(x+2)^2-x^4\big]dx=\pi\Big[\dfrac{(x+2)^3}3-\dfrac{x^5}5\Big]_{-1}^2=\pi\Big[\Big(\dfrac{64}3-\dfrac{32}5\Big)-\Big(\dfrac13+\dfrac15\Big)\Big]=\mathbf{\dfrac{72\pi}5}$. (Agrees with the key.)
  6. On $[0,\pi]$, $5\sin x\ge\sin x$, so outer radius $5\sin x$, inner $\sin x$. $V=\pi\displaystyle\int_0^\pi(25-1)\sin^2x\,dx=24\pi\cdot\dfrac\pi2=\mathbf{12\pi^2}\approx118.4$ (using $\int_0^\pi\sin^2x\,dx=\frac\pi2$).
  7. The lines meet where $x+2=2x-1$, i.e. $x=3$, $y=5$. About the $y$-axis the region (between $x=0$ on the left and the lines on the right) needs two pieces. Solve for $x$: line $y=2x-1$ gives $x=\dfrac{y+1}2$; line $y=x+2$ gives $x=y-2$. - $-1\le y\le2$: the region reaches the $y$-axis ($x=0$), so discs of radius $\dfrac{y+1}2$. - $2\le y\le5$: washers, outer radius $\dfrac{y+1}2$, inner radius $y-2$. $V=\pi\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{2}\dfrac{(y+1)^2}4dy+\pi\int_2^5\Big[\dfrac{(y+1)^2}4-(y-2)^2\Big]dy=\pi\int_{-1}^5\dfrac{(y+1)^2}4dy-\pi\int_2^5(y-2)^2dy=18\pi-9\pi=\mathbf{9\pi}$. Shells (extra) confirm: $2\pi\int_0^3x\,[(x+2)-(2x-1)]\,dx=2\pi\int_0^3x(3-x)\,dx=9\pi$ ✓. (Agrees with the key.)
  8. For $0\le y\le\ln2<1$: $e^{2y}\ge1>y^2$, so outer radius $e^{2y}$, inner $y^2$. $V=\pi\displaystyle\int_0^{\ln2}\big(e^{4y}-y^4\big)dy=\pi\Big[\dfrac{e^{4y}}4-\dfrac{y^5}5\Big]_0^{\ln2}=\pi\Big(\dfrac{16-1}4-\dfrac{\ln^52}5\Big)=\mathbf{\dfrac\pi{20}\big(75-4\ln^52\big)}\approx11.68$. (Agrees with the key.)

Every result above was checked with sympy (python3 -I); items 2, 4, 5, 7, 8 also agree with the OpenStax odd-answer key (https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/chapter-6).

More practice online (outside sources)

  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Volume With Rings (8 problems, solution for each) — discs and washers about the $x$- and $y$-axes and about other horizontal lines.
  • OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1 §6.2 exercises 74–102 (odd answers in the book's answer key, chapter 6) — discs and washers about both axes; the same page also has exercises 58–73 on slicing and cross-sections and 103–113 on applications.
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Volume With Cylinders (8 problems, solution for each) — shells, extra — use to cross-check washer answers.
  • OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1 §6.3 exercises 114–164 (odd answers in the book's answer key) — shells, extra — "use both the shell method and the washer method" problems.

Mistakes in the original course

Every mistake that could be verified in the course text, unit by unit. Page numbers are PDF page numbers, not printed page numbers: the unit pages carry no printed numbers, so “p.N” always means page N of MTH102.pdf. Where the scan is unreadable the entry says “unclear”. The same tables are what the unit chapters were written against.

Unit 1 — Functions and Graphs

Errata: mistakes in the course text

All "p." numbers are PDF page numbers (the unit has no printed numbers). Each item was checked against the rendered page image and, for calculations, with sympy.

Page Course says Correct Note
30 Example 7: $f(1)=1-5+1=-5$ $f(1)=-3$ Serious. p.25 (Checkpoint) correctly says $f(1)=-3$.
30–31 "Is $f$ one to one?" never answered No: $f(0)=f(5)=1$ The question is in the example statement but the solution stops.
31 Graph of $x^2-5x+1$ drawn as two straight segments through $(0,1)$, "(0. 5" and $(4,-3)$ A parabola with vertex $(\frac52,-\frac{21}4)$ through $(1,-3)$ The labelled point $(1,-5)$ follows the arithmetic slip.
33 $g(f(x))=(f(x))^2+1=\dots=4x^2-16x+17$ for $g=x^2+3$, $f=2x-4$ $g(f(x))=(2x-4)^2+3=4x^2-16x+19$ Serious. $g$ was defined as $x^2+3$; "+1" comes from the previous example.
33 Part (a) of the example is headed "$g(f(x))=2(g(x))-4$" $f(g(x))=2g(x)-4=2x^2+2$ Wrong label. The last two lines also give $2x^2-2$ and $2(x^2-1)$: the sum is $2x^2+6-4=2x^2+2$, so both are wrong.
34 Example 8(d): $f(x)=3x+7$, $f^{-1}(x)=\frac13(x+7)$ $f^{-1}(x)=\dfrac{x-7}3$ Serious. $f(\frac{x+7}3)=x+14\ne x$.
34 Example 8(b): "$f(x)=1/5x$, $f^{-1}(x)=5x$" Only correct if $f(x)=\frac15x$. If read as $\dfrac1{5x}$ the inverse is $\dfrac1{5x}$ Ambiguous layout; the printed answer $5x$ fixes the intended reading. Same on p.35 Checkpoint (a).
36 First check labelled "$f^{-1}(f(x))=\sqrt{2(\dots)-3}$" (left column) It is $f(f^{-1}(x))=\sqrt{2\cdot\frac{x^2+3}2-3}=x$ Mislabelled; the right-hand column ($x\ge\frac32$) is correct.
36 Fig. 1.24: $f(x)=\sqrt{2x-3}$ levels off near $y\approx1.5$; $f^{-1}$ rises almost vertically near $x\approx1$ $f$ increases without bound; $f^{-1}(x)=\frac{x^2+3}2$ is a parabola (a mirror image of $f$ in $y=x$) The plotted points $(\frac32,0)$, $(2,1)$, $(0,\frac32)$, $(1,2)$ are right; the curves are not.
19 "Range" defined as "the set of all values taken on" and, in the OR-definition, the whole second set Range = values actually taken; the second set is the codomain Not an arithmetic error, but the two statements contradict each other; same on p.40.
20 Machine "inputs values of the independent variable and inputs values of the dependent variable" "...and outputs values of the dependent variable" Typo.
20 $x+y=1$ rewritten as "$y=v-x$" $y=1-x$ Typo (the table on p.21 has the right form).
21 (also 22) Fig. 1.12: $x$-axis marked $2,1$ to the left of the origin and $-1,-2$ to the right; circle labelled "$x^2+y^2$" with no "$=1$" Positive values to the right; label "$x^2+y^2=1$" Verified at 170 dpi: the line $x+y=1$ crosses the axis at the tick labelled $-1$. The same mirrored labelling is on the graph of $x+y^2=1$ on p.22.
22 Example 2(a): "$x^2+y$" $x^2+y=4$ "$=4$" is lost; the solution uses it.
26 Checkpoint 1(b): $\dfrac{f(x+\Delta x)-f(x)}{f(x)}$ Denominator $\Delta x$ Typo, the whole section is about dividing by $\Delta x$. Answer: $2x+\Delta x-2$.
28 Fig. 1.22(a): "Domain: $x>1$" $x\ge1$ (domain $[1,\infty)$) The text on p.27 and the point $(1,0)$ on the graph show $x\ge1$.
28 "domain is $[1,\infty]$", "range $[0,\infty]$" $[1,\infty)$, $[0,\infty)$ $\infty$ is never included. The sentence "defined for $x<$ and for $x\ge I$" is garbled (should be $x<1$, $x\ge1$).
32 Checkpoint (iv): horizontal axis labelled "$y$" $x$ Typo; both axes are called $y$.
33 Heading "1.4 combinations of functions"; p.38 heading "1.6 Exercises" Contents (p.18): 1.6 Combination, 1.7 Inverse Notation and Exercises Section numbers repeat. Use the page numbers, not the section numbers.
33 $(f.g)(x)=f(g(x))$; domain: "$g(x)$ is the domain of $f$" $(f\circ g)(x)=f(g(x))$; "$g(x)$ is in the domain of $f$" Notation and wording.
39 Exercise 9 difference quotient written with $f(x-\Delta x)$ Very likely $f(x+\Delta x)$ as in Exercises 10, 11: $3x^2+3x\Delta x+(\Delta x)^2-1$ If read literally: $-3x^2+3x\Delta x-(\Delta x)^2+1$. Unclear which is meant; solve with $+$.

Unit 2 — Limits

Errata: mistakes in the course text

Pages are PDF page numbers (the PDF has no printed numbers here). Every item was checked against the rendered page image; calculations were also checked with sympy. "Copy" means the duplicate on pp.50–59.

Page Course says Correct Note
42 (copy 52) Ex 2b: $\lim_{x\to1}\frac{\lvert x-1\rvert}{x-1}=\frac{1-1}{1-1}=\frac00=0$ $\frac00$ is undefined. Left limit $-1$, right limit $+1$; limit does not exist Serious. The final statement "does not exist" is right but the line of working is false.
48 Ex 8: $\lim_{x\to2^-}\frac3{x-2}=\infty$ $\lim_{x\to2^-}=-\infty$ ($x-2<0$); right side $+\infty$ Serious. Sign error; the conclusion "does not exist" is right.
48 Checkpoint: $\lim_{x\to-2}\frac5{x+2}=\frac5{-2+2}=\frac50=\infty$ One-sided limits $-\infty$ (left) and $+\infty$ (right); the limit does not exist Serious. "$\frac50$" is not a number and the answer is not $\infty$.
45 (copy 54–55) Ex 5: numerator printed as $x^2+x+6$; check "$-3^2+(-3)-6=9-3-6=0$"; later factored as $(x-2)(x+3)$ The numerator must be $x^2+x-6$; write $(-3)^2$ With $+6$ the value at $-3$ is $12$, so nothing cancels and the limit does not exist. The same $x^2+x-6$ appears in the graph label (p.46).

43 (copy 52) Replacement Theorem: $\lim_{x\to2}f(x)=\lim_{x\to2}g(x)$ $\lim_{x\to c}f=\lim_{x\to c}g$ The theorem is stated for a general $c$.
43 (copy 52) Checkpoint 1c: $f(x)=x^2,\ x\ne0$; $0,\ x=2$ The two cases overlap at $x=2$. Presumably $x\ne2$ and $f(2)=0$ The limit is $4$ either way.
