Unit 4 — The Derivative as Limit of Rate of Change

Errata: mistakes in the course text

Pages are PDF page numbers (the pages of this unit carry no printed numbers). Every item was read off the page image (zoomed to 200 dpi where marked) and, for calculations, checked with sympy. Because the course uses $\delta y/\delta x$ for the derivative throughout, that single notation point is listed once (row "70–78").

Page Course says Correct Note
68 $m=\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}=\dfrac{\Delta x}{\Delta y}=\dfrac{y_2-y_1}{x_2-x_1}$ $m=\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}=\dfrac{\Delta y}{\Delta x}=\dfrac{y_2-y_1}{x_2-x_1}$ Serious. The middle fraction is upside down. The Summary (p.78, iii) has the correct $\delta y/\delta x$. Also the last denominator prints $x_{2-x_1}$ for $x_2-x_1$.
68, 69 "$\delta x$ and $\delta y$ are called the differentials" They are increments; $dx,dy$ are the differentials Terminology. The figures on p.68–69 label the same quantities $dx,\ dy$ while the text uses $\delta x,\ \delta y$.
68 Curve figure: point $P$ labelled "$X^2$" and "$X_1$" unclear Figure labels are garbled; read the picture, not the labels.
69 $y+\delta y=3(x^2+2x-\delta x+|\delta x|^2)+6$ $3\big(x^2+2x\,\delta x+(\delta x)^2\big)+6$ The sign between $2x$ and $\delta x$ is a misprint (it is a product). $|\delta x|^2=(\delta x)^2$, so the absolute value is harmless but unnecessary. (200 dpi zoom.)
69 First example is unlabelled; the next on p.70 is "Example 2" Label them Example 1 and Example 2 Numbering. Then p.73 starts a second "Example 1/Example 2" under 4.5.
70 $y+\delta y-y=3x^2+6x\,\delta x+3|\delta x|^2+6-(3x^2+6x)$, then $\delta y=\dots+6-3x^2-6x$ Subtract $y=3x^2+6$: $\dots+6-(3x^2+6)$ Wrong substitution of $y$ from eq. (1). The next lines are garbled ("$3x^2+6x\delta x\ 3x^2-3x^2\ldots+6-6$") but land on the right answer $\delta y=6x\,\delta x+3(\delta x)^2$.
70 $\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}=6x+3|\delta x|$ $6x+3\,\delta x$ $(\delta x)^2/\delta x=\delta x$, not $|\delta x|$; they differ for $\delta x<0$. The limit ($6x$) is unaffected.
70–71 Example 2 expansion: $x^2+2x\,dx+\delta x+|\delta x|^2+3x+3\delta x$ $x^2+2x\,\delta x+(\delta x)^2+3x+3\,\delta x$ Stray "$+\delta x$" and $dx$ in place of $\delta x$. Later lines drop the stray term. (200 dpi zoom.)
71 "$8y=2x\delta x+|\delta x|^2+3\delta x$" $\delta y=\dots$ Misprint.
71 $\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}=2x+|\delta x|+3$ $2x+\delta x+3$ Same $|\delta x|$ slip as p.70.
70–78 Final results written as "$\delta y/\delta x=6x$", "$=2x+3$", "$=nx^{n-1}$", "$=\cos x$", "$\delta y/\delta x\to\delta y/\delta x$", table header "$\delta y/\delta x$"; "$\lim\delta y\to0,\delta x\to0$" $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=\lim_{\delta x\to0}\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}$; only $\delta x\to0$ is needed Notation. The limit of the chord gradient is $dy/dx$. Letting $\delta y\to0$ is a consequence, not a separate condition. (The summary on p.78 mixes $\delta y/\delta x$ and $dy/dx$ in one line.)
71–72 Binomial series with "…" used to prove $\frac d{dx}x^n=nx^{n-1}$; "We can established that if $y=x^n$" (unfinished sentence); the table (p.77) states it for all $n$ The series terminates and the proof is valid only for positive integer $n$ Missing hypothesis. The result is true for all real $n$ ($x>0$ if $n\notin\mathbb Z$) but needs another proof (Example 5(b),(c)).
73 "$2.5x^{2-1}-1.4x^{1-1}$", "$3.6x^{3-1}-2.4x^{2-1}+1.7x^{1-1}$" $2\cdot5x$, $1\cdot4$, $3\cdot6x^2$, $2\cdot4x$, $1\cdot7$ The dot means multiplication; it reads like a decimal. The values $10x,\ 4,\ 18x^2,\ 8x,\ 7$ are right, final answers $3x^2+10x-4$ and $4x^3+18x^2-8x+7$ (95 at $x=2$) correct (sympy).
74 $y+\delta y-y=\sin(x+y+\delta x)-y$ $\sin(x+\delta x)-y$ Stray "$y$" inside the sine.
74 "$y=2\cos(\ldots)\sin(\ldots)$" (first line after the identity) $\delta y=\ldots$ Missing $\delta$.
74 $\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}=\dfrac{2\cos(x+\frac{\delta x}2)+\sin(\frac{\delta x}2)}{\delta x}$, then $\dfrac{\cos(\ldots)+\sin(\ldots)}{\delta x/2}$ Product: $\cos\!\big(x+\frac{\delta x}2\big)\cdot\dfrac{\sin(\delta x/2)}{\delta x/2}$ Serious. The "$+$" is wrong (sympy: $\sin(x+h)-\sin x-2\cos(x+\frac h2)\sin\frac h2=0$). The following line has the product right.
74–76 Limit step: put $\delta x=0$ to get $\cos(x+\frac02)\frac{\sin(0/2)}{0/2}$ and then $=\cos x$ (and $-\sin x$) Need $\lim_{\theta\to0}\frac{\sin\theta}\theta=1$ (radians) Serious omission. $\frac{\sin(0/2)}{0/2}$ is $\frac00$; the course never states or proves the key limit that makes the proof work. Also "$\delta y\to0$" should be "$\delta x\to0$".
75 $\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}=\dfrac{-2\cos(\frac{2x}2+\frac{\delta x}2)\sin\frac{\delta x}2}{\delta x}$ $\dfrac{-2\sin(x+\frac{\delta x}2)\sin\frac{\delta x}2}{\delta x}$ $\cos$ should be $\sin$; the line before and after have $\sin$.
76 Quotient rule written with $\dfrac{\partial u}{\partial x},\dfrac{\partial v}{\partial x}$, "equation (1)" never displayed; "(where $\sin^2A+\cos^2B=1$)" $\dfrac{du}{dx},\dfrac{dv}{dx}$; $\sin^2A+\cos^2A=1$ Partial-derivative symbol is for several variables; here $u,v$ depend on $x$ only. The quotient rule is taught in Unit 2 (p.79 onward), after it is used here.
77 Table has "$a^x\to a^x\ln a$" twice; no conditions ($\ln x$: $x>0$; $\tan x,\sec x$: $\cos x\ne0$; $a>0$) One row; add the conditions Duplicate row and missing domains. Rows for $\cot x$, $\sec x$, $\operatorname{cosec}x$, $\sinh x$, $\cosh x$, $e^{kx}$ are correct (sympy) but not proved in the unit.
77 "iii. $y=6x^2-1$ iv. $y=4x^3$" printed as "$6x^2-1$iv." Four separate parts i–iv Typesetting; the four questions are $x^3$, $5x^2+2$, $6x^2-1$, $4x^3$.

Unit 5 — Differentiation Techniques

Errata: mistakes in the course text

Pages are PDF page numbers (the unit has no printed page numbers). Every item was checked against the rendered page image and, for calculations, with sympy.

Page Course says Correct Note
80 Example 1: $\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta x}=x^3\cos x+\sin x\cdot3x=x(x^2\cos x+3\sin x)$ $x^3\cos x+3x^2\sin x=x^2(x\cos x+3\sin x)$ Serious. $du/dx=3x^2$ (stated one line above); the power of $x$ was dropped.
80 Product rule and its proof are written with $\delta$'s: "$\delta y/\delta x=u\delta v/\delta x+v\delta u/\delta x$" $\dfrac{dy}{dx}=u\dfrac{dv}{dx}+v\dfrac{du}{dx}$ Notation. The text "(NB tends to turns to )" has lost its symbol. The step $\delta u\,\delta v/\delta x\to0$ needs $\delta v\to0$ (continuity) — not stated.
81 Example 4 reuses $u,v,y$ for two different things ("$\delta v/\delta x=\delta y/\delta x=\delta y/\delta u\cdot\delta u/\delta x$") and refers to "module 3.3" Use a new letter (e.g. $w=2x-5$); the chain rule is §5.5 here Confusing but the final answer $2x(2x-5)^3(6x-5)$ is right.
82 Checkpoint 5: $y=3x^3\cdot e^3$ $y=3x^3e^x$ The solution (p.84) uses $e^x$.
83 Solution 1: "$\delta u/\delta x=ex$" $2x$ The next line uses $2x$; answer right.
84 Solution 4: "$\delta y/\delta x=\cos x$" for $v=\sin x$ $\delta v/\delta x=\cos x$ Label slip.
84–85 Quotient proof: "$\delta y=\dfrac{v\delta u-v\delta u}{v^2+v\delta v}$", then "$\dfrac{v\delta u+u\delta v}{v^2+v\delta v}$", "$\dfrac{\ldots+\ldots}{v^2+v\,dv}$" $\dfrac{v\,\delta u-u\,\delta v}{v^2+v\,\delta v}$ at every step Serious for the proof. Sign errors on three consecutive lines; the final (4) is correct.
85 "if $\delta x\to0$, $\delta u\to0$ and $\delta x\to0$" "$\delta u\to0$ and $\delta v\to0$" Typo; $\delta v\to0$ is what makes $v\,\delta v\to0$.
85 Example 1: first line "$\sqrt{x+1}\cdot2x-x^2\cdot2\dfrac1{\sqrt{x+1}}$" The second term is $x^2\cdot\dfrac1{2\sqrt{x+1}}$ (since $v'=\frac1{2\sqrt{x+1}}$) Unclear in the scan (nested fraction); the next lines use $\dfrac{x^2}{2\sqrt{x+1}}$, so the final answer is right.
87 Example 3 problem: $y=\dfrac{5e^x}{\cos x}$; solution begins "$y=\dfrac{4e^x}{\cos x}$" Problem and solution must match; for $5e^x/\cos x$ the answer is $\dfrac{5e^x(\cos x+\sin x)}{\cos^2x}$ The solution is correct for $4e^x/\cos x$. Also "$=\cos x$" is missing the "$v$".
88 Checkpoint a: $y=\dfrac{4x+1}{7x-4}$, answer "$\dfrac{-a}{(7x-4)}$" $\dfrac{-23}{(7x-4)^2}$ Garbled and the square is missing.
88 Checkpoint b: $y=\dfrac{x^2-1}{x^3}$, answer $\dfrac{x(-x^2+3x+2)}{(x^2+)^2}$ $\dfrac{3-x^2}{x^4}$ Serious. The printed answer is not equal to the derivative (it equals $\frac{-x^2+3x+2}{x^3}$ if read as written).
88 Section heading "3.5 FUNCTION OF A FUNCTION" 5.5 Section number from another unit.
88 Chain rule derivation: "$\delta y/\delta x=\delta y/\delta u\cdot\delta u/\delta x$ because $\delta u$ cancels"; "Also $\delta y/\delta x\to\delta u/\delta x$" Cancelling needs $\delta u\ne0$; the limit statement should be $\dfrac{\delta y}{\delta u}\to\dfrac{dy}{du}$ Heuristic only (see Key ideas). The second sentence is garbled.
89 Example 1: "let $u=6x-5$, therefore $y=u^2$" $y=u^{-2}$ The following line ($dy/du=-2u^{-3}$) is for $u^{-2}$; answer right.
89 Example 2: $y=(2x+8)^2$, "$y=u^3$", $dy/du=3u^2$, answer $6(2x+8)^2$ $y=u^2$, $dy/du=2u$, answer $4(2x+8)=8x+32$ Serious. Wrong power; wrong final answer.
90 Example 4: "$u=3x=2$", "$3\sec^2(3x=2)$", "Y tan(3x+2)" $u=3x+2$; $3\sec^2(3x+2)$; $y=\tan(3x+2)$ Typos.
90 Checkpoint ii: $y=\sqrt{4x-3}$, answer $\dfrac2{(4x-3)^{3/2}}$ $\dfrac2{(4x-3)^{1/2}}=\dfrac2{\sqrt{4x-3}}$ Serious. Wrong exponent. Also the label "ii. ii." is doubled.
91 "Steps…: it is very important to multiply the differential function of $y$ by the derivative of the function" Differentiate each term in $y$ with the chain rule and multiply by $dy/dx$ Unclear wording.
91–93 Example 2: $2x^2+3y^2-4x-3y+6=0$ "at $x=3$, $y=2$" $(3,2)$ is not on the curve ($18\ne0$), and the curve has no real points Serious. The method and the values $dy/dx=-8/9$, $d^2y/dx^2=-236/243$ are correct as computations. Example 9(a) above uses $-12$ in place of $+6$.
92 "since $dy/dx=\dfrac{-4-4x}{6y-3}$" $\dfrac{4-4x}{6y-3}$ Typo; the line above has the right form.
93 Example 3: "Differentiating with respect to $y$" with respect to $x$ Typo; result right.
93 Example 4 statement "$x^2+y^2=2x+y=6$" $x^2+y^2-2x+y=6$ Garbled; the solution uses the corrected form.
94 "There are two points … $(2,-3)$ and $(2,-2)$"; later "gradient at point $(2,-2)$" $(2,2)$ $(2,-2)$ is not on the curve; the calculation uses $y=2$.
94 Checkpoint II answer $-\dfrac{x^2+y^2}{y^2+2y}$ $-\dfrac{x^2+y^2}{y^2+2xy}$ Serious. The $x$ in $2xy$ is missing.
95 Headings "1.7 Exercises", "1.8 Summary" 5.7, 5.8 Numbering from another unit.
95 Exercise C II: $y=(5x^3-2)$ Probably a power is missing (e.g. $(5x^3-2)^n$) Unclear. As printed the derivative is $15x^2$.
79 "∂x, ∂u" used beside "δx, δu" $\delta$ (small change) throughout Notation; $\partial$ means partial derivative.

Unit 6 — Integration: indefinite integrals and techniques

Errata: mistakes in the course text

Pages are PDF page numbers (the pages carry no printed numbers). Each row was checked against the rendered page image; calculations were also checked with sympy.

Page Course says Correct Note
98 "$\int x^n\,dx=\frac{x^{n+1}}{n+1}$ (where $n\ne1$)" $n\ne-1$ Serious. $n=1$ is fine ($\int x\,dx=x^2/2$); the formula fails at $n=-1$. Repeated on p.99 (formula I) and p.114 (Summary 3).
98 "If $\frac{\delta y}{\delta x}=f(x)$, $v=\int f(x)dx+c$" $y=\int f(x)\,dx+c$ Misprint $v$ for $y$. The course writes $\delta$ for the derivative/differential throughout; standard notation is $dy/dx$, $dx$.
97–99 Contents: 6.4 "Notations for Integration"; body has two sections headed 6.4 (p.98 "Notation", p.99 "Standard Integral") and no 6.3.1 Numbering clash only The unit is called "Unit 3" in the module list but sections are numbered 6.x. Ignore the numbers.
99 Formula II: $\int\frac1x\,dx=\ln x+c$ $\ln\lvert x\rvert+c$ For $x<0$, $\ln x$ is undefined.
99 Formula XII: $\int\frac1{\sqrt{1+x^2}}\,dx=\tan^{-1}x+c$ $\int\frac1{1+x^2}\,dx=\tan^{-1}x+c$ Serious. The square root is wrong; with it the answer is $\sinh^{-1}x$, which the course itself lists as XIII.
99 $\sin^{-1}$, $\cos^{-1}$, $\cosh^{-1}$, $\tanh^{-1}$ results stated with no range; (4) $e^{kx}/k$ and XVI $a^x/\ln a$ with no condition $\lvert x\rvert<1$ for $\sin^{-1},\cos^{-1},\tanh^{-1}$; $x>1$ for $\cosh^{-1}$; $k\ne0$; $a>0,a\ne1$ Omitted hypotheses. For $\lvert x\rvert>1$, $\int\frac{dx}{1-x^2}=\coth^{-1}x$; the log form $\frac12\ln\lvert\frac{1+x}{1-x}\rvert$ covers both.
102 $\int\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)}dx=\ln\{f(x)\}+c$ (also p.114 Summary 5) $\ln\lvert f(x)\rvert+c$ Serious. Example 1 uses $x^2-5x+6=(x-2)(x-3)$, which is negative on $(2,3)$.
102–103 Heading "Integrals of the form $\int\frac{f'}f$ and $\int f(x)f'(x)dx$" No example or formula is given for $\int ff'\,dx$ Omission. $\int f f'\,dx=\tfrac12f^2+c$.
102–103 Example 1 answer $\ln(x^2-5x+6)$; Example 2 answer $\ln(x^3-6)$ $\ln\lvert x^2-5x+6\rvert$, $\ln\lvert x^3-6\rvert$ Same issue (Ex.3's $x^2-4x+5>0$ is the one case where no bars are needed).
104 $\int\cot x\,dx=\ln\sin x+c$ $\ln\lvert\sin x\rvert+c$ Same issue.
107 "Integration by partial fraction for a irreducible denominator … Since the denominator is irreducible, we will have to factorize it" $x^2-3x+2=(x-2)(x-1)$ is reducible (distinct linear factors) Self-contradictory. The only genuinely irreducible factor in the course is $x^2+1$ (p.111).
108 Answer $3\ln(x-2)-2\ln(x-1)+c$ $3\ln\lvert x-2\rvert-2\ln\lvert x-1\rvert+c$ Absolute values.
108–109 "To get $B$, using equation (2) taking the coefficient of $x^2$" equation (1) No equation (2) exists in this example.
109 "$+\frac12\int\frac1{(x+1)^2}dx$" $+\frac12\int\frac1{(x-1)^2}dx$ Typo: $C$ belongs to $(x-1)^2$.
109 Final answer $\frac14\ln(x+1)+\frac34\ln(x-1)+\frac1{2(x-1)}+c$ $\ldots-\frac1{2(x-1)}+c$ Serious. $\int(x-1)^{-2}dx=-(x-1)^{-1}$. With the course's sign, the derivative of the answer differs from the integrand by $-\frac1{(x-1)^2}$ (sympy).
111 "$=\ln(x+2)-4[a^{-1}]-\frac52[b^{-2}]+c$" $\ln\lvert x+2\rvert+4a^{-1}-\frac52b^{-2}+c$ Sign slip in the intermediate line ($-4\int a^{-2}da=+4a^{-1}$); the next line's $+\frac4{x+2}$ is right.
111 Final answer $\ln(x+2)+\frac4{x+2}-\frac5{2(x+3)^2}+c$ $\ldots-\frac5{2(x+2)^2}+c$ Serious misprint $(x+3)$. With $(x+3)$ the derivative is not the integrand (sympy).
112 Example 4 answer $\ln(x-2)$ $\ln\lvert x-2\rvert$ Absolute value; the rest ($\frac1{10}\ln(x^2+1)$, $\frac25\tan^{-1}x$) is correct. Also "$Bx+c$" uses lower-case $c$ for the constant $C$.
113 Exercise C vii: $\int\frac{2x-3}{x^2+3-7}dx$ unclear As printed, $x^2+3-7=x^2-4$ (answer $\frac14\ln\lvert x-2\rvert+\frac74\ln\lvert x+2\rvert$). A missing "$x$" is possible, but then the numerator would not be $f'$ either way ($f'=2x+3$ or $2x-3$).

Unit 7 — Definite integrals: FTC, evaluation, substitution with limits, area

Errata: mistakes in the course text

Pages are PDF page numbers (the pages carry no printed numbers). Only what the course actually says about definite integrals is listed, and each row was checked against the rendered page; the rest of Module 8 (volumes, Unit 8) is left to that chapter.

Page Course says Correct Note
9 Module list: "Module Three, Unit 1: Definite integrals (Application to areas under curve and Volumes of solids)" The body has no such unit and no area-under-a-curve section Omission. The body jumps from Unit 6 (p.114) to "Module 8: Volume of solids of revolution by definite integral" (p.115). The definition of $\int_a^b$, the FTC, substitution with limits and area are never taught.
115 Module 8 contents list "8.7 Exercise" The section is headed "8.7 TMA" (p.120) Label mismatch.
115–116 Region "bounded by the segment of the curve $y=f(x)$ between $x=a$, $y=b$ and the $x$-axis" between $x=a$ and $x=b$ Typo: $y=b$ should be $x=b$.
116 $V_x=\pi\int_a^d y^2\,\delta x$ $V_x=\pi\int_a^b y^2\,dx$ Wrong upper limit. $d$ is a $y$-value (second figure); the limits of an $x$-integral are the $x$-values $a$ and $b$.
116 "Note that the infinitesimal volume of the cylinder representing an element of the integration $V_x=\pi\int\dots$" The element is $\pi y^2\,dx$; the integral is the whole volume Wording conflates the slice with the total.
116 $V_y=\pi\int_o^d x^2\,\delta y$ for the curve $x=g(y)$ "between $y=c$ and $y=d$" $V_y=\pi\int_c^d x^2\,dy$ Lower limit $o$ (zero) only agrees with the text when $c=0$.
118 "right semi circle equation $x=\sqrt{r^2+y^2}$" $x=\sqrt{r^2-y^2}$ Sign error; the next line correctly integrates $x^2=r^2-y^2$. The first displayed form $V_y=\int_c^dx^2\,\delta y$ also omits the factor $\pi$ (it reappears on the next line).

(The values in the worked examples on pp.117–120 — sphere $\frac43\pi r^3$, spherical segment $\pi h\big(r^2-a^2-ah-\frac{h^2}3\big)$, cone $\frac13\pi r^2h$, and $\frac{81\pi}{10}$ — were re-derived with sympy and are correct.)

Unit 8 — Volume of Solids of Revolution by Definite Integral

Errata: mistakes in the course text

Page numbers are PDF page numbers (Unit 8 has no printed numbers). Each item was checked against the rendered page (and zoomed crops); calculations were checked with sympy.

Page Course says Correct Note
115 "bounded by the segment of the curve $y=f(x)$ between $x=a$, $y=b$ and the $x$-axis" between $x=a$ and $x=b$ Typo ($y=b$ for $x=b$); the integral on p.116 uses $a$ to $b$.
116 $V_x=\pi\int_a^{d}y^2\,\delta x$ $V_x=\pi\int_a^{b}y^2\,dx$ Serious. Upper limit is $d$ instead of $b$ ($d$ is the $y$-axis limit of Fig 2). $\delta x$ should be $dx$ in a definite integral.
116 "the infinitesimal volume of the cylinder representing an element of the integration $V_x=\pi\int\dots$" the infinitesimal element is $\pi y^2\,dx$; the integral is the total volume Wording slip: the displayed formula is the whole integral, not an element.
116 $V_y=\pi\int_{o}^{d}x^2\,\delta y$ $V_y=\pi\int_{c}^{d}x^2\,dy$ Lower limit printed as the letter "o"; the text just above defines the limits as $y=c$ and $y=d$. $0$ only if $c=0$.
117 $y^2=(r^2-x^2)^{1/2\times2}$ then $y^2=r^2-x^2$ $y^2=r^2-x^2$ Not wrong, but odd notation; $(\cdot)^{1/2\times2}=(\cdot)^1$. Limits $a,b$ are used before they are set to $-r,r$.
117–118 Figure for the spherical segment (labels "a" at both discs, $y_1$, $y_2$, $h$) unclear The scan labels the lower disc at the $x$-axis and the upper at height $h$, yet the solution integrates from $y=a$ to $y=a+h$. Read the solution, not the picture.
118 "right semi circle equation $x=\sqrt{r^2+y^2}$" $x=\sqrt{r^2-y^2}$ Serious. Plus sign. The next line uses $r^2-y^2$ and is right.
118 $V_y=\int_c^dx^2\,\delta y=\int_a^{a+h}(r^2-y^2)\,\delta y$ $V_y=\pi\int\dots$ $\pi$ missing on the first line (restored on the next). Limits $c,d$ were not renamed $a,a+h$.
118 Segment formula given with no condition valid for $a\ge-r$ and $a+h\le r$ Missing hypothesis. The algebra and final result $\pi h[r^2-a^2-ah-\frac{h^2}3]$ are correct (sympy).
119 $m=\dfrac{\Delta x}{\Delta y}=\dfrac rh$ $m=\dfrac{\Delta y}{\Delta x}=\dfrac rh$ Serious. Gradient is rise over run. Equation (3) $y=\frac rhx$ is correct.
119 "$=\pi\int_0^h\frac{r2x2}{h2}dx$" $\pi\int_0^h\dfrac{r^2x^2}{h^2}dx$ Typesetting: exponents lost in the scan. Result is correct.
119 Example 2: "a plane bounded by the segment of a curvey $=-x^2+3x$" "a plane region … the curve $y=\dots$" Typos. Working and answer $\frac{81\pi}{10}$ are correct (sympy).
119 Figure: the reflected curve for $y=-x^2+3x$ drawn with minimum near $x=2$, peak near $x\approx1.3$ peak at $(\frac32,\frac94)$, mirror minimum at $(\frac32,-\frac94)$ Freehand sketch, not to scale; does not affect the answer.
120 Heading "8.7 TMA" Contents list (p.115) says "8.7 Exercise" Inconsistent heading. "8.8 Summary" is listed in the Contents but does not appear in the text (p.121 goes from the TMA straight to "8.9 References").
121 TMA 2: "The part of the curve $y=x^3$ from $x=1$ to $x=2$ is rotated … find the volume of the solid generated." unclear / ambiguous A curve alone generates a surface. The figure shows a bowl closed by discs at $y=1$ (radius $1$) and $y=8$ (radius $2$), so the intended solid is the one between the $y$-axis and the curve, $\pi\int_1^8x^2dy=\frac{93\pi}5$. A different reading (region under the curve, shells) gives $\frac{62\pi}5$. Ask your tutor.
115, title "DEFINATE INTEGRAL" "DEFINITE INTEGRAL" Spelling (title, Contents, and Guide p.9).

Further help

Each unit's reading list first, then that unit's “more practice online” list, then general references. The links are reproduced from the chapters; they are not re-fetched when this page is built, so a link may have moved since the chapter was written.

Unit 1 — Functions and Graphs

All URLs below were opened and the page titles/contents matched the topic.

  • Function definition, notation, domain and range. Paul's Online Math Notes, "The Definition of a Function": https://tutorial.math.lamar.edu/Classes/Alg/FunctionDefn.aspx
  • Combining functions and composition. Paul's Online Math Notes, "Combining Functions": https://tutorial.math.lamar.edu/Classes/Alg/CombineFunctions.aspx
  • Inverse functions, one-to-one, finding and verifying an inverse. Paul's Online Math Notes, "Inverse Functions": https://tutorial.math.lamar.edu/Classes/Alg/InverseFunctions.aspx
  • Review of functions: domain, range, vertical line test, composition. OpenStax Calculus Volume 1, 1.1 "Review of Functions": https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/1-1-review-of-functions
  • Inverse functions: horizontal line test, restricting a domain. OpenStax Calculus Volume 1, 1.4 "Inverse Functions": https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/1-4-inverse-functions

More practice online (outside sources)

  • OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1 §1.1 exercises 7–13 (evaluate and simplify $f(a+h)$) and 36–48 (sum, product, quotient and composition, with domains); odd answers in the book's Chapter 1 answer key — the same style as exam questions on function notation and composition.
  • OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1 §1.4 exercises 189–194 (find $f^{-1}$ and its domain/range) and 199–206 (test inverses by composition); odd answers in the key — inverses and domain restrictions.
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: The Definition of a Function (full solutions) — functions vs relations, evaluation, difference quotients.
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Combining Functions (full solutions) — $f\pm g$, $fg$, $f/g$ and $f\circ g$ (5 problems).
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Inverse Functions (full solutions) — finding $f^{-1}$ for linear, cubic, root and rational functions (6 problems).

Unit 2 — Limits

All URLs below were opened and the page titles matched the topic.

  • Limit properties (the sum, product, quotient laws). Paul's Notes, "Limit Properties": https://tutorial.math.lamar.edu/Classes/CalcI/LimitsProperties.aspx

More practice online (outside sources)

  • OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1 §2.3 exercises 83–130 (odd answers in the book's answer key) — direct substitution, $\frac00$ by factoring and conjugates, one-sided infinite limits, piecewise one-sided limits.
  • OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1 §4.6 exercises 261–270 (limits at $\pm\infty$; odd answers in the answer key) — rational and radical limits at infinity, then asymptote problems 256–260 and 271–284.
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Computing Limits (full solutions) — factoring, rationalising, direct substitution, piecewise functions.
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Infinite Limits (full solutions) — one-sided and two-sided $\pm\infty$ limits and vertical asymptotes.
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Limits At Infinity, Part I (full solutions) — rational and radical functions as $x\to\pm\infty$.

Unit 3 — Idea of Continuity

More practice online (outside sources)

  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Continuity (full solutions) — where a function is continuous, one-sided continuity, discontinuity types.
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: One-Sided Limits (full solutions) — limits of piecewise functions at the break point, needed before testing continuity there.
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Computing Limits (full solutions) — factoring and rationalising, for finding the limit that decides a removable discontinuity.

  • Derivative as the slope of a tangent line (video and notes). MIT OpenCourseWare 18.01SC, Session 1 "Introduction to Derivatives": https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-01sc-single-variable-calculus-fall-2010/pages/1.-differentiation/part-a-definition-and-basic-rules/session-1-introduction-to-derivatives/

  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Tangent Lines and Rates of Change (full solutions) — secant slopes to a tangent slope, tangent equations and velocity (7 problems).
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Differentiation Formulas (full solutions) — problems 1–12 differentiate polynomials and roots with the power rule.

  • Implicit differentiation and tangent lines. OpenStax Calculus Volume 1, "3.8 Implicit Differentiation": https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/3-8-implicit-differentiation

More practice online (outside sources)

  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Product and Quotient Rule (11 problems, solution for each) — product and quotient rule drills, then values of $(fg)'$ and $(f/g)'$ from a table of values.
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Chain Rule (problems 1–27 differentiate, plus further problems; solution for each) — powers, trig, exponential and log composites.
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Implicit Differentiation (13 problems, solution for each) — $y'$ from an equation (problems 4–9), tangent lines (10–11).
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Derivatives of Exponential and Logarithm Functions (10 problems, solution for each) — $e^x$, $\ln x$ derivatives with the chain rule.
  • OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1 §3.6 exercises 214–259 (odd answers in the book's answer key, chapter 3) — chain rule from $y=f(u)$, $u=g(x)$ to tangent and normal lines.

Unit 6 — Integration: indefinite integrals and techniques

All URLs below were opened and checked against the topic.

  • Indefinite integrals, power rule, constant of integration. OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1, 4.10 "Antiderivatives": https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/4-10-antiderivatives and Paul's Online Notes, "Indefinite Integrals": https://tutorial.math.lamar.edu/Classes/CalcI/IndefiniteIntegrals.aspx
  • Substitution. Paul's Online Notes, "Substitution Rule for Indefinite Integrals": https://tutorial.math.lamar.edu/Classes/CalcI/SubstitutionRuleIndefinite.aspx
  • Integration by parts (including repeated and cyclic cases). Paul's Online Notes, "Integration by Parts": https://tutorial.math.lamar.edu/Classes/CalcII/IntegrationByParts.aspx
  • Partial fractions (distinct, repeated, irreducible factors). Paul's Online Notes, "Partial Fractions": https://tutorial.math.lamar.edu/Classes/CalcII/PartialFractions.aspx and OpenStax Calculus Vol. 2, 3.4 "Partial Fractions": https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-2/pages/3-4-partial-fractions
  • Lectures and notes on partial fractions and integration by parts. MIT OpenCourseWare 18.01SC, Unit 4 Part B: https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-01sc-single-variable-calculus-fall-2010/pages/unit-4-techniques-of-integration/part-b-partial-fractions-integration-by-parts-arc-length-and-surface-area/

More practice online (outside sources)

  • OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1 §5.5 exercises 261–287 (substitution: indicated $u$ in 261–270, your own change of variables in 271–287; odd answers in the Answer Key Chapter 5) — linear and polynomial substitutions, trigonometric powers. Answer key: https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/chapter-5
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Substitution Rule for Indefinite Integrals (17 problems, each with a full solution)
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Integration by Parts (9 problems with full solutions; problem 5 is the cyclic type $\int e^{2z}\cos(z/4)\,dz$)
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Partial Fractions (10 problems with full solutions; problems 5 and 6 have a repeated linear factor)
  • MIT OpenCourseWare 18.01SC, Problem Set 10: Integration Techniques (solutions PDF on the same page)

OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1, 5.3 "The Fundamental Theorem of Calculus": https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/5-3-the-fundamental-theorem-of-calculus and Paul's Online Notes, "Computing Definite Integrals": https://tutorial.math.lamar.edu/Classes/CalcI/ComputingDefiniteIntegrals.aspx

More practice online (outside sources)

Unit 8 — Volume of Solids of Revolution by Definite Integral

All URLs below were opened and their titles matched the topic. (A Khan Academy video page was tried but did not load, so it is not listed.)

  • Disc/washer method with worked examples (about both axes). Paul's Online Math Notes, "Volumes of Solids of Revolution / Method of Rings": https://tutorial.math.lamar.edu/Classes/CalcI/VolumeWithRings.aspx
  • Shells method (extra). Paul's Online Math Notes, "Volumes of Solids of Revolution / Method of Cylinders": https://tutorial.math.lamar.edu/Classes/CalcI/VolumeWithCylinder.aspx
  • Slicing, discs and washers, derivation of the formula. OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1, 6.2 "Determining Volumes by Slicing": https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/6-2-determining-volumes-by-slicing
  • Cylindrical shells and when to prefer them. OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1, 6.3 "Volumes of Revolution: Cylindrical Shells": https://openstax.org/books/calculus-volume-1/pages/6-3-volumes-of-revolution-cylindrical-shells

More practice online (outside sources)

  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Volume With Rings (8 problems, solution for each) — discs and washers about the $x$- and $y$-axes and about other horizontal lines.
  • OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1 §6.2 exercises 74–102 (odd answers in the book's answer key, chapter 6) — discs and washers about both axes; the same page also has exercises 58–73 on slicing and cross-sections and 103–113 on applications.
  • Paul's Online Notes — Practice Problems: Volume With Cylinders (8 problems, solution for each) — shells, extra — use to cross-check washer answers.
  • OpenStax Calculus Vol. 1 §6.3 exercises 114–164 (odd answers in the book's answer key) — shells, extra — "use both the shell method and the washer method" problems.
